# ASB system overview

The current ASB source version is `1.13.0`. See the [publication record](https://github.com/Jamir-boop/ASB/blob/main/docs/OPEN_SOURCE_PLAN.md) for verified release status.

ASB is a local switch board for existing Codex and Claude Desktop Code chats, including remote Code sessions observed in Claude's local cache. Its main view is a small native window: GNOME on Linux, and the same GTK board on Windows (MSYS2). It observes local files and opens the original app. It makes no model calls.

Read [README.md](README.md) for installation, [ASB.md](ASB.md) for controls, and [Privacy](docs/PRIVACY.md) for data access. ASB uses its own version series. Agent Mission Control `0.6.0` is the upstream base, not the ASB release version.

## Runtime path

```text
scripts/asb.mjs (the asb command and npm bin; scripts/asb runs it on Linux after it finds Node)
  -> engine, service (Linux), agent, bridge, group, network, phone, setup, doctor, and --package (Linux)
  -> no command: scripts/asb-desktop.mjs (Linux and Windows)

scripts/asb-desktop.mjs (board launcher, never hosts the server)
  -> scripts/asb-engine.mjs startEngine (startWindowsEngine on Windows, which starts the tray), finds or starts the engine
  -> src/linux-system.mjs cachedBoardCheck, at the same time as startEngine: the saved good result in `board-check.json` (cache folder) while its key holds, else boardCheck; when it fails: no icon registration, no window, the reason, exit 3 (the engine keeps running; a failed check is never saved)
  -> when the window ends with an error (exit code other than 0, a signal, or a start error; not after a stop that the launcher asked for): forgetBoardCheck deletes `board-check.json`, so that the next start probes again
  -> when the check is good: icon registration (Linux), then the window starts at once, while the engine may still start, with the argument `-` and a pipe on its stdin; when the engine answers, the launcher writes one JSON line `{"url","token","pid","node"}` to the pipe (the engine PID and the Node path of the launcher; no argument, environment variable, or file holds the URL or the token); 30 s later, while the window runs, it ends the pipe and exits 0; when the engine start fails, the launcher stops the window and exits 1 with the engine message
  -> on Windows: no icon registration, and the board starts with `windowsHide: true`
  -> scripts/asb-board.py loads scripts/asb-native.py as a module: GTK/Libadwaita window; a thread reads the first line from stdin and loads the first dashboard while the GTK import runs; when its event stream loses the engine, it runs `asb-desktop.mjs --follow` to reconnect to a restarted engine or to close
       -> local dashboard, event, open, and ASB state APIs

src/switchboard.mjs
  -> src/app-sources.mjs, local profile registry
  -> src/codex-data.mjs, read-only Codex reader
  -> src/claude-data.mjs, Claude Desktop Code reader
  -> src/insights.mjs, root grouping and normalization
  -> PendingTracker, ASB attention and pin state

scripts/asb-engine.mjs run (asb engine run, asb service run, or asb.service)
  -> src/switchboard.mjs, loopback HTTP server in service mode through src/asb-server.mjs
  -> src/phone-gateway.mjs, optional LAN, ZeroTier, and Tailscale HTTPS listeners for the Android app and group members
  -> src/machine-group.mjs, optional machine group: group.json, invites, join and leave, and the gateway member callbacks
  -> src/machine-peers.mjs, pinned mutual TLS client, member pollers, session export, and the merge of member rows
  -> scripts/asb-instance.mjs, $XDG_RUNTIME_DIR/asb/service.json (else asb/run in an absolute XDG_STATE_HOME or ~/.local/state), port and source token for the window
  -> src/memory-trim.mjs, the GC trim after a scan, and the V8 flag and glibc settings of the engine start
  -> scripts/asb-tray.py, Linux tray icon (StatusNotifierItem and dbusmenu on the session bus); `Passive` while the top-bar extension runs

scripts/asb-tray.ps1 (Windows, from the HKCU Run value or asb engine start)
  -> node --max-semi-space-size=16 scripts/asb.mjs engine run, started before Windows Forms loads, restarted after a failure
  -> Open ASB and a left click: POST /api/engine/show-window { window: board } first; node scripts/asb.mjs (the launcher above), with no console, only when no board shows itself

scripts/asb-tray.ps1 -Open (Windows, from the Start menu link ASB.lnk that asb engine install writes)
  -> node scripts/asb.mjs (the launcher above), with no console, then exit

gnome-shell/ (the ASB top-bar extension asb@jamir-boop.github.io, in GNOME Shell 45 to 49)
  -> service.json (the checks of scripts/asb-instance.mjs) for the port and the source token
  -> GET /api/events and GET /api/panel on 127.0.0.1 without the token; the token only for POST /api/engine/show-window and /api/engine/stop
  -> owns local.asb.AgentSwitchBoard.Panel on the session bus while it runs

scripts/asb-group.mjs (asb group, asb network, asb phone)
  -> scripts/asb-instance.mjs, loopback group and phone routes of the running engine

scripts/asb-usage.mjs (asb usage)
  -> scripts/asb-instance.mjs, loopback usage export and import routes of the running engine

scripts/asb-setup.mjs (asb setup)
  -> asb service install (Linux) or asb engine install (Windows), then the loopback phone settings route

scripts/asb-agent.mjs (asb agent)
  -> scripts/asb-instance.mjs, safe runtime discovery and authenticated loopback requests
  -> agent status and app-source APIs of the running desktop or service

android/ (Android companion)
  -> HTTPS with a pinned certificate to src/phone-gateway.mjs
       -> allowlisted proxy to the loopback ASB server
```

The board launcher never hosts the server. It calls `startEngine`: when `findRunningService()` answers, it uses that engine. Else, when `systemctl --user is-enabled asb.service` prints `enabled`, it runs `systemctl --user start asb.service`. When `systemctl --user show --property=ConditionResult --value asb.service` then prints `no`, the unit skipped its start because its launcher is gone (`ConditionFileIsExecutable`, for example after an uninstall): the launcher prints one warning that names `asb service install` and starts the engine as below. Else (not enabled) it spawns `node --max-semi-space-size=16 scripts/asb-engine.mjs run` detached, with the glibc settings of the engine start in its environment (see Engine memory), and with stdout and stderr in `$XDG_STATE_HOME/asb/engine.log` (`0600`, truncated at each start). It then polls `findRunningService()` every 200 ms for at most 10 s. The Python window does not wait for this: it starts when the board check is good, with the argument `-`, and the launcher writes the engine URL and the engine's source token to its stdin as one JSON line when the engine answers (see Refresh and performance limits). When the engine does not answer, the launcher stops the window, prints `ASB cannot start its engine. See <log or journalctl --user -u asb.service>.`, and exits 1. When the window exits in the first 30 s after the address line, the launcher exits with the window's code (a failed window also clears the saved board check); the engine keeps running. A SIGINT or SIGTERM in these 30 s stops the window. After 30 s the launcher ends the stdin pipe, stops watching the window (`unref`), and exits 0; the window keeps running (on Windows because it starts detached). When the event stream of the window cannot connect or ends, the window runs `node scripts/asb-desktop.mjs --follow` (the `node` path of the first line) in a thread, with `{"pid","token"}` of its current engine on stdin, a 30 s timeout, and at most 4096 bytes of stdout read (`CREATE_NO_WINDOW` on Windows). At most one helper runs at a time. The helper (`followEngine`) prints one JSON line and exits 0: `{"state":"alive"}` when the old PID still runs after 5 s (it checks the PID each 100 ms, because a stopping engine closes its streams before it saves its state and exits); `{"state":"quit"}` for a matching `stopped.json` quit marker (the SHA-256 of that engine's source token). Without a matching marker, it checks for a new engine every 500 ms for at most 20 s. It never starts an engine during this wait. It accepts a different PID only when `GET /api/agent/status` reports the same `package.json` version and the same resolved package root as the helper: then `{"state":"moved","url","token","pid"}`. A status error, a different version or root, or the 20 s limit give `{"state":"quit"}`. A bad input line exits 2 with a message on stderr. On `moved` the window reconnects with the new address and token on the GTK main loop and keeps the new PID for the next loss. On `quit` it closes, as on SIGTERM. On `alive`, or when the helper cannot run, times out, or prints no valid line (then the window writes one line to stderr), the event stream tries again each 5 s as before, and the helper runs for the next loss at the earliest 30 s later. After an `alive` answer to a loss, the next `connected` event of the stream (also one during that helper run) runs the helper once more with no wait, because the stream may have found a new engine on the same port: `moved` reconnects, `quit` closes, and `alive` runs no further helper until the next loss. The window never reads the runtime record or the quit marker itself. An icon registration error stays visible and does not prevent the native window from opening. Launching from source (`npm run desktop` or `scripts/asb`) keeps an installed desktop entry and reports the conflict. It replaces a source-run entry, including one from another checkout. The launcher attaches only to a running ASB engine, never to another process on the port. The server binds only to `127.0.0.1`; `HOST` cannot change that. The default port is `4629`. `npm start` creates no gateway and no tray.

`asb engine run|start|stop|status` (`scripts/asb-engine.mjs`): `start` uses the `startEngine` rules above. `stop` sends `POST /api/engine/stop` through `service.json` and waits up to 10 s for the engine PID to end. `status` prints the PID, the port, and `user service` when `systemctl --user show -p MainPID --value asb.service` prints that PID, else `detached`; it exits 1 when no engine runs. Other arguments print usage and exit 2. The engine commands need Linux or Windows; see the Windows engine and board below.

Tray: after the engine publishes `service.json`, on Linux with `DBUS_SESSION_BUS_ADDRESS` set and `ASB_TRAY` not `0`, it runs `boardCheck` and spawns `scripts/asb-tray.py` with the Python of the check, only when that Python has PyGObject (else it prints `ASB cannot start its tray icon: no Python 3 with PyGObject was found.` and starts no tray). When the board check fails, the tray also gets `ASB_BOARD=0`. It passes `ASB_ENGINE_PID`, `ASB_ENGINE_URL`, `ASB_SOURCE_TOKEN`, and `ASB_LAUNCHER` (`ASB_INSTALLED_LAUNCHER` when absolute, else `scripts/asb` of this checkout). The tray owns `org.kde.StatusNotifierItem-<pid>-1`, exports `/StatusNotifierItem` and the dbusmenu `/MenuBar` (Open ASB, Messages, Usage, a separator, Quit ASB; with `ASB_BOARD=0` only Quit ASB, and a left click does nothing), and registers with `org.kde.StatusNotifierWatcher` each time the watcher appears. Without a watcher for 60 s, it exits 0 silently. Its icon is `local.asb.AgentSwitchBoard` from the checkout's `assets/icons`. Its tooltip is `Running.` or `Running. Phone access on.` from `GET /api/phone` every 30 s. **Open ASB**, a left click, **Messages**, and **Usage** post `/api/engine/show-window` with `board`, `messages`, or `usage`, so an open board comes to the front (or opens that window). When no board is open (`{ "shown": false }` or an error), they spawn `ASB_LAUNCHER` without the engine variables, under systemd in its own `systemd-run --user --scope`, with `ASB_OPEN_WINDOW=messages` or `usage` for those two. **Quit ASB** posts `/api/engine/stop`. The tray exits when the engine PID is gone (checked every 5 s) or on SIGTERM; the engine sends SIGTERM when it stops. A tray error prints one line and never stops the engine. The tray watches the session bus name `local.asb.AgentSwitchBoard.Panel`: while it has an owner (the ASB top-bar extension), the item `Status` is `Passive`, else `Active`, with a `NewStatus` signal at each change. The AppIndicator host hides a `Passive` item, so the top bar shows ASB one time.

Top-bar extension (`gnome-shell/`, UUID `asb@jamir-boop.github.io`, GNOME Shell 45 to 49): `model.js` is the pure logic (Node tests in `test/gnome-shell-model.test.mjs`), `extension.js` the panel button, menu, and engine client, `stylesheet.css` look B (Capsule). The item goes into the right box of the top bar just left of the system menu (`quickSettings`); when a panel such as dash-to-panel has moved the system menu out of that box, it goes to the end of the box, next to the menu. The client reads `service.json` with the checks of `scripts/asb-instance.mjs` and `/proc/<pid>`, reads `GET /api/events`, and reads `GET /api/panel` after `connected`, and after a `dashboard` event only when the event's `panel` tag differs from the tag of the event that started its last read, or the event has no tag (an older engine; `panelRead` in `model.js`; a failed read forgets the tag): one request at a time, at most one start per second, and one trailing read for the events that come during a wait. A failed or invalid reply keeps the last state, and the client reads again after 3 s; a reply equal to the shown state changes nothing (an open menu is not built again). A stream that ends gives the Off look, and the client starts again after 1 s (a missing or bad runtime file: after 3 s). No proxy is used for `127.0.0.1`. **Open ASB** and **Start ASB** activate the desktop app `local.asb.AgentSwitchBoard.desktop` (**Start ASB** without it runs `asb` from `PATH`); **Messages** and **Usage** post `/api/engine/show-window` and, when no board shows the window, start the app with `ASB_OPEN_WINDOW`; **Quit ASB** posts `/api/engine/stop`; a session row with `canOpen` posts `/api/threads/<id>/open`, another row activates the app. The needs color is the `accent` of the reply, else the GNOME Shell accent (`-st-accent-color`, GNOME 47 and later), else `#3584e4`. The per-user install copies the folder to `~/.local/share/gnome-shell/extensions/asb@jamir-boop.github.io/` and turns it on (GSettings `org.gnome.shell enabled-extensions`) only when it adds it; the system packages put it in `/usr/share/gnome-shell/extensions/`. GNOME Shell loads a new or changed extension only at its next start. St pitfalls: St draws an inset `box-shadow` only on a node with a border or a background, so the capsule has a 1% white background for its ring; actor `width` and `height` are not scaled at a scale factor of 2, so every size is a CSS length (St scales those and `icon_size`); Colloid sets `!important` colors on inert menu items, so the menu rules for the ASB classes use `!important` too. Light surfaces: the extension reads the ink (foreground) color that the theme gives the panel button and the menu (`popup-menu`). A dark ink means a light surface, and the button or the menu actor gets the style class `asb-light`, whose rules in `stylesheet.css` use black with alpha. It reads the colors again on each `style-changed` of the button or the menu and before it builds the menu. It reads the panel ink only while the button has no pseudo-class (hover, focus, open), because a theme can give a highlighted button another ink. The measured nodes get no color from the ASB rules, so the class cannot change its own input. On a light menu, the accent and the machine hues are mixed with black until they have 4.5:1 (accent) or 3:1 (machine dots) on the menu background (`inkOn`; with no board accent, the GNOME accent comes from `St.Settings` `accent-color` and the St color table `ST_ACCENTS`). There is no `stylesheet-light.css` on purpose: GNOME Shell picks it from the color-scheme setting only, and some themes ship one dark stylesheet for every variant (Colloid's `gnome-shell-light.css` is a copy of its dark file), so it would draw dark ink on a dark panel.

`asb engine run` and its alias `asb service run` (`runEngine` in `scripts/asb-engine.mjs`) host the server with no window. Its runtime folder is `$XDG_RUNTIME_DIR/asb` when `XDG_RUNTIME_DIR` is absolute; that folder must exist and be owned by the user. Else it is `asb/run` in `XDG_STATE_HOME` when it is absolute, else in `~/.local/state` (a relative `XDG_STATE_HOME` is not used, as in ASB Bridge): the engine creates the `asb` folder of that path (`0700`) when it is missing and refuses it when it is a link or another user owns it. A refusal names the folder. It listens on `127.0.0.1` at `PORT` or `4629`, starts the phone gateway, gets the machine identity, starts the machine group, starts the message relay supervisor (see [Message relay](#message-relay)), and then writes `service.json` in the runtime folder (`0600`, folder `0700`) with `schema`, `pid`, `port`, and a random `sourceToken`. On SIGTERM, SIGINT, or `POST /api/engine/stop` it saves its reader positions, sends SIGTERM to the tray, removes that file only when the file still holds its own pid and token, stops the message relay (SIGTERM, then SIGKILL after 2 s), stops the member pollers, stops the gateway, and closes the server. The logged cleanup steps are `reader positions save`, `tray stop`, `manifest removal`, `relay stop`, `machine links stop`, `phone access stop`, and `server close`. A stop request exits with code 0, so `Restart=on-failure` does not restart `asb.service`. It does not start when `service.json` is invalid or belongs to a running process. In the state fallback folder, which a restart does not clear, a live PID counts only when that engine answers, as on Windows. `asb service install` first finds the mode (`autostartMode`): `systemd` when `systemctl --user show-environment` exits 0 and `systemctl --user is-active graphical-session.target` prints `active`; else `xdg` when `XDG_CURRENT_DESKTOP` or `DESKTOP_SESSION` is set; else `none`. In `systemd` mode it writes `asb.service` with `ConditionFileIsExecutable=<launcher>` in `[Unit]` (the launcher of `ExecStart`; when it is gone, for example after an uninstall, systemd skips the unit and does not fail it at login), `Restart=on-failure`, `StartLimitIntervalSec=60` and `StartLimitBurst=5` in `[Unit]` (a lasting start error stops after 5 starts), `KillMode=process`, and `WantedBy=graphical-session.target`. Then, when an ASB engine runs, detached or not, it stops it (`stopRunning`, injected as `stop`) and runs `systemctl --user daemon-reload` and `enable --now asb.service`. Thus the unit engine does not refuse the runtime record and restart again and again. In `xdg` mode it writes `${XDG_CONFIG_HOME:-~/.config}/autostart/asb-engine.desktop` (`0644`, marker line `# Generated by ASB: engine autostart`, `Exec=env ASB_NODE=<node> <launcher> engine start`, the same path rules, atomic write, and no write through links), calls `startEngine`, and prints `ASB starts at login through XDG autostart. Window managers without XDG autostart must run: <launcher> engine start`. In `none` mode it changes nothing, prints `No desktop session was found. Run asb service install from your desktop session, or run <launcher> engine start after you log in.`, and exits 1. A file of the own mode without the marker stops the command with an error that names it. After the write, the file of the other mode goes only when it has the marker. For the unit: after `disable --now`, before `startEngine`; when the disable fails, the unit stays with `ASB kept <file>: systemctl --user disable failed.` For the entry: it goes before the engine stop. `KillMode=process` keeps apps that ASB opened alive when the service stops. Under systemd (`INVOCATION_ID` set), each app opener runs in its own user scope through `systemd-run --user --scope`, so the app is not part of `asb.service`. Openers run with ignored stdio and a 5 s timeout, so an app that keeps running cannot hold an open request or keep the service from stopping. If the stop cleanup does not end, the engine exits 5 s after the signal or stop request with code 1, and logs the pending cleanup step and the open Node resources. `ExecStart` uses `ASB_INSTALLED_LAUNCHER` when it is an absolute executable file, else `scripts/asb` of the current checkout. Paths with whitespace, control characters, quotes, `%`, `$`, or backslashes are rejected. `uninstall` removes each of the two files that has the ASB marker: the unit after `disable --now`, then a reload; the autostart entry, then it stops the engine with `stopRunning` (`POST /api/engine/stop`). `status` in `systemd` mode prints `systemctl --user is-active asb.service` and whether the runtime file is valid. In `xdg` mode it prints `XDG autostart entry: <path>` (or `not installed`), in `none` mode `No desktop session was found.`, then `Engine: running` or `Engine: not running`, with exit code 0 or 3. `main` takes injected `env`, `execFile`, `platform`, `log`, `start`, `stop`, and `find`. Other arguments print usage and exit 2. The service commands need Linux.

Engine memory (`src/memory-trim.mjs`): the engine held several times more memory than its live data needs (on a PC with 281 rows, about 450 MB of RSS after many scans, against 43 MB of live JavaScript data). The rest was free memory that V8 and glibc kept: the V8 young generation, free V8 pages, and free glibc blocks. Three settings give most of it back. (1) The trim: `createMemoryTrim` runs the V8 full GC of the `last-resort` kind (`gc({ type: 'major', execution: 'sync', flavor: 'last-resort' })`), which releases the free pages. `loadDashboard` in `createSwitchboardServer` calls the option `onScanDone` after each scan, and `runEngine` passes it the `scanEnded` function of the trim. One timer covers all scan ends before it fires. It fires 3 s after a scan end (`TRIM_SETTLE_MS`) and at most once in 30 s (`TRIM_INTERVAL_MS`). It then reads `process.memoryUsage.rss()` and trims only when the memory is at least 128 MiB (`TRIM_GROWTH_BYTES`) above the level after the last trim, or above the lowest level since then (V8 can give memory back by itself); the first check always trims. A trim needs about 15 ms and makes the next scan cost about 75 ms more CPU, because the freed pages must be mapped again. So a trim must pay. A trim after each scan (at most once in 5 s) took the memory of a 10-minute run to about 135 MB but doubled the CPU, and the rule above gives about 205 MB with a small CPU increase. The `gc` function comes from `v8.setFlagsFromString('--expose-gc')` and `vm.runInNewContext('gc')`, then `--no-expose-gc`: the engine needs no `--expose-gc` argument, and the global object has no `gc`. V8 before version 12 (Node.js 20) ignores the flavor, so there the trim does nothing. A missing or failing `gc`, or a failing memory read, is no error and prints nothing. (2) The Node argument `--max-semi-space-size=16` (`ENGINE_NODE_FLAGS`) makes the young generation smaller. It works only on the command line. (3) The glibc settings `MALLOC_ARENA_MAX=2`, `MALLOC_MMAP_THRESHOLD_=131072`, and `MALLOC_TRIM_THRESHOLD_=131072` (`MALLOC_ENV`) return freed blocks to the system. glibc reads them when the process starts, so they must be in the environment of the start.

The settings (2) and (3) reach the engine at these starts: `scripts/asb` for `service run` and `engine run` (the user unit runs `<launcher> service run`; an update does not write the unit again, so a unit that an older ASB wrote gets the settings after `systemctl --user restart asb.service`); the detached `spawn` in `startEngine` (the argument, and `MALLOC_ENV` in the environment of the child; the XDG autostart entry and the board launcher use it); and `Start-Engine` in `scripts/asb-tray.ps1` (the argument only: Windows has no glibc). Another start, for example `asb engine run` through the npm `asb` link, which does not use `scripts/asb`, gets only the trim. No other command of `scripts/asb` gets the settings. Both starts keep a value that is already set, also an empty one: the launcher uses `${NAME-value}`, and `startEngine` spreads `env` over `MALLOC_ENV`. `runEngine` deletes from `process.env` and from its `env` argument each of the three variables that has the ASB value (`forgetMallocEnv`) before it starts anything, so the message relay, the tray, and the apps that ASB opens get only the values of the user. A user value that equals the ASB value is also deleted (a `shortcut:` comment names this limit).

Engine discovery: `findRunningService()` reads `service.json`. The file must be a regular file with mode `0600` in a `0700` folder, both owned by the user. It then sends `GET /api/service` with the token and a two-second timeout, and accepts only `{ service: true }` with the same pid. An ASB `1.8.1` or older launcher has no attach mode and reports a port conflict while an engine runs.

`scripts/asb-instance.mjs` shares descriptor validation, publication, requests, and owner cleanup. It rejects linked or foreign runtime folders and unsafe files, keeps invalid descriptors, and refuses to replace one whose pid is still alive. Cleanup removes only a descriptor with the owner's pid and token. The agent CLI uses this file, then verifies `apiVersion` and pid through `/api/agent/status`; it does not scan ports or edit `sources.json` directly. The unmanaged `npm start` server has no descriptor and no usable agent interface.

### Dispatcher, npm package, and setup

`scripts/asb.mjs` is the one `asb` command. `package.json` names it in `bin`, so `npm install -g --install-links github:Jamir-boop/ASB` links it. Without `--install-links`, npm 11 installs a git package as a link to its temporary clone and then deletes that clone, so the README and `ASB.md` give the flag. On Linux, `scripts/asb` keeps its Node discovery and SQLite check (with no SQLite check for `build`, `uninstall`, `agent`, `doctor`, `group`, `network`, `phone`, `usage`, and `setup`), then runs `scripts/asb.mjs` for every command. Each command file exports `main()`. The dispatcher keeps the error prefixes of each file (`ASB engine: `, `ASB Bridge failed: `, `ASB service: `). When `scripts/asb.mjs` runs as the entry, it hides only the `node:sqlite` ExperimentalWarning (Node.js 22 and early 24 print it with every command) and prints other warnings as `<name>: <message>`. On Windows, Node.js older than `22.13` gives `ASB needs Node.js 22.13 or later on Windows.` (exit 1), because the engine needs `node:sqlite`. On Linux, Node.js older than 20 gives the message of `scripts/asb` (`NODE_HINT`, with the link to the `Node.js` section of `docs/INSTALL.md`) and exit 1, because npm runs `scripts/asb.mjs` without `scripts/asb`. With no command, Windows opens the board through `scripts/asb-desktop.mjs`, as Linux does. `doctor` runs on Windows too; `service`, `--package`, and other commands print usage (exit 2). On Linux, only no command opens the board (`scripts/asb-desktop.mjs`); any first argument that no branch handles (for example `--help` or a typo) prints `LINUX_USAGE` (exit 2), loads no script, and starts no engine. The `files` list of `package.json` ships `src/`, `scripts/*.mjs`, `scripts/asb`, the two Python scripts, `scripts/asb-tray.ps1`, `public/`, `assets/`, the bridge runtime files, the Codex marketplace file, `LICENSE`, `README.md`, `install.sh`, and `uninstall.sh`. It ships no tests, docs, `android/`, or `demo/`; `test/asb-package.test.mjs` checks this with `npm pack --dry-run`. The Windows-only `scripts/asb-tray.ps1`, `assets/icons/asb-tray-32.png`, and `assets/icons/asb.ico` (the Start menu link icon, made from `local.asb.AgentSwitchBoard.svg`: PNG images at 16, 32, 48, and 256 px in one ICO file) are only in the npm list, not in `RUNTIME_FILES`.

`asb setup [--yes] [--only board|bridge] [--address <address>]...` (`scripts/asb-setup.mjs`) sets up the board part, the bridge part, or both. `--only board` or `--only bridge` chooses one part, and `--yes` without `--only` chooses both. With neither flag, it asks `Set up which parts of ASB?` (`1. Board and bridge`, `2. Board only`, `3. Bridge only`; an empty answer or the end of the input gives 1, another answer gives `Enter a number from 1 to 3.`). `--only bridge` with `--address` is a usage error (exit 2), and the answer 3 with `--address` gives `Bridge only does not use --address.` All questions use one line reader, so piped answers are kept. The board part: 0. on Linux and on Windows it prints the `asb doctor` output (without the firewall lines) and stops (exit 1) when the engine cannot run (on Windows: Node.js without built-in SQLite; a missing board does not stop it, and its lines show how to install it); 1. it runs `asb service install` on Linux or `asb engine install` on Windows; 2. it polls `findRunningService()` for up to 20 s (else `The ASB engine does not answer. Check asb engine status.`); 3. it reads `GET /api/phone` and saves `POST /api/phone/settings` with `enabled: true`, the chosen addresses, and the current port, as `asb network enable` does. `--address` must name listed addresses with no repeats. `--yes` keeps the saved addresses that are listed now, else the first LAN address (else the first address). With neither flag, it lists the addresses and reads numbers from the terminal; an empty answer or the end of the input keeps the defaults. 4. On Windows it prints the `New-NetFirewallRule` command (TCP, the phone port, the chosen addresses in one `-LocalAddress` value, and `-Program` with `node.exe`); it never runs it. On Linux, when firewalld (`firewall-cmd --state` prints `running`) or ufw (`/etc/ufw/ufw.conf` has `ENABLED=yes`) is active, it prints the rule for each chosen address and the phone port under `Phone access and machine links through <firewalld or ufw>:` (`linuxFirewallLines` of `scripts/asb-doctor.mjs`; firewalld adds `--zone=<zone>` when `firewall-cmd --get-zone-of-interface=<interface>` names one, then one `--reload`). It prints a rule only for an IPv4 address, a port from 1 to 65535, and usual interface and zone names, else one note for that address. It never runs the rule. The bridge part reads `status()` of `scripts/asb-bridge.mjs` and installs ASB Bridge only where it is missing: each Codex home whose plugin is not installed (one `install()` call for each home), the Claude home when it exists and has no bridge skill, and the OpenCode config when it exists and has no plugin. It never changes an existing install: it prints `<app> <path>: already installed`, then `Setup does not change an existing ASB Bridge. To see its state, run: asb bridge status`. It skips, with a `Warning: <path>: ...` line, a Codex home with the old plugin, a Codex home whose running app-server cannot find Bun (an install there stops Codex from starting tasks), a skill or plugin folder that is not from ASB, and an OpenCode older than 2.1.2. A failed target does not stop the other targets. After an install it prints the `nextSteps` lines. With no Codex home, Claude home, or OpenCode config, it prints one line and installs nothing. Board and bridge, and board only, end with the group join line (on Windows: `asb group join --name <name>, then paste the link`); board only first prints `To add ASB Bridge later, run: asb setup --only bridge`. Bridge only runs no doctor, autostart, network access, or firewall step, and ends with `The board is not set up. To add it, run: asb setup --only board`. When a bridge target failed, setup ends with `ASB Bridge is not installed for every app. Fix the warnings above, then run asb setup --only bridge.` (exit 1), after the last line of its part. Errors print `asb setup: <message>`.

`asb group join [<link>] [--name <name>] [--yes]`: with no link, it asks `Paste the join link: ` and reads one line (the end of the input gives `No join link.`, exit 1); `--yes` skips only the fingerprint question. Windows needs this form: npm runs `asb` there through `asb.cmd`, and `cmd.exe` splits the link at each `&`. One line reader serves both questions, so piped answers are kept. The invite text adds `On Windows, run asb group join and paste the link when it asks.`.

`asb phone status [--json] | pair | remove <name> | remove --id <id>` (`scripts/asb-group.mjs`) uses the loopback phone routes of the running engine: the list of paired phones, a new pairing link with its end time, and the removal of one phone. A name that matches more than one phone prints their IDs and asks for `--id`.

`asb usage export <file> | import <file>` (`scripts/asb-usage.mjs`, on Linux and Windows) uses the usage ledger routes of the running engine through `service.json` (60 s timeout, 64 MiB limit; see [Usage ledger](#usage-ledger)). `export` refuses a file that exists (`<file> exists. Choose a new file name.`), writes the reply with mode `0600` and the `wx` flag, and prints `Saved the usage of <n> machines (<h> hours) to <file>.`, where `<h>` counts the records (one hour of one app and model). `import` refuses a missing file (`Cannot read <file>.`), a file over 64 MiB (`The file is too large for an ASB usage export.`), or a file that is not JSON (`This file is not an ASB usage export.`), posts the parsed file, and prints `Imported the usage of <n> machines: <name> (<hours> hours), ...`. One machine or hour prints `1 machine` or `1 hour`. Without an engine, both print `ASB is not running. Start it: asb engine start.`; an engine error prints its `error` text. `asb usage backup` prints the backup status (see [Usage backups](#usage-backups)): `Daily backup: on, at 03:00. Keeps the last 5 files.` (`off` in place of `on, at 03:00`; `Keeps 1 file.` for one file), `Folder: <path>` (with ` (default)` for the default folder), only when on `Next backup: <YYYY-MM-DD HH:MM>.` (or `Next backup: in a few minutes.` when that time has passed), `A backup is running.` while one runs, `Backups: none yet.` or `Backups:` with one line for each file (two spaces, the name, the local time, and the size in `bytes`, `KB`, or `MB` with 1024 steps), and `Last backup failed (<YYYY-MM-DD HH:MM>): <message>` after a failure. `asb usage backup now` posts `/api/usage/backup/now` (120 s timeout) and prints `Saved the usage of <n> machines (<h> hours) to <path>.` `asb usage backup on|off [--time HH:MM] [--keep N] [--folder <path>|default]` reads the status, posts all four settings (an option that is not given keeps its value; `--folder default` sends `''`, another folder goes through `path.resolve`, and an empty value is a usage error), and prints the new status. A bad `--time` prints `asb usage: Use --time as HH:MM, for example 03:00.` and a bad `--keep` prints `asb usage: Use --keep with a number from 1 to 30.` (exit 2). An engine without the backup routes (HTTP 404) prints `asb usage: This ASB engine has no usage backups. Update ASB, then start it again.` Errors print `asb usage: <message>` (exit 1); other arguments print `Usage: asb usage export <file> | import <file> | backup [now | on | off] [--time HH:MM] [--keep N] [--folder <path>|default]` (exit 2).

### Linux tools, the board check, and `asb doctor`

Tool discovery uses no fixed tool path. `scripts/asb` finds Node.js in this order: `ASB_NODE`, `node` on `PATH`, `~/.local/bin/node`, `/home/linuxbrew/.linuxbrew/bin/node`, `/usr/local/bin/node`, and `/usr/bin/node`. `src/linux-system.mjs` finds Python: an absolute `ASB_PYTHON` is the only candidate (a relative one is ignored), else `/usr/bin/python3` when it is an executable file, then `python3` on `PATH`, with no duplicates. `xdg-open`, `openssl`, `sqlite3`, `bun`, and the sound players come from a `PATH` search with `findExecutable` of `src/platform-paths.mjs`. Doctor runs `xdg-mime` and `firewall-cmd` by name. NixOS and Guix have no `/usr/bin/python3`, and nvm, fnm, and Homebrew keep Node.js outside the fixed list, so these rules matter there.

The board check runs one Python probe. `probePython` runs `python -c <PYTHON_PROBE>` with a 5 s timeout and reads one JSON object: `python`, `gi`, `gtk`, `adw`, `glib`, `cairo` (`gi.require_foreign('cairo')` works), `qrcode`, and `trayHost` (`org.kde.StatusNotifierWatcher` has an owner on the session bus; `null` without `DBUS_SESSION_BUS_ADDRESS`; a 1 s D-Bus timeout). GTK and Libadwaita import without a display. `boardCheck` probes the candidates in order, uses the first with PyGObject, and gives `{ ok, python, versions, reason }`. `ok` needs Python 3.9, GTK 4.12, and Libadwaita 1.4 (`BOARD_FLOORS`). `engineCheck` needs Node.js 20, `node:sqlite` or `sqlite3`, `openssl`, and `xdg-open`. The window launcher (`scripts/asb-desktop.mjs`), the tray start (`runEngine`), and the per-user install (`checkRuntime`) use these two checks. A failed `boardCheck` means engine only: the launcher exits with code 3, the tray starts with `ASB_BOARD=0`, and the install writes no desktop entry or icon. A failed `engineCheck` refuses the install.

The window launcher calls `cachedBoardCheck` (`src/linux-system.mjs`), because `boardCheck` costs about 800 ms on Windows (about 110 ms on Linux) before each start. `cachedBoardCheck` saves a good `boardCheck` result in `board-check.json` in the cache folder (`cacheDir`). A temporary file is written with mode `0600` in a `0700` folder, then renamed. The file has `python`, `versions` (the probe object), and `key`: `version` (`package.json`), `platform`, `candidates` (the list of `pythonCandidates`), and the `size` and `mtimeMs` of the Python file. Each start reads the key again (the candidate lookup and one `stat`). It skips the probe only when the file is valid, `python` is one of the current candidates, and the key is equal. Else it runs `boardCheck`. A failed check is never saved, and a write error is ignored. When the window ends with an exit code other than 0, with a signal, or with a start error, the launcher calls `forgetBoardCheck`, which deletes the file (a delete error prints one warning); a stop that the launcher asked for does not delete it. `asb doctor`, `asb setup`, and the tray start call `boardCheck` and never read or write the file.

`asb doctor` (`scripts/asb-doctor.mjs`: `collect`, `format`, and `main`) never changes the system. `collect` takes its readers, `execFile`, the Node.js facts, `find`, and `api` as arguments, so the tests fake the whole system. It runs only read-only programs: `openssl version`, `xdg-mime query default x-scheme-handler/<codex or claude>`, `firewall-cmd --state`, `firewall-cmd --get-zone-of-interface=<interface>`, and the Python probe. It reads `/etc/os-release`, `/run/ostree-booted`, `/etc/ufw/ufw.conf`, the user unit and the autostart entry (only the marker line counts), and, only while an engine runs (`findRunningService`), `GET /api/phone` with a 2 s timeout. It calls no `sudo`, `doas`, `pkexec`, `systemctl`, package manager, or firewall change; `test/asb-doctor.test.mjs` records every `execFile` call. The checks come in this order: Engine (`node`, `sqlite3` only when Node.js has no `node:sqlite`, `openssl`, `xdg-open`), Window (`python` only when Python is missing or too old, `gi`, `gtk`, `adw`), and Options (`qrcode`, `sounds`, `tray`, `bun`, `autostart`, `runtime`, `links`). The `bun` check uses `findBun`, the lookup of the engine (`PATH`, then `BUN_INSTALL/bin`, then `~/.bun/bin`). Bun on `PATH` gives `ok` and `Bun (<file>), for ASB Bridge`. Bun in one of the other folders also gives `ok`, but the detail is `Bun (<file>), not on PATH. The message relay finds it. A Codex task needs it on PATH.`, because the Codex plugin runs `bun` from the PATH of Codex (the `asb bridge install` check and the app-server PATH check do not change). On a system with nothing installed, the required keys are `node`, `sqlite`, `openssl`, `xdg`, `gi`, `gtk`, and `adw`, and the optional keys are `gicairo`, `qrcode`, and `sound`; the install guide blocks of each family use these lists. A key without a package in the table (`null`) is left out, because another package of the family includes it. `installCommand(family, keys, { mode, root })` of `src/linux-distros.mjs` makes each command. The root word is `doas` only when `doas` is found and `sudo` is not. Transactional mode uses `rpm-ostree install` (Fedora) or `transactional-update pkg install` (openSUSE). Read-only mode, NixOS, and an unknown family have no command, so doctor prints the needs in words, and for NixOS also the flake line. `nodeRoute` gives the module command for release 9 of RHEL and its rebuilds, the NodeSource text for the debian family, and the archive text for read-only mode. The exit code is 0 when every Engine check is `ok`. `--json` prints `schema`, `system`, `checks`, `board`, and `commands`, with each command as an array of words. The Links check shows the desktop file that handles the `codex:` and `claude:` links, or an `info` line when an app has none, because ASB opens a session with `xdg-open <link>` and reads only the native app folders. The runtime folder rule and the autostart modes are in the engine paragraphs above.

The package table (`FAMILIES` in `src/linux-distros.mjs`) has nine families: `debian`, `fedora`, `opensuse`, `arch`, `alpine`, `void`, `gentoo`, `solus`, and `nixos`. Each has `ids` (values of `ID`), `like` (values of `ID_LIKE`), the `install` words, the `ci` install words (`null` for Gentoo, Solus, and NixOS), and the `packages` of each key. The keys are `node`, `sqlite`, `openssl`, and `xdg` (required), `gi`, `gtk`, and `adw` (the window), `gicairo`, `qrcode`, and `sound` (optional), and `xvfb`, `dbus`, and `font` (CI only). `detectFamily` matches `ID` first, then each `ID_LIKE` value, and gives the mode: `transactional` when `/run/ostree-booted` exists or `ID` is `opensuse-microos`, `opensuse-aeon`, or `opensuse-kalpa`; `readonly` for `steamos`; else `normal`. `node src/linux-distros.mjs <family> <key>...` prints the CI install line (no root prefix), and `node src/linux-distros.mjs detect` prints `<family> <mode>`. The fedora `gtk` key is `gtk4 gobject-introspection libglvnd-gles`: Fedora 43 and later no longer pull `gobject-introspection` (the `cairo-1.0` typelib) in with `python3-gobject`, and GTK 4.20 aborts in libepoxy without `libGLESv2.so.2`, which the `gtk4` package does not require. The family blocks of the install guide (`docs/INSTALL.md`) are checked against `installCommand` by `test/asb-docs.test.mjs`.

### Windows engine and board

On Windows, ASB runs the engine and the board. The board is the same `scripts/asb-native.py` as on Linux, run by MSYS2 UCRT64 Python with PyGObject, GTK 4, and Libadwaita (see the Board and Doctor items below). Without them, only the engine runs, with no window: it still serves paired phones and the machine group. ASB `1.10.0` ran only the engine on Windows. The board, the Start menu link, `asb doctor`, and the PC sounds on Windows came in `1.11.0`.

ASB Bridge and the message relay also run on Windows (plan 17). `bridge/src/platform.ts` holds the parts that differ: a Windows receiver listens on `\\.\pipe\LOCAL\cc-msg-<32 hex>`, writes the key file `<pid>.<sha256 of the lower-case pipe path>.key` (`peerToken`, `procStart`, `pidDomain`) before its row, and closes a connection whose first line is not the `auth` line with that token. `procStart` is the `GetProcessTimes` creation FILETIME (`bun:ffi`), `pidDomain` is `win32:<host name>`, and the outbound socket is `\\.\pipe\LOCAL\asb-out-<the same 32 hex>` with the same `auth` line. File checks refuse links but skip owners and modes. The Codex desktop IPC is `\\.\pipe\codex-ipc`; `SessionEnd` removes the row and key file of the stopped receiver. `asb bridge install` finds `bun` and `codex` with `PATHEXT`, runs a `codex.cmd` through `cmd.exe /d /s /c` with each part quoted (and refuses `"`, `%`, `^`, `&`, `|`, `<`, `>`, `!`, CR, LF), and refuses `link-claude`. For an exact local send, each bound write goes only through a pipe handle whose server ASB Bridge proved before the first byte: the registry PID, its creation FILETIME (`procStart`), and the same Windows user. Linux and macOS behavior does not change.

`src/platform-paths.mjs` gives the ASB folders: Linux keeps the XDG values byte for byte (`configDir`, `stateDir`, and `runtimeDir`, which is `asb/run` in `XDG_STATE_HOME` when it is absolute, else in `~/.local/state` without an absolute `XDG_RUNTIME_DIR`); Windows uses `%APPDATA%\asb`, `%LOCALAPPDATA%\asb\state`, and `%LOCALAPPDATA%\asb\run`, with `<home>\AppData\Roaming` or `Local` when the variable is not absolute. The win32 branches take injected `platform`, `env`, and `execFile`, so `test/asb-windows.test.mjs` and `test/asb-win32-files.test.mjs` check them on Linux.

- File safety: win32 skips the uid and mode checks of `service.json` and its folder, still refuses a link (`lstat`, then an `O_RDONLY` open), and does not `chmod`. A live PID in an old `service.json` refuses a new engine only when that engine answers `GET /api/service` with the same PID in 2 s, because Windows reuses PIDs. At start, the engine limits the config, state, and runtime folders with `icacls <folder> /inheritance:r /grant:r <USERDOMAIN\USERNAME>:(OI)(CI)F`; a failure logs `ASB cannot limit <folder> to this user: <reason>` and the engine continues.
- Certificate: win32 creates `cert.pfx`, `pfx-pass` (a random passphrase), and `cert.cer` with one Windows PowerShell script (`WINDOWS_CERTIFICATE_SCRIPT`, ECDSA P-256) that gets the folder and passphrase only in `ASB_TLS_DIR` and `ASB_PFX_PASS`. A failure removes all three files. The fingerprint is the SHA-256 of `cert.cer`. `credentials()` gives `{ pfx, passphrase, fingerprint }` on win32 and `{ key, cert, fingerprint }` on Linux; the peer client passes whichever TLS fields it gets. `asb group reset-identity` removes the three win32 files there.
- Network names: `addressKind` treats `/^tailscale/i` and `/^(zt|zerotier)/i` adapter names as Tailscale and ZeroTier, so the Windows adapter names `Tailscale` and `ZeroTier One [...]` also work.
- Engine and tray: `asb engine run|start|stop|status|install|uninstall`. `install` writes the `ASB` value of `HKCU\Software\Microsoft\Windows\CurrentVersion\Run` from Node with `reg add` (PowerShell 5.1 drops the quotes of a native argument), then writes the Start menu link, then does `start`. `start` runs a short `powershell.exe -NoProfile -NonInteractive -Command Start-Process ...` that starts the hidden tray with the Run value text in `ASB_TRAY_ARGS` (`-NoProfile -ExecutionPolicy Bypass -WindowStyle Hidden -File "<root>\scripts\asb-tray.ps1" "<node.exe>"`), because a detached child has no console and `powershell.exe` then exits at once. A failed start gives `ASB cannot start its tray. Windows PowerShell failed.`. Then it waits up to 20 s for the engine. `uninstall` first removes the Start menu link, then deletes the value only when `reg query` finds it, then stops the engine. `status` prints `running. PID <pid>, port <port>, tray.`. `run` writes its output to `%LOCALAPPDATA%\asb\state\engine.log` when it has no console or `ASB_ENGINE_LOG=1`, which the tray sets because its hidden console is a TTY. The tray script holds the mutex `Local\ASB-tray`, starts `node --disable-warning=ExperimentalWarning scripts\asb.mjs engine run`, and starts it again after a failure. The first start comes right after the mutex check, before the script loads Windows Forms and Drawing and builds the icon and the menu, because `Start-Engine` needs only `System.Diagnostics`. If the tray fails after that start, the engine keeps running with no tray. After more than 5 failures in 10 minutes, it shows `ASB stopped after repeated failures.` and exits. An engine exit with code 0 ends the tray. Its menu has **Open ASB**, **Messages**, **Usage**, a separator, **Copy phone pairing link** (the loopback pairing route with the source token, to the clipboard), and **Quit ASB** (`POST /api/engine/stop`). **Open ASB** and a left click on the icon start `node --disable-warning=ExperimentalWarning scripts\asb.mjs` with no console, and do nothing while an earlier launcher runs. The 1 s timer checks that launcher: when it exits with code 3, the tray shows the balloon `ASB has no window on this PC. Run asb doctor.` (10 s). **Messages** and **Usage** post `/api/engine/show-window`; when no board shows the window and no earlier launcher runs, they start that launcher with `ASB_OPEN_WINDOW=messages` or `usage` (a failure shows the balloon `ASB cannot open its window.`). Its icon is `assets/icons/asb-tray-32.png`. With the switch `-Open`, the script starts that launcher (the same function as **Open ASB**) and waits up to 30 s for its exit. When the launcher exits with code 3, the script shows `ASB has no window on this PC. Run asb doctor.` in a message box. Then the script exits with code 0. It takes no mutex, shows no icon, and does not start the engine, because the launcher starts the engine when none runs. The engine can play sounds on win32 with `powershell.exe` (see Sounds). The sound gate is the same, so it plays a sound only while a board window reports itself.
- Start menu link: `asb engine install` writes `ASB.lnk` in the Programs folder of the Start menu (`[Environment]::GetFolderPath('Programs')`) with one Windows PowerShell script (`START_MENU_LINK_SCRIPT`, `WScript.Shell` `CreateShortcut`, `-NoProfile -NonInteractive -Command`, `windowsHide`). The script text has no path: the arguments, the ASB folder, the icon path, the mode, and the app ID are in `ASB_LINK_ARGS`, `ASB_LINK_ROOT`, `ASB_LINK_ICON`, `ASB_LINK_MODE` (`write` or `remove`), and `ASB_LINK_APP_ID`. The link starts `%SystemRoot%\System32\WindowsPowerShell\v1.0\powershell.exe` with the Run value text and ` -Open`, in the ASB folder, minimized (window style 7, so the hidden console does not flash), with the icon `assets\icons\asb.ico` and the description `Agent Switch Board (generated by ASB)` (the marker). In write mode, the script then sets the AppUserModelID of the link (`System.AppUserModel.ID`) to `local.asb.AgentSwitchBoard` through the shell property store (`SHGetPropertyStoreFromParsingName` in a small C# type of `Add-Type`). The board process has the same ID (`scripts/asb-board.py`), so the taskbar puts the board window under this link: the taskbar menu shows ASB, and a pin starts this link, not Python. The script changes or removes only a link with the marker. An `ASB.lnk` without it stays, and the script prints its path: `ASB kept <path>: it was not made by ASB.`. On success the install prints `ASB is in the Start menu.`. A failed script prints `ASB cannot write its Start menu link: <reason>`, and the install continues. `uninstall` runs the same script in `remove` mode before the other steps; a failure prints `ASB cannot remove its Start menu link: <reason>` and the uninstall continues.
- Board: `asb` with no command, **Open ASB**, and the Start menu link run `scripts/asb-desktop.mjs`. On Windows it uses `startWindowsEngine` and no icon registration, and starts the window with `windowsHide: true` and `detached: true` (`boardSpawnOptions`), because Node puts a child that is not detached into a job object that ends it when the launcher exits. The detached window has no console of the launcher: its stdout and stderr are `ignore`, so its output goes nowhere (as before when the tray started the launcher with no window). On Linux the window shares the stdout and stderr of the launcher. `pythonCandidates` on win32 gives an absolute `ASB_PYTHON` (the only candidate), else the MSYS2 default `C:\msys64\ucrt64\bin\python.exe` (`MSYS2_PYTHON`), then `python` on `PATH` (`findExecutable`, `PATHEXT`); `boardCheck` uses the first that has PyGObject, as on Linux. `cachedBoardCheck` saves `board-check.json` in `%LOCALAPPDATA%\asb\cache` (the same folder as `cacheDir`). `scripts/asb-board.py` keeps the compiled board code in `%LOCALAPPDATA%\asb\cache\pycache` (`<home>\AppData\Local` when `LOCALAPPDATA` is not absolute, the same folder as `cacheDir`). It also sets `GSK_RENDERER=cairo` before the board code runs (`prefer_cairo`), unless `GSK_RENDERER` is set and not empty or `GDK_DEBUG` contains `dcomp`; the child programs of the window inherit it. On X11, the launcher also sets `GSK_RENDERER=cairo` and `GDK_DISABLE=gl,vulkan` unless the user set either variable to a nonempty value; measured RSS/PSS fell from 237.5/133.3 MB to 146.7/95.5 MB and focus repaint from 281 to 640 ms (GL) to 40 to 80 ms (Cairo). GTK 4.24 on Windows tries GL and fails (`OpenGL requires Direct Composition`) before it uses Cairo. With Cairo set, the first rows show about 270 ms sooner (1388 ms against 1664 ms) and the board uses about 90 MB less private memory (143 MB against 233 MB). On Windows, `scripts/asb-board.py` sets the AppUserModelID `local.asb.AgentSwitchBoard` before GTK opens a window, and the board window loads `assets/icons/asb.ico` as its window icon. GTK makes no window icon from an icon file outside an icon theme folder. So the taskbar button shows the ASB icon, not the Python icon. The Start menu link has the same ID, so the taskbar menu and a pin also show ASB. Linux icon handling does not change. `scripts/asb-native.py` keeps `theme.json` and `layout.json` in `%APPDATA%\asb` (`config_dir`, the same folder as `configDir`; `<home>\AppData\Roaming` when `APPDATA` is not absolute). The window does not call `GLib.unix_signal_add` on Windows: the launcher ends the window process, also when the engine PID is gone. `tooltip_model` shortens a Windows home folder with `\`. Preferences shows no QR code, because MSYS2 has no Python `qrcode` package; it shows the link with **Copy link**. GTK works only in a signed-in desktop session: in an SSH session (session 0), even a plain `Gtk.Window()` ends with an access violation. So the board checks run in the desktop session (for example a one-time scheduled task with `/it`), not over SSH. Tested on Windows 10 21H2 with MSYS2 Python 3.14.8, PyGObject 3.58.1, GTK 4.24.1, and Libadwaita 1.10.0.
- Doctor: `asb doctor` and `asb setup` step 0 run on Windows (`collectWindows` in `scripts/asb-doctor.mjs`). The Engine group checks Node.js `22.13` or later with built-in SQLite only (no `/etc/os-release`, `openssl`, `xdg-open`, or firewall check). The Window group is the `boardCheck` block of Linux. The Options group has the QR code (`info` when `qrcode` does not import), Bun (the same `findBun` lookup: on `PATH` only `bun.exe`, then the folders `%BUN_INSTALL%\bin` and `%USERPROFILE%\.bun\bin`), and the autostart (`reg query` of the Run value). When the board check fails, it prints `winget install --id MSYS2.MSYS2 -e` (only when `<root>\usr\bin\pacman.exe` does not exist) and `<root>\usr\bin\pacman.exe -S --needed mingw-w64-ucrt-x86_64-python-gobject mingw-w64-ucrt-x86_64-gtk4 mingw-w64-ucrt-x86_64-libadwaita` (`-Syu` when GTK or Libadwaita is too old, because pacman supports no partial update). `<root>` is the folder before `\ucrt64\bin\python.exe` of the probed board Python, else of the first Python candidate with that form, else `C:\msys64`, so a user with MSYS2 in another folder (from `ASB_PYTHON` or `python` on `PATH`) does not get a second MSYS2. The command has no quotes, because MSYS2 does not support a folder with a space. ASB never runs them. The exit code is 0 when the Engine group is ok, so a missing board does not fail it.
- Readers and opens: the Claude app folder is `%APPDATA%\Claude`, else the first existing `%LOCALAPPDATA%\Packages\Claude_*\LocalCache\Roaming\Claude` (sorted), else `%APPDATA%\Claude`. The remote cache reader is off on win32; the Claude source warns `Claude web sessions are not read on Windows.` only when the cache folder exists. A win32 source launcher must be an existing `.exe` with an app name; the execute bit is not checked. Openers spawn with `windowsHide`. A win32 session link opens with `cmd /c start "" <url>` only when it matches `^[A-Za-z][A-Za-z0-9+.-]*:[A-Za-z0-9/?=._:~-]*$`; else `This session link cannot open on Windows.`.

The `windows-test` CI job (`windows-latest`, Node.js `22.13.0` and `24`) runs the test files that `test/windows-tests.json` lists. The `bridge-windows` job (Bun `1.4.2`) runs `bun run check` and `bun test` in `bridge/`; the bridge tests that need Unix socket files, `/tmp`, modes, links, or signals skip on Windows. Tests that start the full switchboard server or use real home paths are not in the list. `test/asb-package.test.mjs` checks on Linux that each listed file exists. The `board-windows` job (`windows-latest`, Node.js `24`, MSYS2 UCRT64 with the Python, GTK 4, and Libadwaita packages from `msys2/setup-msys2`) runs `test/windows-board.test.mjs` with `ASB_BOARD_SMOKE=1` and `ASB_PYTHON` set to the MSYS2 Python. The test starts a fake engine process and `node scripts/asb.mjs` with a temporary `LOCALAPPDATA`, `APPDATA`, and `ASB_SNAPSHOT`. It waits up to 90 s for the board image, stops the fake engine, and checks that the launcher exits with code 0 within 20 s. Then it starts a second fake engine and runs the command of the Start menu link (`powershell.exe -NoProfile -ExecutionPolicy Bypass -File scripts\asb-tray.ps1 <node.exe> -Open`) with the same folders and a new `ASB_SNAPSHOT`. That PowerShell process waits at most 30 s for the launcher (the cleanup ends it too), so the test waits up to 90 s for the second image, stops the second engine, and checks that the board closes within 20 s. The launcher that `-Open` starts is not a child of the test, so the test finds its board by ancestry: it reads all processes once (`Get-CimInstance Win32_Process`) and takes the descendants, whose command line has `asb-board.py`, of the PowerShell process that it started for the command (the board has no engine URL in its command line, and a match on the command line alone would also find the board of the user's own ASB). The same finder checks that the board exists and that it closes, and ends any board that stays. The file is not in `test/windows-tests.json`, because the `windows-test` job has no GTK.

Python 3 needs PyGObject, GTK `>=4.12`, and Libadwaita `>=1.4`. On Windows, these come from MSYS2 UCRT64 (`mingw-w64-ucrt-x86_64-python-gobject`, `-gtk4`, and `-libadwaita`). The board and the tray run on GTK 4.12, Libadwaita 1.4, and GLib 2.88: Remove and Leave use `Adw.MessageDialog` when `Adw.AlertDialog` (Libadwaita 1.5) does not exist; a message lights its two rows when `Gsk.PathBuilder` (GTK 4.14) does not exist; both scripts have a `unix_signal_add` helper that uses `GLibUnix.signal_add` when it exists (GLib 2.88 has no `GLib.unix_signal_add`, and GLib 2.84 has `GLibUnix` with only `signal_add_full`); and the QR code shows only when `qrcode` imports and `gi.require_foreign("cairo")` works. Node.js `>=22.13` uses built-in SQLite with read-only connections. Older supported Node.js (`>=20`) uses `sqlite3 -init <null device> -readonly -json` (the init file is off, so a `~/.sqliterc` that prints text cannot break the JSON, and the output limit is 20 MiB); the Debian package keeps that dependency. ASB's runtime has no external npm dependencies. Development tests use Node.js `>=22.13` and built-in SQLite fixtures, without the SQLite CLI.

Rust GTK/Libadwaita and Qt/C++ frontends were evaluated as unreleased `0.1.0`
prototypes, then removed with their harnesses. The [Rust comparison](docs/benchmarks/python-rust-2026-10-12.md)
found faster startup and lower frontend PSS, but mixed interaction timing and
similar repaint cadence. Both Python and Rust passed fourteen critical native
groups; [the acceptance record](docs/benchmarks/rust-acceptance-2026-10-12.md)
retains coverage and limits. The [Qt comparison](docs/benchmarks/python-qt-2026-10-13.md)
found no meaningful scroll speed gain in one valid main-monitor manual pair.
Qt had lower resize callback p95 and higher PSS, with different resize paths.
Eight bounded Qt native groups passed. These results do not prove complete UI
parity or physical display FPS. The experiments cannot be run from this tree.
Production ASB uses Python at `1.13.0`.

ASB uses `DashboardSnapshot` in `src/dashboard-snapshot.mjs` for snapshots, source events, watchers, caches, and request coalescing. It also uses local HTTP utilities, Codex open helpers, and provider cache helpers.

The package whitelist (`RUNTIME_FILES`) names 108 runtime files in ASB `1.13.0`: `src/claude-messages.mjs` of the Claude Code messages joined the 107 of the usage backups, where `src/usage-backup.mjs` of the usage backups and `src/usage-ledger.mjs` and `scripts/asb-usage.mjs` of the usage ledger joined the 104 of the top-bar extension, whose eight `gnome-shell/` files joined the 96 of the Messages and Usage windows (`src/activity-messages.mjs`, `src/activity-usage.mjs`, the ten `src/usage-*.mjs` files, of which `src/usage-antigravity.mjs`, `src/usage-grok.mjs`, and `src/usage-pi.mjs` are the newest three, `THIRD_PARTY_NOTICES.md`, and `assets/icons/asb-chat-symbolic.svg`). It named 82 in ASB `1.12.0` and `1.12.1` (`src/bridge-coverage.mjs` joined the 81 of ASB `1.11.0` and `1.11.1`), 75 in ASB `1.10.0`, and 73 in ASB `1.9.0`. In `1.11.0`, `assets/icons/asb-filter-symbolic.svg`, `assets/icons/asb-globe-symbolic.svg`, `assets/icons/asb-link-symbolic.svg`, `bridge/agent-skills/opencode/bridge/SKILL.md`, `bridge/dist/opencode.js`, and `src/memory-trim.mjs` joined the list. In `1.10.0`, the three WAV sounds replaced the three MP3 files, and `scripts/asb-board.py` and `src/reader-positions.mjs` joined it. It includes 12 ASB Bridge files (with `bridge/dist/relay.js`), `assets/icons/asb-drawer-symbolic.svg`, `assets/icons/asb-muted-symbolic.svg`, `src/session-references.mjs`, `src/session-targets.mjs`, `scripts/asb.mjs`, `scripts/asb-setup.mjs`, `src/platform-paths.mjs`, `src/phone-gateway.mjs`, `src/machine-group.mjs`, `src/machine-peers.mjs`, `src/machine-relay.mjs`, `scripts/asb-service.mjs`, `scripts/asb-engine.mjs`, `scripts/asb-tray.py`, `scripts/asb-agent.mjs`, `scripts/asb-group.mjs`, `scripts/asb-instance.mjs`, `scripts/asb-doctor.mjs`, `src/linux-system.mjs`, and `src/linux-distros.mjs`. The published ASB `1.8.1` package shipped 30 runtime files, including the app-source registry. ASB `1.8.0`, `1.7.0`, `1.6.1`, `1.6.0`, `1.5.0`, and `1.4.0` also shipped 30. ASB `1.3.0` shipped 32. ASB `1.2.0` shipped 31, down from 42 in ASB `1.1.0`.

## Main files

| File | Responsibility |
| --- | --- |
| `src/switchboard.mjs` | ASB source selection, state, attention, pins, open validation, and server entry. |
| `src/app-sources.mjs` | App-source registration, validation, profile colors, and local settings. |
| `src/asb-server.mjs` | Restricted ASB HTTP server for native and browser clients. |
| `src/dashboard-snapshot.mjs` | Snapshots, source events, watchers, caches, and request coalescing. |
| `src/local-http.mjs` | JSON and static-file HTTP utilities, the `Accept-Encoding` check, and the gzip list body. |
| `src/session-opener.mjs` | Codex app opens, response fields, and the shared app opener (`runOpener`, `scopedCommand`). |
| `src/data-cache.mjs` | Provider cache helpers. |
| `src/codex-data.mjs` | Read-only Codex database, names, lifecycle, questions, and matched native read marks. |
| `src/claude-data.mjs` | Claude Desktop Code metadata and matched local transcript signals. |
| `src/claude-remote-data.mjs` | Approved cached remote session metadata, cursor-linked updates, and explicit worker state. |
| `src/claude-cli-data.mjs` | Claude Code CLI sessions: live registry records and recent CLI transcripts, as view-only rows. |
| `src/opencode-data.mjs` | Read-only OpenCode 2 database: root sessions, subagent counts, last message times, outcomes, and the native read mark. Also reads the receiver view of the ASB Bridge plugin database (peer ID, status, time, kind; no text). |
| `src/insights.mjs` | Shared thread normalization and root/descendant grouping. |
| `src/asb-sounds.mjs` | PC sounds for Done, Needs input, and Failed: the Android event rules and the `pw-play` player, with `paplay` and `aplay` as fallbacks. On Windows, one hidden `powershell.exe` plays all sounds. |
| `src/phone-gateway.mjs` | Optional phone gateway: settings, TLS certificate, pairing, bearer tokens, throttling, the route allowlist proxy, and the group member routes. |
| `src/machine-group.mjs` | Machine group: `group.json`, merge rules, names, invites, join links, and `createMachineGroup` (flows, pollers, member callbacks). |
| `src/machine-peers.mjs` | Session export, merge of member rows and messages, the pinned peer client, and the member poller. |
| `src/bridge-coverage.mjs` | ASB Bridge coverage: the state of each enabled profile from `asb bridge status --json`, the cached check, the install of one profile or all, the member report, and the states of the group members. |
| `gnome-shell/extension.js` | The ASB top-bar extension for GNOME Shell: the capsule button, its menu, the actions, the engine client, and the panel bus name. |
| `gnome-shell/model.js` | Pure top-bar logic, also imported by Node tests: the runtime file checks, the `/api/panel` reply check, the texts, the machine hues, the event parser, and the inks for light surfaces (`inkOn`, `contrastRatio`, `colorHex`, `ST_ACCENTS`). |
| `gnome-shell/stylesheet.css`, `gnome-shell/metadata.json`, `gnome-shell/icons/` | Look B (Capsule), the extension metadata (GNOME Shell 45 to 49), and copies of four `assets/icons` marks. |
| `src/machine-relay.mjs` | Message relay engine side: the relay body checks, the supervisor of `bridge/dist/relay.js`, the member peer lists, and the relay route handlers. |
| `src/activity-messages.mjs` | Messages activity: one record per ASB Bridge message ID from the local bridge databases (read-only; for the rows of the last 30 days it selects `text` only to make the hash, and keeps only the hash) and the records of `src/claude-messages.mjs`, the status groups and fixed reasons, the merge with the member replies, the join of a send and a receipt with the same hash of the trimmed body without the envelope (the receipt at most 24 hours after the send, the send at most 2 minutes after the receipt, the newest such send, and the same sender seat unless one of the two is `unknown:`), the seats, the text of the newest 200 messages of a member reply (read again from the bridge database or the transcript line, checked against the hash), the thread of a pair or a session (the newest 60 messages, each with its text), and the member reply. |
| `src/claude-messages.mjs` | Claude Code messages, read-only: the `*.jsonl` files directly in each project folder of the projects folder of each enabled Claude profile (default `~/.claude/projects`), changed in the last 30 days. Sent side: `SendMessage` tool calls (and tools whose name ends in `send_message`) with a target (`to` or `session_id`) and a message; a target `main` or a subagent (`a` and 16 hex digits) does not count, and a tool result that reports a failed send gives `Not sent`. Received side: the `<cross-session-message>` delivery record (a meta `user` record, or a `queued_command` prompt that ends with the closing tag); the body is the text between the opening tag and the last closing tag. The record ID is `claude:<tool use ID>` or `claude:<record uuid>`. A socket target (`uds:<path>`) keeps only `uds:`, so no path goes to the group. Its file cache keeps no text: per message the IDs, the time, the status, the name from the message (at most 120 characters), the hash (the first 16 hex digits of SHA-256 of the body), and the place of the line in the file. `text(record)` reads that one line again and gives the body (at most 2,000 characters) only when its hash matches. The engine saves the per-file read state in `usage/claude-message-cache.json` in its cache folder: SHA-256 path keys, file signatures and offsets, record IDs, directions, times, statuses, body hashes, and line locations, never names, paths, or message text. The first read loads the state; it reads the session names and endpoints again from the saved message lines, not the whole transcripts. A grown file with the same inode and first bytes reads only its appended bytes; a replaced, shrunk, or rewritten file starts again. A broken or old-version cache is ignored. A read saves changed state at most once in 5 minutes (mode `0600` in a `0700` folder); a failed save keeps the changes for a retry. No background timer reads messages. On made-up data (3,000 transcripts, 3.84 GB, 60 bridge databases), the first 30-day reply after a restart with saved state took 266 ms, read 2.57 MB in 4,112 known message lines, and scanned no transcript bytes; the state file was 1.17 MB. |
| `src/activity-usage.mjs` | Usage activity: the token scan of the enabled sources and of the Grok, Pi, and Antigravity folders, the private usage cache, the usage ledger merge and its hourly timer scan, the report of this machine, the group members, and the imported machines (with the `maker` of each record), the hourly member reply, and the export and import. |
| `src/usage-codex.mjs` | Codex rollout reader: the `token_count` deltas and the model from `turn_context`. Port of T3 Code. |
| `src/usage-claude.mjs` | Claude transcript reader: the assistant `usage`, one count per message and request ID. Port of T3 Code. |
| `src/usage-opencode.mjs` | OpenCode token usage: `opencode.db` and `opencode-<name>.db`, the tables `session_message` and `message`, a SQLite query that selects only the message ID, the token counts, the model, and the times with JSON functions, pages of 8 rows, and one count per message ID. Port of T3 Code in part. |
| `src/usage-grok.mjs` | Grok reader: the `turn_completed` usage lines of `updates.jsonl`. Port of T3 Code. |
| `src/usage-pi.mjs` | Pi reader: the usage of the assistant, tool result, usage, compaction, and branch summary lines of the session files. The record model is the bare model name (`responseModel` or `model`), which LiteLLM prices; the provider is only in the dedupe key. Port of T3 Code. |
| `src/usage-antigravity.mjs` | Antigravity reader: the folders, and the SQLite `*.db` files with protobuf metadata (`node:sqlite`), with one count per usage ID. Port of T3 Code. |
| `src/usage-pricing.mjs` | The LiteLLM rate table, the model lookup (when the rates of one model name do not agree, the rate of the model's own company counts; a name with a prefix that is not a LiteLLM key, such as `z-ai/glm-5.2`, takes the rate of its bare name), the cost formula (a missing cache rate uses the input rate), and `modelMaker` (the company that makes a model). Port of T3 Code. |
| `src/usage-aggregation.mjs` | The periods, the local hour, day, and month buckets, and the totals per app and model. Port of T3 Code. |
| `src/usage-scan-cache.mjs` | The per-file scan by size and change time (a grown file is read from its offset; a `*.jsonl.zst` file is read again in full), the readers of the Codex, Claude, Grok, and Pi lines, the cache encoding (version 2), and the private JSON files. Port of T3 Code. |
| `src/usage-rates.mjs` | The LiteLLM price list download with its 24-hour disk cache. Port of T3 Code. |
| `src/usage-ledger.mjs` | The usage ledger: its path, the merge by the larger value, the version 1 file, the load that sets a bad file aside, the export document, and the all-or-nothing check of an import. |
| `src/usage-backup.mjs` | The daily usage backups: the settings file, the file name, the write and the rotation, the due rule and its timers, the status, and **Back up now**. |
| `THIRD_PARTY_NOTICES.md` | The source, the commit, the ported files, and the MIT license of T3 Code. |
| `scripts/asb-desktop.mjs` | Native launcher, owned child-process cleanup, phone gateway start, and service attach mode. It starts the window while the engine starts and writes the engine address to the window stdin. |
| `scripts/asb-service.mjs` | Optional `asb service` user service (`run`, `install`, `uninstall`, `status`) and its runtime file. |
| `scripts/asb-agent.mjs` | Local agent status, source commands, partial updates, and saved/backend/UI verification. |
| `scripts/asb.mjs` | The `asb` command and npm bin: dispatches every subcommand; with no command, it opens the board on Linux and on Windows. |
| `scripts/asb-setup.mjs` | `asb setup`: the parts question, autostart, the engine wait, the network addresses, the Windows and Linux firewall text, and the install of a missing ASB Bridge. |
| `scripts/asb-tray.ps1` | Windows tray: one instance, the engine start (before the icon loads), the engine restart limit, **Open ASB**, **Messages**, **Usage**, **Copy phone pairing link**, and **Quit ASB**. **Open ASB** and a left click post `/api/engine/show-window` with `board` first, so an open or minimized board comes to the front; the launcher starts only when no board shows itself. With `-Open` (the Start menu link), it starts the launcher and exits; when the launcher exits with code 3 within 30 s, it first shows `ASB has no window on this PC. Run asb doctor.` in a message box. |
| `src/platform-paths.mjs` | The ASB config, state, and runtime folders on Linux and Windows, and the `PATH` search (`PATHEXT` on Windows). |
| `src/linux-system.mjs` | The Linux tools: the Python candidates (`ASB_PYTHON`, `/usr/bin/python3`, `python3` on `PATH`; on Windows `ASB_PYTHON`, `C:\msys64\ucrt64\bin\python.exe`, `python` on `PATH`), the Python probe, `boardCheck` (Python 3.9, GTK 4.12, Libadwaita 1.4), `cachedBoardCheck` and `forgetBoardCheck` (the saved result for the window launcher only), and `engineCheck` (Node.js 20, SQLite, `openssl`, `xdg-open`). |
| `src/linux-distros.mjs` | The package table of each Linux family, `/etc/os-release` parsing, family detection, install commands, and the Node.js routes. |
| `scripts/asb-doctor.mjs` | `asb doctor [--json]` on Linux and Windows: the Engine, Window, and Options checks, and the install commands that it prints and never runs (on Windows, the `winget` and `pacman` commands for MSYS2 and the board packages). When the engine runs with phone access on and firewalld or ufw is active, it also prints the firewall rules for the selected addresses (`commands.firewall` in the JSON). |
| `scripts/asb-group.mjs` | `asb group`, `asb network`, and `asb phone` commands through the running engine. |
| `scripts/asb-usage.mjs` | `asb usage export`, `asb usage import`, and `asb usage backup` through the running engine. |
| `scripts/asb-instance.mjs` | Shared private runtime descriptor, instance requests, and owner cleanup. |
| `src/memory-trim.mjs` | Engine memory: the GC trim after a scan (`createMemoryTrim`), the V8 flag and the glibc settings of the engine start (`ENGINE_NODE_FLAGS`, `MALLOC_ENV`), and `forgetMallocEnv`. |
| `scripts/asb-board.py` | The board launcher. It keeps the compiled board code in `~/.cache/asb/pycache` (`XDG_CACHE_HOME`; on Windows `%LOCALAPPDATA%\asb\cache\pycache`), not next to the source, because the install refuses a release folder with other files. On Windows it also sets `GSK_RENDERER=cairo` (`prefer_cairo`). On X11 it sets `GSK_RENDERER=cairo` and `GDK_DISABLE=gl,vulkan` unless the user set either variable to a nonempty value; measured RSS/PSS fell from 237.5/133.3 MB to 146.7/95.5 MB and focus repaint from 281 to 640 ms (GL) to 40 to 80 ms (Cairo). |
| `scripts/asb-native.py` | Native rows, filters, search, layout, theme, input, local label clock, the Preferences Phone and Machines pages, the Preferences window made on first use, the Messages and Usage windows, and `LauncherStart` (the engine address from stdin and the early first dashboard). |
| `docs/benchmarks/` | Historical frontend comparisons, numeric samples, acceptance coverage, and X11 timing limits. |
| `scripts/asb-icon.mjs` | ASB icon and desktop entry registration. |
| `scripts/asb-package.mjs` | Linux package build (`.deb`, RPM, `PKGBUILD`, and the portable archive), the `stage` command for the Nix flake, runtime check, and per-user install/uninstall. |
| `flake.nix`, `nix/package.nix` | The Nix flake. It is not in the npm package, the archive, or the packages. |
| `scripts/asb-bridge.mjs` | Optional bridge status (`--claude-home` adds the skill state of each Claude home), per-provider install/uninstall, and explicit Claude registry linking. |
| `.agents/plugins/marketplace.json` | Local `asb` marketplace for the `asb-bridge` Codex plugin. |
| `bridge/` | Optional ASB Bridge plugin, Bun/TypeScript source and tests, committed bundles, and usage skills that the model loads by itself (a short entry in the skill list; the text loads when the task needs it). |
| `android/` | Optional Android companion: Kotlin and Compose app, Gradle build, and JVM tests. See `android/README.md`. |
| `public/switchboard.html` | Optional ASB browser view. |
| `test/switchboard.test.mjs` | ASB data, attention, API, and source-event checks. |
| `test/claude-cli-data.test.mjs` | Claude Code CLI rows: live and recent sessions, exclusions, title order, the 500 limit, the registry wait, the Unknown state of an open task whose process ended, and the source wiring. |
| `test/opencode-data.test.mjs` | OpenCode states, outcomes, read marks, subagents, schema warnings, the WAL cache check, and the read-only column and table limits. |
| `test/asb-desktop.test.mjs` | Isolated launcher, port-conflict, icon-failure, win32 engine start and spawn, window start before the engine is ready and the address on stdin, saved board check delete, and owned-child checks without GTK. |
| `test/phone-gateway.test.mjs` | Gateway address kinds, schema 1 migration, several listeners, caller rules, settings, file modes, pairing, tokens, throttling, allowlist, and proxy checks. |
| `test/machine-group.test.mjs` | Group merge, names, strict validation, `group.json`, invites, join links, and the leave on a tombstone. |
| `test/machine-peers.test.mjs` | Export whitelist, merge, offline rows, messages across machines, the pinned TLS client, and poller back-off. |
| `test/machine-link.test.mjs` | Two in-process engines on `127.0.0.2` and `127.0.0.3`: invite, join, remote cards, actions, sound, offline, leave, and remove. |
| `test/asb-group.test.mjs` | The `asb group`, `asb network`, `asb phone`, and `asb setup` commands (also the parts question and the bridge part, with fake bridge calls) with a fake engine. |
| `test/bridge-coverage.test.mjs` | The profile states of each app, live counts, install commands, the check cache, the install route, the member report, and the member states with their cache and deadline. |
| `test/platform-paths.test.mjs` | The folders, adapter names, certificate script, Claude folder search, URL rule, launcher rule, Run value, firewall text, and Node.js check on both systems. |
| `test/bridge-windows.test.mjs` | The Windows `PATHEXT` search, the `codex.cmd` command line and its refusals, the `link-claude` refusal, and the relay supervisor on win32. |
| `test/asb-windows.test.mjs` | The win32 certificate, `icacls`, remote cache, engine commands, dispatcher, and Python candidates with injected `execFile` and `spawn`. |
| `test/asb-win32-files.test.mjs` | Linux only: the win32 runtime-file rules with real links and modes, Linux credentials, and the tray icon. |
| `test/windows-tests.json` | The test files of the Windows CI job. |
| `test/windows-board.test.mjs` | The board smoke test of the `board-windows` job. It skips unless the system is Windows and `ASB_BOARD_SMOKE=1`. The real launcher opens the real GTK board against a fake engine process, and the test checks the saved window image and the launcher exit code after the engine stops. |
| `test/asb-phone-api.test.mjs` | Loopback phone and service routes, token, Host/Origin, and strict-body checks. |
| `test/asb-service.test.mjs` | Service unit, install/uninstall, runtime file, and attach-mode checks with a fake `systemctl`. |
| `test/asb-agent.test.mjs` | Synthetic CLI, source operations, verification, and runtime safety checks. |
