| `test/asb-agent-api.test.mjs` | Agent authentication, revisions, stale writes, strict UI reports, the closed report, client bounds, and expiry; the `resolve-session`, `resolve-bound`, `list-references`, and `send-member` routes. |
| `test/asb-docs.test.mjs` | ASB install, media, privacy, and credit checks, and the install block of each Linux family in the install guide against `installCommand`. |
| `test/linux-distros.test.mjs` | The package table, `/etc/os-release` parsing, family detection, install commands, the Node.js routes, the CLI, and `test/distro-probe.sh`. |
| `test/linux-system.test.mjs` | The Python candidates, `boardCheck` with fake probe output, the saved board check (key, damaged file, failed check, delete), and `engineCheck` with stub programs on `PATH`. |
| `test/asb-doctor.test.mjs` | The `asb doctor` output for fake systems, the exit codes, the read-only program list, the firewall lines, and the Links check. |
| `test/asb-package-formats.test.mjs` | The RPM spec, the `stage` command, the Nix package, and the same-bytes build of the RPM and the `PKGBUILD`. |
| `test/distros-workflow.test.mjs` | `distros.yml` read as text (the `probe` and `check` rows) and the guards of `test/distro-check.sh`. |
| `test/distro-probe.sh`, `test/distro-check.sh` | The POSIX `sh` scripts that the CI jobs run inside each image. |
| `test/preferences-check.py` | Step 11 of a `check` row with `preferences: check` (no row has it since the `alma-9` rows left CI; it also runs by hand): Preferences at 420 px, Profiles, and the Claude profile dialog with the installed board script, made-up profiles, and no engine. |
| `test/asb_native_logic_test.py` | Native data, layout, theme, search/filter, scheduling, and row-reuse checks with no GTK display, the machine texts, join link summary, and group requests, and the renderer rule of `scripts/asb-board.py`. |
| `test/asb_native_test.py` | Native GTK checks on a hidden display, including the Preferences pages, the Machines page flows and dialogs, the Usage page, and the narrow section list. |
| `docs/status-mapping.html` | App support table, rename notes, and status mapping worksheet; update it when app support, a state, an icon, or a Pending rule changes. `README.md` has the same support table; `test/asb-docs.test.mjs` keeps it equal to the `FEATURES` and `SUPPORT` lists of the page, so change both. |
| `demo/` | Separate Remotion project with synthetic public demo data. `demo/message-light.mjs` (Node.js, no packages) writes the README animation `docs/media/board-message-light.svg`: the board message light, with made-up sessions, as an animated SVG (SMIL; no motion with reduced motion). `demo/messages-window.mjs` and `demo/usage-window.mjs` (also Node.js, no packages) draw the scene files `demo/messages-window.json` and `demo/usage-window.json` (the geometry, texts, and colors of the GTK windows, measured with the fixtures of `test/fixtures/activity/`) as the README animations `docs/media/messages-window.svg` and `docs/media/usage-window.svg`. `demo/hero.mjs` (Node.js, no packages) writes the README hero `docs/media/asb-hero.svg` (960 × 540, a 30-second loop) with three modules: `demo/hero-board.mjs` draws `demo/hero-board.json` (the GTK board in the Comfortable view, measured with made-up sessions; a card that glides is opaque), `demo/hero-apps.mjs` draws illustrated Claude, ChatGPT (Codex tab), and OpenCode windows, and `demo/hero-smil.mjs` has the SMIL timeline, camera, and checks. |

## Sources and list scope

ASB loads enabled app sources independently. One failed source does not remove another source's rows. The default sources use these paths:

| Source | Read path |
| --- | --- |
| Codex | Latest `~/.codex/state_N.sqlite`, `session_index.jsonl`, `.codex-global-state.json`, and matched `sessions/**/rollout-*.jsonl`. In the `threads` query, a column that the table does not have reads as null. When the read with the `thread_goals` join fails, the reader reads the threads again without goals. `readThreads` keeps the last result for each database path, with its limit and the signature of the database and its `-wal` file (`dev`, `ino`, `size`, `mtimeMs`; a missing `-wal` is part of the signature). The same signature skips the `pragma` and the thread query. The kept rows are frozen: a scan (`readThreadRows`) reads them and builds new thread objects, and `readThreads` gives new shallow row objects. A signature is stable only when both files changed more than one second before the `stat`; else the query runs. `invalidateCodexData` drops the kept result (for a file hint, only that of the database). |
| Claude Desktop Code on Linux | `$XDG_CONFIG_HOME/Claude/claude-code-sessions/local_*.json`, or `~/.config/Claude/claude-code-sessions/local_*.json`, in any subfolder except `waiting-input/` (the messages that wait for a busy session, under its ID). The app approval log at `<appDir>/logs/main.log` is a best-effort wait source. |
| Claude transcript signals | Matched JSONL files under `~/.claude/projects`. |
| Claude Code CLI sessions | Live registry records in `~/.claude/sessions/<pid>.json` with `entrypoint` `cli`, and root JSONL files under `~/.claude/projects` with `entrypoint` `cli`. See [Claude Code CLI sessions](#claude-code-cli-sessions). |
| Claude remote Code | Session-list and watch response bodies in Claude's `Cache/Cache_Data`. |
| ASB Bridge messages | Read-only `<CODEX_HOME>/plugin-state/asb-bridge/<thread>.sqlite` files, for session messages only. |
| OpenCode | Read-only `$XDG_DATA_HOME/opencode/opencode.db`, or `~/.local/share/opencode/opencode.db`. From `session_v2`, root sessions (`parent_id is null`) with `id`, `title`, `slug`, `directory`, `time_created`, `time_updated`, `time_suspended`, `time_idle`, `idle_outcome`, `time_viewed`, and `time_archived`, and the count of subagent sessions per `parent_id`. From `session_message`, only `session_id` and `time_created`, for the last message time of each root and its subagents. Also reads the receiver view of the ASB Bridge plugin database at `<OpenCode state>/plugin-state/asb-bridge/` (peer ID, status, time, kind; no text). |

### App-source registry

`src/app-sources.mjs` stores up to eight sources in `~/.config/asb/sources.json`, with owner-only `0600` access. XDG config overrides apply. Each source has a stable ID, provider, label, color, dot visibility, data folder, installed launcher, and enabled flag. Claude can also set a transcript folder. The three default sources (Codex, Claude Desktop Code, and OpenCode) stay registered; users can edit or disable them. Additional sources can be removed. The same physical store cannot be registered twice. Each source also has a name: the lowercase label with each run of other characters changed to `-`, trimmed, and at most 80 characters, or the source id when that is empty. ASB never stores the name: `GET /api/sources` and source write replies report it, and it does not change the settings revision. A save fails with `400` when the new name is the name or id of another source. Older settings can still contain equal names.

The OpenCode source has the ID and provider `opencode`. Its folder is fixed: `$XDG_DATA_HOME/opencode`, else `~/.local/share/opencode`. It has no launcher, because OpenCode 2 has no link that opens one session. A saved `sources.json` without it stays valid: the registry adds the default OpenCode source in memory after the saved sources, and the next save writes it. A file that already has eight sources does not get it. When the saved file has the `opencode` source, the registry replaces its saved folder with the default folder and its launcher with an empty value. A saved source with the ID `opencode` and another provider, or with the provider `opencode` and another ID, makes the file invalid. A new source with the provider `opencode` gets `400` (`Only the default OpenCode source is supported.`). An edit of the OpenCode source that changes the folder or sets a launcher gets `400`. Name, color, dot, and the enabled flag can change.

A Codex source reads its `CODEX_HOME` folder with the same relative files as the default source. A Claude source reads its app profile folder, including `claude-code-sessions` and `Cache/Cache_Data`; its transcript folder defaults to `~/.claude/projects`. The app profile folder and its `claude-code-sessions` folder can be absent, for example on a PC with only Claude Code CLI. Then the source lists only the CLI sessions, with no warning. It is ready when it has CLI sessions, else missing. This supports separate profiles that use one app binary with isolated `CODEX_HOME` and Electron profile folders. On the first registry read, when `sources.json` is absent, ASB registers the known ChatGPT Personal profile only if its local store and installed launcher are present. ASB does not change logins, credentials, app data, or default URL handlers. ChatGPT-labeled sources expose local Codex coding chats; ordinary ChatGPT cloud chat history is outside this source.

Default sources keep their existing row IDs. Additional sources prefix row and parent IDs with the stable source ID, while `externalId` keeps the original app ID for opens. Equal app IDs in different stores remain separate. Rows provide `sourceId`, `sourceLabel`, read-only `sourceColor`, and `sourceShowMarker`. The compatibility fields `sourceNumber` and `sourceCount` remain metadata; numbers are not visible. Numbers start at one per provider in registry order, including disabled sources. Removal can change numbers, but source IDs define identity. Colors do not merge sources or chats. Disabling or removing a source does not delete ASB attention, pins, unread marks, or settings.

Source settings schema `1` accepts optional `color` as a six-digit hex value and saves it in lowercase. Old settings without a color get a stable default from the source ID. An edit that omits `color` keeps the existing color. Dark picker colors also save; native rendering adjusts only the displayed color to keep 3:1 contrast on the current base and hover backgrounds. Optional boolean `showMarker` defaults to true for old settings. An edit that omits it keeps the existing value. False hides only the profile dot in both views; color, source reads, opens, state, unread, and pins stay unchanged. Claude sources accept optional `remoteClaude` (`auto`, `bridge`, or `native`; absent means `auto`) for the message relay (plan 9.6). Other values give 400 `Remote Claude sessions must be Automatic, Through ASB, or Claude Remote Control only.`; other providers refuse the field. An edit that omits it keeps the existing value. It counts in the source revision, and `asb agent sources` reads and edits it. The native Profiles form shows it as **Remote Claude sessions** (Automatic, Through ASB, Claude Remote Control only) under Transcript folder, only for Claude sources, and sends it on Save. The native editor uses explicit Save and shows save success or errors.

`appSourcesRevision` hashes ordered, normalized source settings into an opaque 64-character lowercase hex revision. Counts, provider status, and other report fields do not affect it. `GET /api/sources` returns `revision`; the dashboard returns `sourcesRevision` from the exact source configuration used by its scan. The revision is not stored in `sources.json`, which remains schema `1`. Update and Remove check an optional quoted `If-Match` revision inside the registry's serialized write queue after reading current settings. A stale revision gives `409` with no write; a malformed header gives `400`. Older clients without the header keep their existing behavior.

Additional profiles need a supported installed launcher path. ASB sends that launcher one validated app URI through detached `spawn`, without a shell. The launched app runs independently after dispatch; ASB does not wait for or stop it. Default sources can leave the launcher empty to use the existing URL handler. Each profile's files and directories use the shared debounced watchers and existing polling fallback.

Root chats are the list unit. Explicit workers and subagents stay grouped under the root. ASB omits `subagent` and `guardian_review` source rows. It also omits the `/btw` side chats of the Claude app, archived or not: their session file has `spawnedFrom.btw: true` (seen in the app's files, not a published API), so the thread gets `sideChat: true` and `isSwitchboardRoot` leaves it out. The loader keeps the thread, so the CLI list still knows its CLI session ID and never lists its transcript as a CLI row. Hand-offs, user forks, and side sessions that another session started have no `btw` and stay. Codex side chats need no rule: Codex makes them ephemeral and never writes them to `state_5.sqlite`. Each source has a 5000-record limit. ASB does not discover orphan Codex rollouts outside the database. Archived rows are available but hidden by default.

The row time (`updatedAtMs`) is the time of the last turn. It is the "N ago" of the card, the sort time of read rows, and the time in the browser view, the phone API, and the machine group export. A Working row sends its turn start (`workingSinceMs`) in its place, or, when it has none, the row time of the first scan in which it was Working (the engine keeps these times by row ID and forgets a row that stops working or leaves). Its last event changes at almost each scan, and each session writes at its own time, so the exact value (and the 10 s steps before it) moved the list tag, and each client downloaded the full list. The rows of the list (`threads` of `/api/dashboard`, also with member rows), the member export, and the phone list are in the order of the sent time (newest first), then the row ID; the member export also lists the message keys of each row sorted and in row order. The board and the phone sort and label Working rows by `workingSinceMs`. Opening a chat must not change it, so each reader leaves out the entries that an open or a resume writes with no turn:

- Codex: the time of the last rollout entry that is not `thread_settings_applied` (the app writes it when a chat opens, and Codex moves `threads.updated_at` with it), `session_meta`, or `turn_context`. `attachRolloutSignals` takes it from the tail that the reader already reads (`latestEventAtMs`), else from the last lifecycle time of the full scan, so it is the same after an engine restart with saved reader positions and after a full read. A rollout with no such entry, or no readable rollout, keeps `threads.updated_at`.
- Claude Desktop Code: the newer of `lastActivityAt` and the last `user`, `assistant`, or `result` record of the transcript (`latestEventAtMs`). The modification time of the `local_<id>.json` session file is only the last fallback, because the app rewrites that file when a chat opens (it also has `lastFocusedAt` and `indexedAt`, which ASB does not read). Records with no turn (`attachment`, `queue-operation`, `pr-link`, `frame-link`, `last-prompt`, and the title and mode notes) do not count.
- The group time of a Claude row (`embeddedSubagentUpdatedAtMs`) is the last turn record of a linked child that still works. The file time of a child log does not count: a copy or an open changes it with no turn.
- Claude Code CLI: the last such turn record. The transcript file time and the start time of the live process are used only when the transcript has no turn record.
- OpenCode: the newer of `time_idle` and the newest message time of the session and its subagents. `time_updated` counts only when the session has neither: OpenCode moves it with no turn (in a real store, 8 of 39 sessions had it 43 min to 11 days after their last message, each time when another session got a turn). `time_viewed` is not part of it.
- Claude remote (cloud) rows from the HTTP cache: `last_event_at`, else `updated_at`, else `created_at`. The server moves `updated_at` when the record changes with no turn (the read state, the connection state, the title).

Codex names prefer the desktop/session-index name, then the raw stored title, with the existing empty fallback. ASB does not select `first_user_message` for title derivation. A row title is limited to 300 characters; a longer title ends with `…`. Whole Unicode code points are kept. Codex stores can hold a full prompt as a title, so the thread query selects only `substr(threads.title, 1, 301)` on both SQLite paths: 301 is one more than the limit, so the cut still shows, and a large store does not pass the 20 MiB output limit of `sqlite3`. Search and the tooltip use the limited title. Claude Desktop Code titles keep their own shorter limit. Claude metadata must have a valid `local_<uuid>` ID to provide an open action. App links are constructed from valid IDs:

- `codex://threads/<uuid>`
- `claude://code/continue?session=local_<uuid>`
- `claude://code/<validated cse_ or session_ ID>`

Default app opens use the source apps' registered URL handlers. Registered profile launchers receive the same validated links. ASB does not create chats or run a CLI resume command for its open action.

Remote cache reads accept only production `https://claude.ai/v1/code/sessions` and `/watch` response bodies. They accept any query parameters on these two paths, because the Claude app adds new ones (for example `keepalive_tolerance_seconds`). `resume_token` gives the start cursor. HTTP headers, credentials, transcript-event endpoints, and raw account/config fields are not used. Completed cache entries use Chromium stream boundaries; open gzip watches use their partial body and zero-filled reserved tail. ASB selects the newest response and exact cursor-linked continuations, not a union of old login streams. Missing full-list metadata produces a source warning and only observed rows. Cache delay, eviction, and offline periods can limit coverage. No remote request is made.

The bridge aliases of desktop rows and Claude Code CLI rows deduplicate matching remote rows. For bridge records, validated `session_<token>` and `cse_<token>` links use the same identity. ASB keeps the local row and its folder, and the direct-open link of a desktop row. Invalid aliases and aliases with more than one local owner cannot remove a remote row. Cloud rows require an exact stored alias. Titles and times do not establish identity. Aliases stay outside the view payload.

Remote execution requires a response observation within six hours plus explicit worker/session state. Server sync receipt cursors provide observation time; file modification time is a fallback for an approved body write. An observation up to five minutes after the PC clock counts, because the cursor time is server time and the PC clock can run behind. Bridge sessions also need an explicit connected state. Cloud sessions do not need that bridge field. Unknown, stale, or disconnected records give Unknown. Remote Working has no inferred start time. Folders stay empty; safe Git source names can identify a project. Fresh cached unread marks use the same ASB attention rules as other native marks.

### Claude Code CLI sessions

`loadClaudeCliThreads` in `src/claude-cli-data.mjs` reads Claude Code CLI sessions for each enabled Claude source, also when the source's app profile folder does not exist. `loadSwitchboardClaudeThreads` calls it after the desktop and remote reads and puts its rows after the desktop rows. It passes `desktopSessionIds`: each `cliSessionId` and `priorCliSessionIds` of the desktop threads of the same source, so a session that the Desktop app owns stays a desktop row. It also passes the watch file scan (`fileScan`), so the CLI pass takes the cached stat of a project folder or a root transcript that the watchers saw no change in (`claudeFileStat`), as the desktop pass does. A CLI read failure gives a source warning and no CLI rows; the source fails only when the desktop read fails and the remote and CLI lists are empty.

- Live sessions come from `readLiveClaudeRegistry` in `src/claude-registry.mjs` (the same checks as for the waiting status, on Linux and Windows) for `<config home>/sessions`, with `entrypoint` `cli`. In one `loadSwitchboardDashboard` call, `readLiveClaudeRegistryFolder` reads each real registry folder once for the desktop and CLI passes of all Claude sources (a `Map` of the read promises by real path, made for each call); a record read allocates a buffer of the file size. A record file with the same lstat (device, inode, size, modification and change times) as at its last read is not opened again, and the parsed record is kept; a file that changed in the 2 s before its read is read again at the next read, because file times are coarse. The process check of each record runs at each read, because a process can end with no file change. The reader also keeps `startedAt`, `bridgeSessionId` (only `cse_` or `session_` IDs), and `messagingSocketPath` (only for `peerProtocol: 1`). It never opens the `.key` files in that folder.
- Transcripts come from the project index of `src/claude-data.mjs`. Only root files (`projects/<project>/<id>.jsonl`) count; subagent and workflow logs do not. A root file is read when its current `mtimeMs` is in the last 7 days or its session is live, and its ID is not in `desktopSessionIds` (the newest 500 such files). It is a row when its signals have `entrypoint` `cli` and its last turn (`latestEventAtMs`, else the file time) is in the last 7 days, or its session is live: a resume or a copy can change the file time with no turn.
- A live session with no transcript yet is a row with `cliNoTranscript: true`; `switchboardStatus` gives it Idle with the reason `The CLI session has no task yet.`
- A transcript with an open task (`lifecycleRunning` true) and no live registry record of its session gets `cliProcessEnded: true`, and `switchboardStatus` gives it Unknown with the reason `The CLI process ended during a task.` (a task that is open in the log but whose process was killed, ran out of memory, or ended with a reboot would stay Working for six hours, and the board would scan every 2 seconds in that time). The record is matched like the waiting status (`liveClaudeRecord`: the session ID, `priorCliSessionIds`, and `hostSessionId`), among the records of all entrypoints, so a live process of another entrypoint is not an ended process. The rule applies only when the registry folder exists, the platform is Linux or Windows (the systems with a registry reader), and the read worked: with no folder (an old CLI), on another system, or after a read error, the card keeps the state of the transcript. A turn in the last 10 seconds does not count (`PROCESS_START_GRACE_MS`), because a new or switched session writes its transcript a moment before its record names it. Only the Working result changes: a card with no open task stays Idle, and a Waiting card stays Waiting. The change shows at the next scan: the 30-second safety scan finds a process that ended with no exit write, because that has no file event. A CLI in another PID namespace (a container with the folder mounted) has no verifiable record, so its open task shows Unknown. Codex CLI rows do not use the registry.
- A row has `id` `cli:<session ID>`, `externalId` the session ID, `provider` `claude-desktop-code`, `source` `claude-cli`, `surface` `cli`, `cliSessionId`, and the same transcript state rules as a desktop row, with the same best-effort registry `waiting` status. `bridgeSessionIds` holds the live `bridgeSessionId`, so messages resolve to the row.
- The title is the registry `name`, then the last `customTitle` of a `custom-title` record, then the last `agentName` of an `agent-name` record, else `Claude Code session <first 8 characters of the ID>`. ASB reads no other field of these records, does not read `bridge-session` records, and never uses prompt text as a title.
- Two Claude sources that share a registry folder (`asb bridge link-claude`) can list the same live session. `loadSwitchboardDashboard` keeps one `claude-cli` row per `externalId`: the row with a transcript, else the first in source order.

### Session messages

The dashboard has the top-level list `messages`: `[{ from, to, atMs }]`. Each entry is one message that one chat row sent to another in the last two minutes. `from` and `to` are row IDs, and `atMs` is a time in milliseconds. The native window animates these entries: a light flies between the two rows when GNOME animations are on and `Gsk.PathBuilder` (GTK 4.14) exists; else both rows show the accent color for 2 s. A light that comes during a card motion starts when the motion ends, between the final card places. While the board window is not active, a refresh moves the cards with no glide, and a new message plays no light, lights no card, and counts as seen; a glide or light that already runs finishes. The list is always present. It holds at most the newest 50 entries, sorted by `atMs`, then `from`, then `to`. Exact duplicates are removed.

ASB uses receiver-side records only, so each message counts once. There are three sources:

- Claude transcript: each incoming message has one delivery record. The first form is a meta `user` record whose text has the `<cross-session-message` opening tag in its first 200 characters, after one plain system line. The second form is a `queued_command` attachment whose string `prompt` starts with the tag. A tag after other markup, for example in a task notification, does not count. A `user` record that is not meta, or an array prompt, does not count. The lifecycle reader parses only the opening tag. The sender is `from-session` when it is `local_<uuid>` or a UUID. Otherwise it is `from` when it is `local_<uuid>` or `bridge:session_<token>` or `bridge:cse_<token>`. Other records are ignored. The lifecycle state keeps the last 20 senders and record times in file order. Thus an incremental read and a full read give the same list, and the full lifecycle scan of a large transcript also collects them. Desktop threads get `incomingMessages` and `priorCliSessionIds` from the metadata. Remote cache threads get neither field. For the Messages window, `src/claude-messages.mjs` reads the same two forms (the `queued_command` prompt must also end with `</cross-session-message>`): the body is the text between the opening tag and the last closing tag, trimmed, and the name is `from-name` or `name` (at most 120 characters). It also reads the sent side: an assistant `tool_use` named `SendMessage` (or ending in `send_message`) with a string `to` or `session_id` and a message that is not empty.
- ASB Bridge database: `readBridgeMessages` in `src/codex-data.mjs` lists `<CODEX_HOME>/plugin-state/asb-bridge/`. The file name is the receiving Codex thread ID. A file whose newest time (main file or `-wal`) is older than the window is not opened. The reader checks the `messages` columns, then selects only `peer_id`, `status`, `created_at`, and `kind` (when present) of `in` rows inside the window. It keeps the statuses `accepted`, `started`, and `steered`, and only `kind = 'message'` when the column exists. `peer_id` must be a UUID. `created_at` is in seconds. A per-file cache signature includes the `-wal` stat, because a WAL append does not change the main file. A read error skips only that file. `loadCodexDashboard` attaches `incomingMessages` to the receiving thread.
- ASB Bridge OpenCode plugin database: `readBridgeMessages` reads `<OpenCode state>/plugin-state/asb-bridge/`. OpenCode state is `$XDG_STATE_HOME/opencode`, else `~/.local/state/opencode`. The file name is the bridge ID of the receiving session (UUID v5 of `opencode:<ses_...>`). `loadOpenCodeDashboard` attaches `incomingMessages` to the session with that bridge ID on each load, also when its database cache hits. The reader uses the same receiver view as for Codex (peer ID, status, time, kind; no text).

`resolveSessionMessages` in `src/switchboard.mjs` maps native IDs to row IDs across all sources. Codex rows use the thread UUID. OpenCode rows use the session ID and its bridge ID (the `from-session` of the messages that the session sends). Claude rows use `local_<uuid>`, `cliSessionId`, each of `priorCliSessionIds`, and `bridge:session_<token>` and `bridge:cse_<token>` for each bridge alias. Remote Claude rows use the same two `bridge:` keys. A thread that is not a row, such as a Codex subagent, resolves to its root row. A key with two different rows is not used. ASB keeps a message only when both rows are on the board, the sender is not the receiver, and the time is in the window (up to five seconds in the future). Rows never get `incomingMessages` or `priorCliSessionIds`. The payload has no message text, sender name, or address. No new watcher is added: the normal refresh (two seconds while Working, else five seconds) reads new messages.

In a machine group, the merge also resolves messages between sessions on different members (see Machine group).

These messages are not shown: messages to a session that is not a card (a Claude CLI session with no live record and no transcript in the last 7 days, or a remote receiver), messages between subagents inside one session, and messages written in an old CLI transcript of a chat.

### Usage limits

Each row has the number `limitResetsAtMs`, a time in milliseconds. It is greater than 0 only when a usage limit stopped that session and the reset is still in the future. Else it is 0. The native window greys out such a card and shows "Resets in" with a countdown. The card is also a Stopped card (see Execution and attention). There are two sources:

- Codex rollout: a full window does not stop a session, because an account with credits keeps working past it. The stop is the end of the turn: a `task_complete` record whose `error` is an object with `codex_error_info` `usage_limit_exceeded`. Other `codex_error_info` values (for example `cyber_policy` and `server_overloaded`) are not limits. ASB never reads `error.message`. Codex can refuse the next request before a window shows 100 percent (the last window record often shows 94-98 percent), and the failed turn writes one more `token_count` with `rate_limits.limit_id` `premium` and `primary` and `secondary` null. The rollout lifecycle (`applyRolloutLifecycleEvent`) keeps three values. The stop flag: `task_started` clears it, that error sets it, and any other turn end clears it. The stop also keeps its `task_complete` time `limitStoppedAtMs` (cleared with the flag). The window record: for each `token_count` with at least one non-null window object (`rate_limits.primary` or `rate_limits.secondary`), `limitWindowAtMs` is the record time and `limitWindows` keeps each window as `[used_percent, resets_at (in seconds) times 1000]`. It stays across turns. A `token_count` with no window (for example `premium`) changes nothing. Plus accounts have a 300-minute primary and a 10080-minute secondary window. Pro accounts have only a 10080-minute primary window, so a reset can be up to 7 days away. `rate_limit_reached_type`, `limit_name`, `credits`, and `plan_type` are not used. The thread gets `limitResetsAtMs` when the latest turn ended with the stop and the window record is at or before the stop. `limitStopResetsAtMs` then takes, among the windows of that record that reset after the stop and show 90 percent or more (`LIMIT_STOP_USED_PERCENT`), the most-used one (highest `used_percent`; on a tie the later reset). With no such window it gives 0. So a full 5-hour window that reset before the stop gives way to a full weekly window, and a low snapshot (for example from usage on another machine) cannot give a false reset days away. An old reset that a later retry stop leaves in the log does not count, because only a reset after the stop counts. When the tail has the stop but no window record, the reader scans the full log for the newest window record. The lifecycle scan parses a `token_count` line only when its raw text has a `used_percent` number, so other lines are skipped with one regular expression test. A retry turn can fail at once with no window record. `loadCodexDashboard` then sets the reset of each stopped thread from the newest window record at or before its stop, chosen among its own record and the records of all threads of the same Codex home (the own record wins a tie), with the same window rule. A newest record that fails the rule gives 0; an older record is not tried. A stop with no known reset gives 0. `limitWindowAtMs`, `limitWindows`, and `limitStoppedAtMs` are internal thread fields and are not on the rows.
- Claude transcript: the lifecycle state (`applyClaudeLifecycle`, which also runs in the full lifecycle scan of a large transcript) keeps the limit. An assistant record with `isApiErrorMessage: true`, `error: "rate_limit"`, and a number `quotaLimits.resetsAt` (in seconds) sets it. A new human prompt, a task start, or a later assistant record that is not such an error sets it to 0. Desktop threads get `limitResetsAtMs`. Remote cache rows get 0.

`buildSwitchboardDashboard` gives a row the thread limit only when it is after `nowMs` and the row is not Working and not Waiting with a required action. The state of a limited row stays Idle. A usage limit does not hide attention. A limit stop is a failed end: an Idle row whose thread has `limitStoppedAtMs` or `limitResetsAtMs` above 0 gets `lastOutcome` `limit`, `failedAtMs` at the end time, `completionAtMs` 0, and the Discard task kind `failed`. These thread values stay after the reset until the next task, so the outcome does not change at the reset. The row thus gets failed attention, the `!` mark, and Pending until Read. Discard and Mute do not hide it. The reset times are in the expiries of `nextStatusCheckAtMs`, so ASB builds the cached dashboard again at the reset.

Limits: a Claude limit record without `quotaLimits` (model limits and the older text form) gives no limited state. ASB does not parse the message text. Only the session that the limit stopped is limited, not every session of the profile.

## Execution and attention

Execution uses Working, Waiting, Idle, and Unknown. A root is not Working only because it is unarchived. Open task/request signals require activity within six hours. Source signals can lag or survive a crash; old open signals become Unknown.

Codex roots include unarchived descendants with explicit database links, including nested subagents. The existing bounded rollout cache reads their lifecycle. A recent open child task keeps the root Working after the root's own task ends. Only recent open members set the current start time. Missing or stale child lifecycle gives Unknown unless another member has current work. Archived, unrelated, and orphan rollouts do not change root execution. A rollout that ASB cannot read (a missing file or a read error) gives empty signals, and its card shows Unknown. When ASB cannot read the rollout of any unarchived root that has a rollout path, `loadCodexDashboard` reports the provider status `warning` with the message `ASB cannot read the Codex session logs, so Codex cards show Unknown.`; one readable rollout, also one with no event, keeps the status `ready`. Archived rows and linked children do not count. A cached read and a saved reader position give the same status as a full read: the `stat` and the tail read come first and fail alone, so a saved position does not hide a missing log.

When the app moves a session into a worktree, it copies the root transcript `projects/<project>/<cliSessionId>.jsonl` into the new project folder and writes only to that copy, so the Desktop Code and CLI readers use the copy with the newest current `mtimeMs` when a session ID has more than one copy. The Claude Desktop Code reader includes child files under the matched root transcript in the child count. An async Agent launch links its returned agent ID to the child file `agent-<agentId>.jsonl`. The reader uses the file in the `subagents` folder of that exact root first. The desktop app can give a chat a new CLI session ID, and then the child files stay in the folder of the old ID. Thus, when the root's own folder does not have the file, the reader uses the file that is directly in the `subagents` folder of another session in the same project folder (the folder that holds the root transcript). With more than one such file, it uses the one with the newest current `mtimeMs`. It never uses a file from a different project folder. The child count includes only the root's own files. A background workflow has a different link. Its launch result is the result of a `Workflow` tool call with the status `async_launched`, a `taskId`, and a `runId`; it has no agent ID. The key of the link is the `taskId`, and the link keeps the `runId`. The run writes its logs (one `agent-<id>.jsonl` for each workflow agent, and `journal.jsonl`) to the folder `subagents/workflows/<runId>` of a session folder. These logs have no end record. Thus the link is open work when at least one `.jsonl` file directly in that folder has a current `mtimeMs` at or after the time of the launch call; the newest such `mtimeMs` is the activity time. The reader uses the run folder of the root's own session folder first. When the root has no such folder, it uses the folder with the same `runId` in another session folder of the same project folder, and never one in a different project folder. With no folder, or with no file at or after the launch call, the work of the link is unknown. Only the task notification with the `taskId` closes the link. A resumed run has a new launch result with its own `taskId`, so it is a new link. The files of a run are not in the child count. A `SendMessage` result with `success: true` and a valid `resumedAgentId` opens the link of that subagent again, or creates the link when the launch is not in the transcript. In the child transcript, the message that starts an ended subagent again is a meta `user` record with `isSidechain: true`; it starts a task when the child has no open task. A meta record in a root transcript starts no task, except a message from another session in an idle chat (below). The summary record of a context compaction (a `user` record with `isCompactSummary`) is not a prompt. It starts no task and does not change the start time of the open task. The app writes the records of the open turn after the summary with older timestamps, and these records stay in order because the summary does not move the event time. An assistant record that has a `tool_use` block is not the end of a turn, also when its stop reason is `refusal`, because the app runs the tool. A task notification has two record forms in the root transcript. The first form is a `user` record whose string content starts with `<task-notification>`; its `timestamp` is the delivery time. The second form is an `attachment` record with `attachment.type` `queued_command` and the notification text in the string `attachment.prompt`; the app uses it when the chat is busy. The `timestamp` of the second form is the end of the task, not the delivery time, so the record can come after records with a newer timestamp. In each form, a notification with the status `completed`, `failed`, `cancelled`, `aborted`, `stopped`, or `killed` closes the link, but only when its timestamp is not before the launch or resume time of the link. `completed` gives a completion, `failed` gives a failure, and the other statuses give a cancellation. A notification without a `<status>` tag closes no link. The first form is also a prompt: the app runs the model on it, so it starts a task like a human prompt (also for a task that is not linked). It starts no task when its status is `stopped` or `killed` (these usually get no reply) or when the record has `queueTranscriptOnly: true`. Thus a linked failed or cancelled notification at an idle root does not end the group at the notification; the end of the reply turn sets the outcome. The reply turn continues the task of the prompt for Discard: it sets the Working timer start but not `latestTaskStartedAtMs`, so a Discard armed while the background agent ran stays armed and applies to the end of the reply. An `attachment` record changes nothing else, and `queue-operation` records are ignored. The child log writes its end 0.1 to 2 seconds before the notification reaches the root. Thus an open link (no notification yet) whose child log has ended counts as open work for 10 seconds after the child end (`CLAUDE_CHILD_END_GRACE_MS`), with the child end as its activity time. The thread field `childEndGraceAtMs` (internal, not on the rows) is the earliest such grace end, and it is in the expiries of `nextStatusCheckAtMs`, so ASB builds the cached dashboard again when the grace ends. After the grace, the child end counts as before. A lost notification thus gives at most about 10 seconds of Working. Recent child request, thinking, and tool events keep the root Working after its own response ends. Child completion, interruption, and error events close that work. Unrelated children cannot change execution. Missing linked lifecycle gives Unknown unless the root has current work. Stale child work cannot set a fresh root's timer start.

Each non-empty Claude user reply starts a task, including `ok`, `y`, `1`, `.`, and `继续`. A user record that is not meta and has an image item and no `tool_result` item (a prompt with only images) also starts a task. A message from another session (the meta `user` record form in [Session messages](#session-messages), when `crossSessionSender` accepts it) starts a task only when the root is not running; during a running turn it does not move the start time. Background Bash (`run_in_background`) and Monitor tasks are not linked: the model is idle while they run. Local command records (`<local-command-caveat>`, `<command-name>`, `<local-command-stdout>`) do not start a task. Both readers skip a JSONL line that is not an object.

Current synchronous questions can block execution in Waiting. Async questions add attention while Working or Idle remains visible. Matching answers, failure, abort, or cancellation resolve the question. Real human input supersedes old questions. Automatic goal continuation, context, and partial answers do not. Question and answer bodies stay outside ASB view payloads.

A pending Claude `AskUserQuestion` tool call is a question. It gives Waiting and question attention. If it is the only pending tool and a linked child has recent open work, the row stays Working with question attention. The Claude Desktop app can end a turn with the `mcp__ccd_turn__wrap_up` tool. The tool result ends the turn as `task_complete` (also in the lifecycle-only scan of a large file). The model can still write records after it: usually an `end_turn` text and a `stop_hook_summary` within seconds, sometimes more tool calls. Some turns have no later end record. Later ends keep the question, and an active assistant record clears it. When the wrap-up input has a non-empty `ask`, ASB records a pending `AskUserQuestion` under the key `wrap-up:<tool use id>`, so the same question rules apply. No tool result clears it: the next root turn start (a human prompt, a delivered wake, `task_started`, or an active assistant record), a cancel, a failure, or a result clears it. ASB keeps only whether `ask` is non-empty, never the ask text, the recap, or the actions. A wrap-up without `ask` is a normal completion. Other pending permission tools, including `ExitPlanMode`, give Waiting with a passive `?` until a tool result or terminal event resolves them. A pending Claude permission, plan approval, or question tool gives Waiting and Pending only for six hours after its tool event. After that, the usual rules apply, and an old open task gives Unknown. A fresh remote worker `requires_action` uses the same wait rule. Ordinary Bash, Edit, Write, and MCP permission prompts write no transcript record. On each Claude load, ASB reads the live Claude session registry (`<config>/sessions/<pid>.json`, next to the transcript folder) through `src/claude-registry.mjs`, the same reader that `asb agent peers` uses. On Linux, a record counts only when its file is owned by the user, its pid is alive, and `/proc/<pid>/stat` has the recorded start time. On Windows, the engine has no process start time, so a record counts when its PID is alive and, for a record with a message pipe (`\\.\pipe\LOCAL\cc-msg-<id>`), when that pipe is in the list of pipe names. A record whose `hostSessionId` is the Desktop session ID matches first; otherwise its `sessionId` must be the CLI session ID or a prior one. The newest `statusUpdatedAt` wins. Only `status` `waiting` changes the row: it is a permission wait with Waiting and Pending, or, for `waitingFor` `input needed`, a question with question attention. A permission wait (any other `waitingFor`) with a `statusUpdatedAt` gets a 10 s grace period from that time: the row stays Working, and the status clock checks again when the grace ends. A question, a wait with no `statusUpdatedAt`, and an older wait show at once. This rule is the same for Claude Code CLI cards. Other statuses leave the transcript state unchanged, and each load starts again from the transcript, so no live wait stays. These fields were observed in Claude Code `2.1.299` and are not a published API, so this is best effort: a missing or unreadable registry changes nothing. ASB does not cache the registry; a watcher on the registry folder starts a new scan when a record changes. ASB Read and successful opens cannot clear these waits: the row stays Waiting with its `?` mark and the note "Waits for your permission.". Read only moves the card down to the read cards (see the approval read mark below).

ASB also reads open approvals from `logs/main.log` in each Claude app profile folder. Each line starts with the local time `YYYY-MM-DD hh:mm:ss [info] `. ASB reads only these line forms: `Emitted tool permission request <id> for <tool> in session local_<id>` opens a request. `Received permission response for <id>:`, `LocalSessions.respondToToolPermission: requestId=<id>,` (any decision), and `Permission request <id> for <tool> aborted` close it. A tool name has 1 to 128 characters of `A-Z`, `a-z`, `0-9`, `_`, `:`, `.`, and `-`. A request ID that the app writes two times is one request. Only `willQuit: handler fired` closes all requests: the app writes it only after a finished quit. A vetoed quit (`beforeQuit: vetoed by before-quit interceptor`) and a second app launch keep the requests open. The reader opens the file without a block on a FIFO (`O_NONBLOCK`, not on Windows) and reads only a regular file. It starts with the last 1 MiB, skips the first partial line, then reads from the byte after the last complete line. It keeps no log text: a partial last line is read again later. A file rotation, truncation, or addition of more than 4 MiB resets this window and the open requests. It keeps at most 256 open requests, with only the request ID, session ID, tool, and local time in memory. This is best effort, like the live registry: a request before the window or across a rotation can be missed. When the reader sees 3 or more response lines and no request line since the last reset, the app changed its request line: the Claude Desktop Code source shows the warning `ASB cannot read the approvals in the Claude app log. Update ASB.` (not over a source error). On Linux and Windows, an approval counts only while the chat has a live registry record, and not when that record's `startedAt` is 1 s or more after the request (the request is from an older process). `AskUserQuestion`, `ExitPlanMode`, and the other tools that the transcript shows as permission tools stay with the transcript rules. A turn end at least 2 s after the request cancels its effect: the newest of the final agent answer, the user message, and the task end. Other transcript activity, for example a parallel tool result, does not. A running linked child (a background subagent or a workflow run) that wrote nothing after the request can be the agent that waits, so then the turn end of the main chat does not cancel it: `childWaitAtMs` is the oldest last activity of the running children, and a request counts while `childWaitAtMs` is before the request time plus 1 s (log times have whole seconds). Other approvals get a 10 s grace period before Waiting, so a quick answer does not flash Waiting or play Needs input. The status clock checks again when the grace ends, then every 5 s while the approval shows Waiting, so the card clears at most 5 s after the answer. ASB does not watch the log.

OpenCode rows come from `loadOpenCodeDashboard` in `src/opencode-data.mjs`. Only `session_v2` root sessions are rows; subagent sessions add to the root's `subagentCount` through `embeddedSubagentCount`. The title is `title`, else `slug`. The execution claim (`time_suspended`) sets `lifecycleRunning` and the task start. The newest `session_message.time_created` of the root and its subagents is the activity time, else the claim time. So a claim with a message in the last six hours is Working, an older claim is Unknown, and no claim is Idle. `idle_outcome` `succeeded`, `failed`, and `interrupted` map to `task_complete`, `failed`, and `turn_aborted` at `time_idle`: Done, Failed, and the stop square with no attention. Native unread is `time_idle > (time_viewed or 0)`; no `time_idle` gives unknown read status. OpenCode keeps permission asks and questions only in memory, so OpenCode rows have no Waiting and no question attention. Rows have `providerLabel` `OpenCode`, `canOpen: false`, and no app link; `openSwitchboardThread` refuses them with `OpenCode cannot open one session from a link.` A missing database gives the `missing` status. A database without `session_v2`, `session_message`, or a needed column gives the warning `ASB reads OpenCode 2 sessions only. Update OpenCode.` and no rows.

Codex approval wait is unsupported. The local state schema has no current unanswered approval field, and the examined rollout schema has no reliable request/resolution pair. ASB does not read the app-server `waitingOnApproval` flag or infer approval from tool names, time, or policy fields. An open task keeps its current Working rule.

Linked Codex child questions add root attention. A current synchronous child question gives Waiting when no recent open member can continue. The root's own current synchronous question keeps its Waiting priority. Native read status still comes from the root. ASB Read acknowledges group attention without changing source questions or read marks.

Pending is separate from execution. Every visible unread dot, `!`, or `?` counts as Pending, including Unknown and archived rows when they have a visible dot. The one exception is a read required action (below). A stop square does not count as Pending. The row field `lastOutcome` of an Idle row is `stopped` (a user stop), `limit` (a usage limit stopped the task), `failed`, or empty, in this order. The row field `alert` is true when `unread` comes from a failed or limit-stopped task (`pendingSource` `observed-failure`, or `native-unread` with `lastOutcome` `failed` or `limit`). Such a row shows the `!` mark in place of the dot. Manual Unread and completed tasks keep the dot. Each engine computes `alert` for its rows, also for member rows, so it is not in the export. An older engine sends no `alert`, and clients then use false. An Idle row with `lastOutcome` `failed` or `limit` shows the state word `Stopped` until the next task starts, also after Read. Its `state` stays `idle`, so filters, sorting, and counts keep it in Idle. A Working chat cannot be unread. While a row is Working, `unread` is false and only question attention makes it Pending. Stored manual, native, completion, failure, and retained marks do not change; they show again when Working ends.

A required action (`actionRequired`: Waiting for a user action in the original app, such as a permission or plan approval) has its own read mark, like the question mark. `PendingTracker` keeps `actionSeen` and `actionAck` in each record. While a row has a required action, `actionSeen` is the newest wait time (`pendingToolAtMs`, or 1 for a member row, which has no wait time). When the row has no required action, both go to 0, so the mark lasts for one wait; an offline member row keeps them. `actionAttention` is true while `actionSeen` is more than `actionAck`. Read (the menu, the `?` corner, or an open when Persistent unread is off) sets `actionAck` to `actionSeen`. Only `actionAttention` makes the row Pending with `pendingSource` `user-action`. A read required action stays Waiting with its `?` mark and its note, but it is not Pending, so it moves down to the read cards. A new wait of the chat is unread again. The board and the phone offer Read while `actionAttention`, `unread`, or the drawer shows, else Unread. The drawer still refuses a required action. A record with `actionSeen` more than `actionAck` is kept like other marks.

Native Codex unread needs a creator identity and exact local host match. Missing or unmatched metadata gives Unknown read status. Identity fields stay outside the API. Local Claude chats have no reliable native unread source. ASB completion and failure marks require an observed Working-to-Idle change and a new terminal event. A task that goes Working, Waiting, then Idle keeps this observation and gets its completion or failure mark. Historical Idle rows do not gain either mark on first load. Sources with native read truth keep their native rule. Abort and cancellation add no new unread mark.

Codex and Claude terminal attention is held until the root and its linked child work end. The last group end sets the outcome. A successful end can add completion attention. A failed end can add a failed-task dot until Read, under the same observed-end, open acknowledgment, and Persistent unread rules. Codex failure uses a non-null terminal `task_complete.error`. Claude failure uses terminal `result.is_error`, a linked failed task notification that ends the group (a queued or transcript-only copy; a delivered notification starts a reply turn whose end sets the outcome), or a fresh remote session `failed` state. Linked child task notifications handle the statuses `completed`, `failed`, `cancelled`, and `aborted`. Ordinary tool errors, refusal text, child shutdown, and interruption do not establish task failure. A later completion or cancellation takes priority over an older failure. Child IDs, error bodies, prompts, and transcript paths stay outside the ASB view and state file.

A final root or group cancellation gives Idle with a passive stop square, including historical rows. Read does not clear this source outcome. New work or a later end removes the square. Existing question or unread attention remains visible in preference to the square.

Discard applies once to one task: the current Working task (a current-task arm), or the next task of an Idle row (a next-task arm, below). A verified successful root/group end clears the arm and suppresses its completion and matching native unread attention, including new Persistent unread retention. It does not clear manual Unread, question attention, or earlier failed-task attention. Failed ends keep their normal dot; cancellation keeps the stop square. Discard is not attention and does not change state or sort order. For a current-task arm, any observed Idle clears the arm. Waiting and Unknown keep both kinds of arm stored.

A next-task arm is for an Idle row whose next task the user knows will go well. Its record field is `discardNext` (0 or 1, set only with `discard` 1). `discard-result` with exactly `{"next": true}` sets it after the same forced scan, and it saves `discardEnd` (the newest task end that ASB saw) as a current-task arm does. The arm stays through Idle, Waiting, and Unknown scans until a task ends after it, that is, an end that is newer than `discardEnd`. When ASB sees the row Working, the arm becomes a current-task arm (`discardNext` is 0 and `discardStart` is the start of that task), and every rule of a current-task arm applies from then on. A task that starts and ends between two scans is the armed task: when the row is then Idle, or Working with a later task, an end that is newer than the arm is the end of the armed task. That end takes the discarded-success path (a success), keeps the normal failed dot (a failure), or keeps the stop square (a cancellation), and the arm clears in each case. Keep clears both kinds of arm. The row field `discardResult` is true for a Working row with an arm and for an Idle row with a next-task arm, and false for each other row. A source that gives no task end time keeps a next-task arm until the row is Working.

Existing lifecycle caches retain explicit member starts and terminal timestamps/kinds. The tracker uses these signals through private row Symbols, without exposing identities or transcript paths. A root ending while a linked child continues does not clear Discard. A child's first start uses explicit Codex creation or Claude launch/request evidence to cover late starts; missing evidence remains conservative. A proven new root task after its terminal event clears the old arm, including when Idle was missed between scans. A verified group end followed by new member work also clears it. A follow-up message in an armed Claude task does not clear the arm. Only a task that starts after an end later than the arm clears it. The displayed Working timer is not a task identity.

The first matched native unread episode for a discarded success is acknowledged locally, including a mark that arrives one scan later. A later observed Read-to-Unread cycle is preserved. The first late mark cannot be distinguished from a source-app manual Unread for the same completion; that first episode can be suppressed. A verified newer end clears the old native mask, and a failed end cannot use it. Local readers keep only the latest terminal data: if an armed task and a whole later task both end before the next observation, the later successful end can be discarded. Discard arming therefore scans current source state and, for the body `{}`, rejects an already-Idle row; Keep uses the normal cache. This prevents a late cached click from arming after both tasks ended. The body `{"next": true}` takes an Idle row on purpose: it arms the next task, so it covers no task that has ended. Remote `completed`/`review_ready` and `failed` terminal metadata are supported. Generic remote Idle without a terminal timestamp clears a current-task arm but does not suppress unknown/native attention. It does not clear a next-task arm. A remote end and new run wholly between observations cannot be proved from the cache metadata, so the old arm can remain. ASB makes no timing guess or remote call.

Mute (`PendingTracker.setMuted`, record field `muted`, row field `muted`) is per session. In `observe()`, a muted success is a scan where the row is muted, Idle, and not archived, its completion is newer than `seen`, and the end is not a failure (the task kind is not `failed`, and `failedAtMs` is not newer than `completionAtMs`). A muted success takes exactly the discarded-success path: `discardedAt` is the task end, `discardNative` is reset, the pending mark is acknowledged unless it is a failure, a retained `native-unread` or `observed-completion` source is cleared, no new pending mark is set, `seen` and `drawerSeen` move to the end, and the native unread of that task is suppressed, also when it arrives in a later scan. So completion attention, native attention, the Done sound, and the Done phone notification stay off. Failures, questions, permission waits, manual Unread, and the drawer rules do not change. Mute applies only to tasks that end after it is on; turning it on clears no dot. A later muted success clears an older success dot but not an older failure dot or manual Unread. The native menu has no Discard result for a muted row; a Discard armed before Mute stays.

The drawer holds unread rows that the user wants out of the way. Only a row with an unread dot can go in. A row with question attention, a required action in the original app, or a Working state cannot. In the dashboard, a drawer row has `drawer: true` and `unread: false`. It is not Pending and sorts with the read rows. The drawer has no time limit. The row leaves in these ways: take out; Read; Unread (through the API; the row panel of a drawer row offers **Mark read** and **Take out of drawer**); a successful open when Persistent unread is off; Working or a wait for the user; new attention; or no stored mark is left. With Persistent unread on, an open keeps the marks and the row stays in the drawer. The row leaves at once when its chat is Working (`state` `working`), has question attention, or has a required action with attention (`actionAttention`). `apply` keeps the stored marks: a Working row shows only Working and sorts with the Working rows, and when the task ends it shows its marks again (the new result, or the old mark after a stopped task). The tracker saves `drawerSeen`, the newest result time that ASB had seen for the chat when the row was put in the drawer. The row comes out as normal unread and Pending when ASB sees a result or failure time that is newer than `drawerSeen`. This includes a task that ASB did not see in its Working phase, for example a short task between two scans. A native unread that is raised again in the original app with no new result does not take the row out. When no stored mark is left, for example the chat was read in the original app and ASB has no other mark for it, the row leaves the drawer as a read chat. An unknown native read state for a time keeps the row in the drawer. A task that ends with Discard result on acknowledges the completion and native marks, as in 1.5.0, so a row whose only mark is of that kind is read. A manual Unread mark stays: the row is unread, or, after a short task between two scans (no Working scan), it stays in the drawer and is not Pending. The drawer changes nothing in Codex or Claude.

Read, Unread, pins, pin order, and the drawer belong only to ASB. A successful open acknowledges ASB attention when Persistent unread is off. It does not change execution or answer a question. Failed opens preserve attention. Persistent unread retains native, completion, and failure attention until Read, even after source Read or successful opens. It does not retain question attention: a resolved question leaves no mark, and a stored `user-question` retained source from an older version loads as none. It is on by default when the saved setting is absent or non-boolean. An explicit saved false stays off.

All clients share one tracker. Tracking uses the complete scanned list before filters. `pending.json` contains IDs, marker/acknowledgment values, manual marks, retained sources, pin order, Persistent unread, numeric Discard fields (`discard`, `discardNext`, `discardedAt`, `discardStart`, `discardEnd`, `discardNative`; `discardNext` is 0 or 1 and loads as 0 unless `discard` is 1, and a file from an older ASB has none), two numeric drawer fields (`drawer`, 0 or 1; `drawerSeen`, the newest result time that ASB had seen for the chat when the row was put in the drawer), and the Mute field `muted` (0 or 1; any other value loads as 0). Each record also has `lastSeen`, the start of the UTC day of the last scan that listed the row. It moves at most once a day, and at the same scan for all rows, so only the first scan of a UTC day rewrites the file for it. A record with no valid `lastSeen` (a file from an older ASB) gets the time of the load. `PendingTracker.observe` drops a record that was not seen for 30 days and that has no pin, no mark (`manual`, `retained`, an unacknowledged result or question or native unread, or an armed Discard), no drawer (`drawer`), and no Mute. With the group members (`machineGroup.memberIds()`, from the engine; `null` before the group loads, and an empty list when this machine is in no group), it also drops the records `m:<machineId>:...` of a machine that is not an active member (with their pins), but not the record of a row in the same scan. So after this machine leaves its group, or a member removes it, the records and pins of the old members go at the next scan. Version 1 remains compatible with old records, and files from `1.5.0` load as they are; missing/invalid numeric fields use zero. `PendingTracker` has a `dirty` flag. `set()` (for one field of a record) and each other code path that changes a record, the pin order, or Persistent unread set it, and `save()` writes only when it is set. So a scan that changes nothing serializes nothing and writes nothing. Read, Unread, the pin actions, the drawer, Mute, Discard, and Keep still save before their reply. A write is one `JSON.stringify` and the atomic rename (temporary file, then rename). A record is saved without the fields that hold their default (0 or the empty string), so a file of idle records is less than half of its old size. These five fields are always saved: `seen`, `working`, `pending`, `ack`, and `lastSeen`. ASB `1.10.0` rejects a record that lacks one of the first four, and `lastSeen` has no default. ASB `1.10.0` loads the new file with the same meaning, because its loader reads each missing field as that default (it ignores `discardNext`); `test/pending-tracker.test.mjs` has a copy of that loader rule and checks it. The file has owner-only access. It contains no chat titles, message bodies, call IDs, or transcript paths.

ASB shows the Pending state warning when it cannot use `pending.json` in full. The causes are an unreadable file, invalid JSON, a version other than 1, a bad shape, or a dropped record. ASB then moves the old file to `pending.json.bad` before the first save. An older `.bad` file is replaced. The same rule applies to `sources.json`: ASB moves it to `sources.json.bad` before the next source update or remove. The warning that ASB cannot save Pending state clears after the next good save.

### Sounds

The ASB server plays a sound on the PC for Done, Needs input, and Failed. It uses the same rules as the Android notifications (`notify/Events.kt`). `src/asb-sounds.mjs` checks the dashboard root rows after `PendingTracker.observe`. Done is completion attention, or native attention on an Idle row (a Codex chat with native read state) whose last outcome is not `failed` or `limit`. Failed is failure attention, or such native attention with the last outcome `failed` or `limit`. Needs input is question attention or an unread required action (`actionAttention`). An archived row has no events. Only a bit that is newly set since the previous update plays. A row that is missing from one update keeps its old bits. A usage limit does not hide a sound: a limit stop plays Failed once, when it happens, and the reset plays nothing. The first dashboard after start only sets the baseline. The first rows of a member machine also only set its baseline, because they can arrive in a later update; new events of a known member play. The rows of an offline member (Unknown, `<name> is not reachable.`) are left out of the update, so they keep their old bits and a result that was already known plays no sound again when the member is back. The Android app reads less often than the PC scans, so it also saves the result times (`completionAtMs`, `failedAtMs`) with the bits (`data.Mark`): a Done or Failed mark that stays set with a newer result time posts again. A saved time of 0 (a baseline of an older app version) gives no such event.

Each dashboard update plays one sound at most: Needs input, then Failed, then Done. The files are in `assets/sounds/`. They have the Android base names, but they are louder copies (a gain of 8 to 19 dB and a limiter at about −1 dB peak), because the original opencode files are too quiet on PC speakers. They are 16-bit PCM WAV files (32 to 38 KiB each), decoded from the earlier MP3 files with the same samples and the same gain: MP3 needs libsndfile 1.1 or later built with MPEG support, and WAV plays with every libsndfile and with ALSA alone. The Android app keeps its own MP3 files. The files are `opencode_staplebops_01.wav` (Done), `opencode_staplebops_02.wav` (Needs input), and `opencode_nope_03.wav` (Failed), with `opencode-LICENSE.txt`. On Linux, the player runs `pw-play --media-role Notification -P '{ application.name = "ASB" }' <file>` detached. The PipeWire node has `application.name` ASB, but GNOME lists the stream as `pw-cat` (an event sound), because GNOME takes the name from the client. Its volume follows the System Sounds volume. If `pw-play` is missing, ASB uses `paplay --client-name=ASB --property=media.role=event`. If `paplay` is also missing, ASB uses `aplay -q`. If no player exists, ASB plays no sound and writes one log line that names the three players and `asb doctor`. On Windows, ASB starts one hidden `powershell.exe -NoProfile -NonInteractive -Command <script>` at the first sound, because a new `powershell.exe` takes about 280 ms. The script is fixed text (`WINDOWS_PLAYER_ARGS`). It reads one full WAV path per line from stdin (as UTF-8 bytes, not the console code page) and plays each file with `Media.SoundPlayer` `PlaySync()`, in order. The path is only data on stdin, never in the arguments or the command text. A sound that comes while another plays waits for it. A bad line or a missing file skips that sound, and the loop goes on. The child has `windowsHide` and is not detached, because a detached child on Windows gets its own console. The child, its stdin, and the idle timer are `unref`'d, so the engine can exit at once; the closed pipe then ends the process. After 5 minutes with no sound, ASB closes the stdin and the process ends. The next sound starts a new process. It does the same after the process exits or its stdin fails. If `powershell.exe` is missing, ASB plays no sound and writes one log line: `ASB cannot play sounds: Windows PowerShell was not found.`. Another failure writes one log line too (`ASB cannot play a sound: ...`). The dashboard path never waits for the player.

The sounds run in the one ASB server process: the engine or `npm start`. The engine (`serviceMode`) plays a sound only while a native window has a UI client in the engine (`server.boardOpen()`): it sent a UI report (`POST /api/agent/ui`) in the last 45 s, or its event stream `/api/events?ui=<clientId>` is open (then the 45 s count from the end of the stream). A window that closes sends a last report that removes it (`report_ui_closed`, which waits at most 0.75 s), so the sounds stop with the last window; after a window crash they can continue for up to 45 s. The watcher moves its baseline also while it is silent, so a window that opens later plays only new events. The `npm start` web view sends no UI reports and keeps its sounds. `createSwitchboardServer` plays sounds only when the caller passes `playSound`, so tests and other imports stay silent. There is no sound setting. The phone keeps its own sounds.

## Native view contract

- The toolbar stays in one row at every width and shows only icons. The ⋮ main menu (`Gtk.MenuButton`, accessible label `Main menu`) and native Close stay fixed at the right. The disc stays at the left at widths of at least 320px; below 320px, the disc and the search button are hidden. Drawer and Filters pills stay pinned with no text labels; they keep their accessible labels and tooltips. A horizontal native scroller holds filter chips; active chips keep their text. Pointer scrolling and native keyboard focus make hidden controls reachable. At 680px or more, Refresh is an icon before ⋮; below 680px it is in Preferences. The search field is never in the toolbar: at every width it is an inline row under the toolbar (`search_row`, a `Gtk.Revealer` that slides down in 150 ms, with no motion when animations are off), not a popover. The search button (`system-search-symbolic`), typing on the board, and Sessions > Find a session or folder open it. It stays while it has a query or the focus; with an empty query, it hides when it loses the focus. One click on a card opens the session while you search. The main view shows no numeric counters or summary row. Native move, resize, outer border, and row layout stay unchanged.
- Each active filter value has one removable toolbar chip. The chip shows Pending, the chosen states, the chosen profiles, or Include archived. × resets only that value. Clearing a focused chip moves focus to another visible chip or the Filters pill. Search prefixes still override profile selection. Persistent unread has no chip.
- Compact is the default with rows of a nominal 22 pixels. Comfortable uses rows of a nominal 68 pixels with the folder first and a two-line title. These are minimum heights; a font with higher lines makes rows higher.
- The card title label shows at most 85 characters (`card_title()`, `CARD_TITLE_CHARS`): a longer title shows its first 84 characters and `…`, cut on whole characters. The tooltip, the accessible name, and the row panel show the full title.
- Comfortable uses C Restore on hover: only Idle and Waiting rows without visible ASB unread/question attention or Pending use quiet title/folder/state/age text. Pinned rows use the same rule; provider marks and controls keep their strength. Hover/focus restores normal text. Source read metadata cannot override ASB acknowledgment. Quiet colors derive from the active palette and keep 4.5:1 contrast on background and highlight. Compact is unchanged.
- View-only rows (`surface` `cli` or `remote`) use option D, "Machine in the meta line", with the Grey tone. Comfortable has a right part in the bottom line: the where glyph (`asb-terminal-symbolic` or `asb-machine-symbolic`, 11 px), the where label (`CLI ·` or `<machineName> ·`, at most 12 characters, ellipsized first), then the age. Compact has the label `· CLI` or `· <machineName>` (at most 10 characters) after the state and no glyph. Other rows hide both. A view-only row with no unread, question, or Pending attention and no usage limit gets `asb-view-only-quiet` (quiet title, folder, age, and where text); when it is not Working it also gets `asb-view-only-rest` (quiet state). Hover, focus, the limit look, and the drawer peek bring the normal colors back. The tooltip flags are `CLI session. Runs in a terminal. ASB does not open CLI sessions.`, `On <machineName>. Open it on <machineName>.`, or `On <machineName> · CLI session. Open it on <machineName>.` The accessible label adds ` On <machineName>.`, ` CLI session.`, and ` View only.` The row panel button says `Runs in a terminal` or `On <machineName>` and is insensitive. A remote row has no Discard offer on the card or in the row panel. The web view shows `state · CLI · age` or `state · <machineName> · age` and the same reason in the row title.
- Comfortable uses the approved C Corner pair: a top-right Read dot beside Pin/Unpin. Pin appears on row hover or keyboard focus; a pinned control stays visible. Both use the existing ASB state APIs and stay separate from opening the chat. Saved pin order, drag reorder, and the row panel remain available.
- The Comfortable card corner is a row of four: copy, drawer, read, pin. The drawer button shows only while the pointer is on the card, while the card has visible keyboard focus, or while the button itself has visible keyboard focus. A row that looks unread gets the put-in button. A row in the drawer gets a filled glyph in the accent color that takes it out. The copy button shows with the same rule as the drawer button, for every row state. It copies the chosen kind from `card_copy(row, kind)`: Relative is `copy_values(row)[0]`, the same text as the row panel's **Copy relative reference** row; Absolute is `copy_values(row)[2]`, the same text as the row panel's **Copy absolute reference** row. One board-wide setting, optional `copyKind` in schema `1` `layout.json` (value `absolute`; absent means Relative), holds the kind for all local cards. `change_copy_kind` saves it before it applies; a failed save keeps the old kind and shows the error on the board. View, width, and card limit saves keep it. The wheel on the copy button switches the kind only after a 250 ms rest from enter, or while the tag shows (`copy_rest_ready`, `copy_wheel_captured`). Before that rest the wheel scrolls the column. One wheel notch is one switch (`copy_wheel_step` with an injected clock); a trackpad burst is one switch. GTK sends no enter or leave when a card moves under a still pointer, so a glide or a repack keeps the tag and the wheel (`clear_hover(copy_tag=False)`), and after the layout `start_motion` hides the tag only when the pointer is no longer on the button (`pointer_on`). Alt+Up and Alt+Down on the focused copy button switch the kind; Enter and Space copy. While the pointer rests on the button or the button has visible keyboard focus, a `Gtk.Revealer` tag slides out to the left over the folder line. It has the complete word **Relative** or **Absolute** in a `Gtk.Stack` (200 ms slide, exponential ease-out feel), a kind glyph (`asb-link-symbolic` or `asb-globe-symbolic` on the copy button while the tag shows), and two position dots. Click copies the chosen kind. For 1.6 s the footer `Gtk.Stack` (crossfade, 100 ms) shows its copied page: `object-select-symbolic` and **Relative session copied** or **Absolute session copied** (`COPY_DONE_TEXT`). The line page keeps the limit clock, state, and age. The same text is the accessible description. The tooltip does not change, so the text does not show twice. A remote row (`remote` is true) copies only Absolute. Its tag always says **Absolute**, the wheel does not move the kind, and its tooltip is **Copy absolute reference.** plus `copy_relative_reason(row)` when the absolute value exists. With no safe value for the chosen kind, the button stays in its place with no action, the accessible state DISABLED, and the reason of that reference as its tooltip. A copy error names the kind (`Cannot copy the relative reference: <error>` or `Cannot copy the absolute reference: <error>`), and a later good copy clears either text. The action signature of the card holds the value, the reason, the kind, and the tag state, so the button updates when a remote row gets or loses its absolute reference. The row panel keeps both copy rows. After a copy from a row, its caption shows the done text for 1.6 s. It is local: it sends no request, has no pending state, and a click never opens the row. A drawer row outside the drawer view hides its Read target until the pointer or visible focus is on the row. The title keeps its width: the controls lie over the end of the first title line, with a 16px fade to the window background, not to the hover color. On a highlighted row this gives a slightly darker pocket behind the buttons. The fade is on whenever the column is 190px or wider, because the copy button shows there. A column narrower than 190px has no copy or drawer button. Compact has no card buttons. The **Put in drawer** and **Take out of drawer** rows of the row panel put a row that looks unread in the drawer and take a drawer row out, in both views.
- In both views, an unread row looks read while the pointer is on it: the unread dot is not shown and the state label has the read tone. It looks unread again when the pointer leaves. In Comfortable, the Read slot shows its check cue while the pointer is on the card. A `?`, the stop square, and the Discard ring do not change on hover. The read look (no dot, and the check cue in Comfortable) also shows while the row has visible keyboard focus or while one of its controls has visible keyboard focus. Focus that comes from a mouse click does not change the row: its dot stays. In Compact, focus never hides the dot.
