For the pointer, the ASB CSS uses the `asb-hovered` and `asb-unhovered` classes, not `:hover` on a card: a motion controller on each card sets exactly one of them (`row_hover()`, on `notify::contains-pointer`). Buttons keep `:hover`. GTK sends no enter or leave when cards move under a still pointer, so after the layout of a repack or a reorder, `sync_row_hover()` reads the pointer position and window pick and gives `asb-hovered` only to the card under the pointer (`hovered_card`); at most one card has it.
- Outside the drawer view, a drawer row looks read: no dot, not Pending, sorted with the read rows. In the drawer view it looks unread as normal. While the pointer is on the Drawer pill and the drawer view is off, the drawer rows show their unread look in place (the peek). The peek changes CSS only on the visible drawer rows, with no list render. The row tooltip flags can be Pinned, Archived, In the drawer, and No direct link (for a view-only row, the where flags below). A drawer row keeps its normal unread note; the note text does not name the drawer. A Working drawer row has the flag and no note. The accessible label of a drawer row ends with "In the drawer. Still unread."
- Comfortable actions use 24-pixel circular targets in an unmeasured overlay. Folder and title reserve 62 pixels at the right, including the content inset. State and time remain in the footer. Compact and the outer frame keep their existing layout.
- Comfortable paints one shared hover highlight behind cards and actions, across columns. One native animation uses 200 ms for movement and 100 ms for arrival/leave, aligned with the approved command-palette motion reference; rapid targets start from the current painted bounds. GNOME colors stay dynamic; custom colors use the existing highlight mix. GNOME animations off gives immediate feedback. Repacking, filters, resize, scroll, the start of a card motion, unmap, and close clear it. Scroll clears the shared paint once. Pointer motion cannot restart it while the wheel spring is active. After scroll, stationary or generated motion cannot restart it; real pointer movement restores the glide. Native row hover and keyboard focus keep their feedback. Only the card under the highlight has the `asb-hover-paint` class, so a hover change restyles that card only. Hover makes no source reads and adds no permanent frame loop. Compact and keyboard focus keep their existing feedback.
- One indicator serves both views with priority `?`, dot, Discard ring, stop. A current permission wait or question attention shows the accent `?`. Unread shows the 7-pixel dot. Armed Working, or Idle with a next-task arm, uses a hollow ring with a 9-pixel outer size (6-pixel space and 1.5-pixel border), using GNOME foreground or custom text. It does not count as Pending. An Idle stopped outcome with no attention shows a 7-pixel muted square with a 1-pixel radius. The stop mark and a read permission-wait mark are passive and have no Read action or check cue. An unread permission wait (`actionAttention`) has the Read action in its `?` corner. Tooltips and accessible labels distinguish current wait, stopped, failed, completed, and armed Discard. They do not report stored unread while `unread` is false, except for a drawer row that is not Working. The indicator updates in place and keeps row geometry and pin controls.
- Comfortable uses the existing 24-pixel Read slot for Discard while Working without question attention. Before arming, its dashed muted ring appears on row hover or keyboard focus. Armed, the solid ring stays visible and offers Keep result. Discard actions have no Read check cue or confirmation and cannot open the row. Questions retain the Read action or passive permission-wait behavior. Reading a Working question returns directly to the Discard ring without a check; Idle/Waiting Read confirmation stays unchanged. New Working without a question clears old Read feedback. Compact shows only the passive armed ring and keeps the timer. The row panel of a Working row has the **Discard result** or **Keep result** row as the keyboard path. No card text is dimmed by Discard. A muted row has no Discard ring, menu entry, or switch; a Discard armed before Mute stays. The same slot offers Discard for the next task on an Idle row with no indicator (no `?`, no dot, no stop square), not muted and not remote. The dashed ring shows on row hover or keyboard focus, and activation sends `discard-result` with `{"next": true}` (a Working row still sends `{}`). While the check of a Read is on show, the slot keeps the check, and the offer comes back when the check ends. An armed Idle row shows the solid ring and offers Keep result. Compact shows the passive ring. The menu and the row panel of an Idle row with no mark have **Discard next result**, and those of an armed Idle row have **Keep next result**, also when a mark takes the slot. The control tooltips on an Idle row are `Discard next result: go to read Idle when the next task ends` and `Keep result: show the dot when the next task ends`, the tooltip note is `Discard is on for the next task.`, and the accessible label adds ` Discard is on for the next task.` Working texts do not change. The phone keeps Discard for Working rows only and shows nothing for an Idle arm.
- A muted row shows a 12px `asb-muted-symbolic` icon (`asb-mute-glyph`, `dim-label`) right after the folder name in Comfortable and after the state in Compact. The folder ellipsizes first, so the icon stays visible in a narrow column. The icon has the color of the folder name: `dim-label`, the quiet caption color of a read Idle row or a view-only row, or the limited color of a limited row. The icon is a one-fill ASB SVG, loaded as a file icon (`Gio.FileIcon`), because GTK 4.18 does not recolor symbolic icons from the icon search path. It has no filter: the old `filter: opacity(.35)` gave it about 1.9:1 on a black board, so a muted card looked unchanged. The menu offers **Mute** or **Unmute** after the drawer entries, and the row panel has the same **Mute** or **Unmute** row, right after the drawer row. **Mute** and **Unmute** close the panel, which covers the card, so the card shows or hides the icon at once; a failed request shows its error in the card tooltip. The tooltip flag is **Muted** and the accessible name ends with "Muted."
- Both views use the approved A Unfolded card tooltip: 14px provider icon plus full muted path, full 13px wrapped title, then an 11px state/time and app/source footer. Home uses `~` only at a directory boundary. A native FlowBox wraps the app block onto its own right-aligned line. The Soft profile dot follows the row's existing rule. One optional note uses the actual indicator and one fixed sentence; short flags and the current row error share its hairline block. Read historical failures have no failed-unread note. All text uses plain labels, WORD_CHAR wrapping, and an approximate 36-character width, with no title/path truncation or fixed height. The title is the limited title of at most 300 characters from the server. The GTK tooltip surface stays native.
- Each tooltip query computes a fresh cheap model from the cached row and current clock/error, with no source read. The row lazily retains one content widget and complete render key; unchanged models reuse it, and every query calls GTK `set_custom`. Model, provider/state tint, and custom-theme changes rebuild content. Palette changes clear all row tooltip caches, including hidden rows. Each row owns one query handler; release/close clears its content/key and disconnects it separately from the six input controllers. Rows have no static tooltip-text property. Visible tooltips omit state reason and long source/read metadata. The accessible description keeps the full original path, title, state/time, note, flags, error, state reason, and source name/ID; row names and control tooltips stay unchanged.
- A successful Read clears only ASB attention and shows a check for 1.6 seconds. It works with either Persistent unread setting. Failure keeps the dot. Row identity, view, feedback generation, and current attention guard against stale results or new unread marks. Read does not change execution, original-app read state, or source question resolution.
- A successful completed Read records a per-row monotonic time. Discard/Keep activation (also the Idle actions) is ignored for 0.5 seconds after it, before busy state or a request is created. The ring/offer is visible immediately. Menus, server behavior, and Idle/Waiting Read confirmation stay unchanged; row release clears the stored time.
- While a row action is pending, its controls keep pointer sensitivity but lose their action bindings. They report `BUSY` and `DISABLED` to accessibility APIs. The confirmation has no Read action binding. Pointer and keyboard activation cannot open the row through these controls: a row with a card action in flight ignores activation. Before this correction, in 1.5.0 and earlier, a real pointer click on a card button also opened the session in its original app.
- The board shows only the first `cardLimit` cards: `limit_rows()` runs after `filtered_rows()` (all filters, search, and sort) and puts one synthetic More row (`asb:more`) in place of the rest. The More card uses the normal card widget, stays in the row cache when it is not on the board, cannot open, and sends no request. Its activation sets a show-all flag that is not saved, and the **All cards** filter chip clears it.
- Rows flow down each column, then across. The list scrolls horizontally. The viewport height and the height of the highest visible row after layout set row capacity, so a scrollbar that is not an overlay and a font with higher lines do not cut the last row. Shared width sets visible columns. An order-only refresh with the same IDs and packing moves only changed rows inside existing columns, without explicit menu or scroll teardown. Each render moves only the cards outside the longest run of each column that already has the target order (`column_moves()`). `filtered_rows()` keeps the first row of each ID: one card twice in a column makes GTK 4 list it twice in the internal list of the column (`get_row_at_index`) with no warning, and GTK lays out that list, so the extra entry shows as an empty slot and later removals fail with "Tried to remove non-child". The cards of a column come from its widget children, and a card that still has a parent leaves it before it is inserted. Before the moves and after them, `mend_columns()` compares each column with its internal list (and after the moves with its target cards); when one differs, it replaces all columns with new `SessionColumn` widgets that hold the target cards. The old columns stay in `retired_columns` with no card, because a card can keep a pointer into the internal list of its old column until it is inserted again. Card tooltips are set only when their text changes, and a new Comfortable card gets them before it enters the board, so GTK makes no tooltip query for them. The card buttons have `clicked` handlers that activate the window actions, and no action names, because GTK made card moves slower over time with action names on many buttons. Profile dots use one shared display style provider with one class for each color. Widget/action identity and focus are retained when possible. When a moved row had keyboard focus, the window sets the focus on it again. Columns keep the shared width when the list has fewer columns than the window can show, for example search results with long titles. Filter round trips reuse detached column containers. The full dashboard count and current row capacity bound this cache; source removal releases excess containers.
- Card motion applies to both views. When data or filters change the order or the set of visible cards, each card that stays visible glides from its old place to its new place, and each new card fades in. A card that leaves disappears at once. The causes are a row that goes from unread to read, a new order from a refresh, a pin move, search text, the pills, the Preferences filters, and the archive setting. List changes from the drawer use this same motion. One shared animation of about 350 ms with a critically damped spring feel drives all cards. Each glide sets the spring epsilon from the longest card move D: the largest value from .0001 to .001 for which the jump at the spring end, D × epsilon × (1 - ln epsilon), is at most half a pixel (`glide_epsilon`), so a short move or a fade ends after about 350 ms and a move longer than about 490 px keeps the 460 ms spring. A new change during the animation starts from the current painted places. There is no motion at the first render, when the window is not mapped, when GNOME animations are off, or when the packing dimensions change (window size, column width, view). The motion is paint only: allocation, input, focus, and accessibility are at the final layout at all times. So a capture gesture on the list keeps the point of a primary press (`card_pointer`), and during a glide `open_row` opens a card from the pointer only when `painted_places()` draws that card under the point; else the click does nothing. Enter, the number keys, and the More card open as before. The card CSS limits transitions to `background-color` and `color`, so a theme rule that animates all properties (for example `transition: all` on `row.activatable`) does not animate the card height, and a view change lays out the board once. Motion frames invalidate a column only when its motion bounds intersect the viewport. The final clear also clears cached paint outside the viewport. The shared hover highlight is cleared when a motion starts. The motion adds no permanent frame loop. The drawer adds no timer and no frame loop.
- Wheel scrolling uses one critically damped Libadwaita spring toward the target, with a continuous velocity. The damping ratio is 1. The spring settles in about 350 ms with no overshoot. The step per click is unchanged. Same-direction input adds to a clamped target; reversal starts from the visible position. Trackpad input stays direct and cancels wheel motion. GNOME animations off gives immediate feedback. Bounds, layout/filter/view changes, external scroll changes, unmap, and close cancel old targets. Unchanged refresh preserves active scroll; frames make no source reads and add no permanent loop.
- ASB pins come first in saved order. Other rows follow Pending, Working, Idle, then Unknown. A read Waiting row is not Pending and sorts with the Idle rows (`bucket` in `filtered_rows`, and `filteredRows` in the Android `Rules.kt`). A Working row sorts by the start of its current task (`workingSinceMs`), not by its last activity, so it does not move while it works. A Working row with no known start sorts after the Working rows that have one, by ID. In the Pending group, Working rows (a question on a Working chat) come first, by task start, and the other Pending rows follow by newest activity. A row moves when its state or group changes or a new task starts. Other rows sort by newest activity, with ID as the tie-break. A drawer row is not Pending and sorts with the read rows.
- The Filters popover is the one place for filters. It has `autohide=False`, so it does not take the first click outside it: a capture `Gtk.GestureClick` on the window closes it on a press outside the popover and the pill, and the same press goes on to the card or control under the pointer. It also closes when the keyboard focus leaves it, when the window stops being active, unmaps, or closes, and on Escape or a pill click. Only Escape and the pill click return the focus to the pill (`set_focus_on_click(False)` on the pill). The chips change when it closes. Pending, states, profiles, and Include archived combine with AND between groups and OR inside a group. An empty states set shows all states. Working plus Pending shows Working or rows with Pending or visible ASB unread/question attention. Search prefixes and archive checks still restrict the union; prefixes temporarily take priority over profile selection. `oc:` and `opencode:` select OpenCode. The optional `cardLimit` in schema `1` `layout.json` (50, 100, 200, 500, or 0 for All; default 100, not written when 100) is the **Cards on the board** choice in Preferences > Sessions. It saves before applying, a failed save selects the old choice again, and all other layout saves keep it. The optional `copyKind` in the same file (value `absolute`; absent means Relative, not written when Relative) is the card copy kind. The copy button wheel and Alt+Up/Alt+Down save it; a failed save keeps the old kind and shows the error. `read_layout` still accepts optional strict boolean `showAppPills`; `write_layout` no longer writes it. Old layout files with `showAppPills` load with no error.
- Drawer follows Working. It has the existing drawer glyph and a plain occupancy dot, and no text label at any width. The dot is visible when the archive setting permits at least one drawer row, with no numeral or count bubble. Search, app, and state filters do not change it. The selected pill has an accent background and an inverted dot. The tooltip keeps its existing drawer/peek help. Accessible text says that the drawer is empty or has sessions. The list shows only drawer rows when selected. Drawer combines with app, search, state, and archive filters. It does not combine with Pending: turning one on turns the other off. The empty drawer view keeps its existing message.
- At start, no widget has the keyboard focus, so no text cursor blinks and repaints the window. Tab follows the native visible toolbar controls. Typing a printable key opens the search row with the typed text. Typing from a row starts search with no fixed delay. The × in the field and Escape (`reset_view`) clear the query, the prefix, and all filters (Pending, the states, the profiles, Include archived, All cards, and the Drawer view) and hide the search row. Backspace that empties the query keeps the filters. An open popover (Filters, row panel, main menu) takes Escape first and closes only itself. With no query and no filter, Escape does nothing. Queued row focus restoration stops if focus has changed. The row panel (approved option B, Action list) has a one-line title, **Open in <app>**, **Show messages** (`asb-chat-symbolic`; sensitive for every row, also a view-only or remote row; it closes the panel and opens the Messages window with the thread of that session), the permission note for an `actionRequired` row, one flat row per action that `row_menu_actions()` returns (one stable button per slot from `row_panel_controls()`, so the focus stays on the slot when its label changes), **Copy relative reference**, and **Copy absolute reference**. `copy_values(row)` gives both values and their reasons. A value counts only when it is 1 to 384 printable ASCII characters with no space; Python does not parse the reference grammar. The relative value is `sessionReference` from a new engine (a row with `reference` or `referenceUnavailable`), else `session_reference(row)`. The absolute value is `reference`. Else each row shows the text for its `referenceUnavailable` code (`unknown-source` has a member variant with the `machineName`), the old-engine text when the row has neither key, or a fallback text. An unavailable row stays focusable, has the accessible state DISABLED, and copies nothing. A right-click opens it when the right button is released (`released` of the button-3 `Gtk.GestureClick`). The row panel, main menu, and Usage machine filter use `non_autohide_popover` with `autohide=False`, as Filters does: there is no X11 grab to keep ASB active or take another app's keys. A capture press outside closes the popover and acts on the card or control under the pointer; a board press in the place of a panel that has not mapped is claimed. They also close when keyboard focus leaves, when the window becomes inactive, unmaps, or closes, and on Escape. Escape returns focus to the card or menu button. `non_autohide_choice` applies the same close ways to the internal popovers of the Messages and Usage period drop-downs, the three Preferences drop-downs, and the profile dialog App and Remote Claude sessions combo rows. It keeps the native widgets, labels, selection, and accessible names. `close_popovers_when_inactive` remains the delayed close for other native popovers; a no-grab popover closes directly on `notify::is-active`. It has mouse and keyboard access. While an action for the row runs, its controls stay sensitive and busy (`asb-action-pending`, BUSY and DISABLED) and do nothing, so that GTK does not move the pointer target to the board and close the panel. It closes when the pointer leaves it, but not when the pointer moves between its controls. A keyboard-opened panel stays open while the pointer is outside until the pointer enters and leaves it. Pin movement has a keyboard alternative. The **Move pin earlier** and **Move pin later** rows of the row panel move the pin before or after the adjacent visible pinned row, with search and filters applied. They send no request at the first or last visible pin.
- Number keys: `number_key_rows()` takes the visible rows in board order and their bounds in the scroll viewport, and returns the first nine IDs that can open and are fully inside it (0.5px tolerance; zero sizes do not count). The window computes it again after each render, after each scroll allocation (one idle), and 100 ms after the scroll position stops changing. Each numbered row gets the accessible key shortcut `Control+N`; other rows have none. The window key controller (capture phase) starts a 400 ms hold on a press of Control with no modifier already held and returns false, so Control is not consumed. Key repeat and a hold that a popup interrupted already carry Control, so they start nothing; a release that a popup took cannot block the next hold. Ctrl+1 to Ctrl+9 and keypad digits with Control only compute the numbers from the current geometry and open the card through `open_row()`, whose busy guard covers key repeat. Another key with Control cancels the hold and hides the numbers, then follows the usual path. Release of Control, `notify::is-active` false, unmap, the row panel or any other popover of the window, a scroll event, and a press on the scrollbar hide them; programmatic scroll restores do not. A `map`/`unmap` emission hook keeps the set of mapped popovers of the window, so a popover blocks the keys also when it does not take the focus; it adds no timer or frame scan, and close removes it. Each render takes the hook off and puts it back after, because card moves map and unmap many widgets; no popover opens inside a render. Close removes the hold and queued updates. The `asb-number-shown` class on each numbered keycap shows it. A keycap that gets or loses its number while the keycaps show gets or loses the class; showing them rebuilds nothing.
- The ⋮ main menu has a `Gio.Menu` model: a first section with **Messages** (`app.messages`, Ctrl+M) and **Usage** (`app.usage`, Ctrl+U), and a second section with **Preferences** (`win.preferences`, Ctrl+,). GTK draws the separator and shows each accelerator. Ctrl+M and Ctrl+U work in every ASB window (Board, Messages, Usage, Preferences); Ctrl+Shift+U stays free for GTK Unicode input. Ctrl+W closes only Messages or Usage; on the Board it does nothing. Escape closes Usage (a `Gtk.ShortcutController`); an open menu or popover in it takes Escape first. In Messages, Escape closes an open thread.
- The main menu item **Preferences** opens one separate, nonmodal native **Preferences** window. Repeated selection raises the same window. Closing it hides it and keeps the main window running; reopening keeps the selected section and form edits. Main-window shutdown destroys it. The approved Option B uses left navigation at wide widths and tabs at narrow widths for **Sessions**, **Appearance**, and **Profiles**. Sessions holds Cards on the board, Persistent unread, search, and narrow-window Refresh. The Filters popover is the one place for filters. The search action hides Preferences, keeps its form, presents the main window, and focuses search. Appearance holds view, shared width/reset, and the five-color theme editor.
- **Messages** and **Usage** are separate native GTK windows (`MessagesWindow` 968×760 and `UsageWindow` 948×760 at first open, on the shared base `ActivityWindow`). Each has one instance and is nonmodal: opening it again presents the same window, and closing it does not close ASB. They have no webview: the round table, the share bars, and the charts are custom `Gtk.Widget` subclasses (`TableCanvas`, `StatusShare`, `UsageBar`, `UsageRound`, `UsageChart`) that draw in `do_snapshot` with `Gsk` paths, which need GTK 4.14 (`Gsk.PathBuilder`); with GTK 4.12, both windows show `Charts need GTK 4.14 or later.` under the toolbar. The windows have no header bar. One toolbar row in a `Gtk.WindowHandle` is the top bar and the drag area: the 16 px ASB mark, the period menu (a flat `Gtk.DropDown`: Messages `24 hours` (default), `7 days`, `30 days`; Usage also `90 days` and `All time`, default `All time`), a start area, a space, an end area, and `Gtk.WindowControls` with `:close` only. Both windows draw every surface in the Board background. `activity_palette` gives `win`, `rule` (13% text, the lines between rows), `track` (17%, the empty part of a bar), `popup` (the border of the chart tooltip, as the Board popovers), `warn` (`#e0b45a`), and `bad` (`#f5a090`). With custom colors, the window has the `asb-custom` class: a checked linked button is the accent. The Android screens take the same colors from `activityColorsOf`. While a window shows, it reads its loopback route again after `dashboard` events of `/api/events` (at most once a second) and every 15 s; the Usage window reads every 2 s while the scan runs or the prices load. Messages (`MessagesWindow`, minimum 480 × 420) opens on the dashboard: the round table (`RoundTable`, a `Gtk.Overlay` over `TableCanvas`) and the ledger (`messages_view(reply, selected)`: the totals, the statuses, `Between apps`, `Between machines`, and `Recent`, the 6 newest). The table seats the 12 most recent sessions (`MESSAGE_SEATS`), the selected ones first, and draws chords between seated sessions. Pins and chords have focusable `MessageProxy` widgets with accessible names (`docs, Claude Code, alpha: 10 sent, 8 received`; `docs and release-plan: 7 messages`). The center (or, under a table smaller than 480 px in the narrow layout, the reader) shows the hovered, focused, or open pair or session, else the newest message of the reply with its `text` in two lines (`No text` in dim text when it is `null`); it makes no thread read. A thread opens only from a click on a chord, a pin, or a shown seat label (`RoundTable.hit`), Enter or Space on a pin or chord proxy, a Recent row (the pair thread, with that message outlined for 1.2 s), or **Show messages** (`show_session(seat, title)`; before the first reply the window keeps it and opens it after the reply). `open_thread` reads `/api/activity/thread` with the default priority, as the window replies, and shows `Latest 60 of N`, day lines, and one block per message: the sender (`message_end_title`: the `fromLabel` or `toLabel` of an `unknown:` end, else the seat title or `Other sessions`), the time, the status when it is not Accepted (`message_status_label`: the reason of a not-sent message, for example `Machine offline`), and the text (wrapped at words or characters, selectable, `width_chars=20`; `No text` in dim text). A block uses `ThreadBlockLayout`, a `Gtk.BoxLayout` that gives its natural width for any height: a start or end aligned block of a wrapped label is otherwise measured by a slow width-for-height search. Escape, **Back to totals**, or a click on an empty part of the table closes the thread. A light flies only for a message that is not in the last reply and is at most 120 s old (`message_arrivals`), at most `MESSAGE_FLIGHT_LIMIT` (6) at a time, never for the first reply, with animations off, or on GTK 4.12. At rest the window has no tick callback; `RoundTable.tick` runs only while a seat moves or a light flies. A reply with the same `message_reply_key` (the period, the day of `nowMs`, the machine IDs, names, and states, the seats, the messages, and `truncated`) changes no widget and queues no draw. Closing hides the window and also closes the thread and stops the table motion (`close_to_hidden`); `finish` stops them too. Messages ignores the `buckets` of the messages route. Usage shows cost or tokens by machine, app, provider, and model. `Cost` and `Tokens` are in the start area. The machine filter (a flat `Gtk.MenuButton`, tooltip `Machines`, accessible name `Machine filter`, shown only with 2 or more machines) is in the end area, and its popover holds the machine chips. While a scan runs, a line under the toolbar shows `usage_scan_text` (`Reading usage: 120 of 2,707 files`). The price line (`usage_prices_text`) is the tooltip of the ring chart. Machines, Apps, and Providers are `Gtk.Expander` folds: Machines open, Apps and Providers folded. The unpriced note (`usage_unpriced_note`) is one line, `1 unpriced model: 12K tokens not in the cost.`, and its tooltip names the models. A machine chip shows `Offline` only for an offline machine; its tooltip is the last report or the state, and its accessible label is `<name>, <state>`. Usage has two breakpoints, `narrow` (max-width 899 px) and `small` (639 px), read in `notify::current-breakpoint` (Libadwaita applies only the last one that matches): the overview rows are 34 px with a one-line machine state (ellipsized, the full text in its tooltip); the Totals figures are a homogeneous `Gtk.FlowBox` (2 to 5 to a line); narrow stacks the overview with a 240 px ring (`UsageRound.set_size`; the arcs of `usage_round_arcs` keep their 320 px frame, and the drawing and `arc_at` scale) and keeps the two type blocks side by side; small stacks them. Its apps are Codex, Claude Code, OpenCode, Antigravity, Grok, Pi, and Other, in this order; the Android Messages screen keeps Codex, Claude Code, OpenCode, and Other. The Providers section has one row for each `maker` (bar `#8c929a`; in Cost, a maker with no priced record shows `Unpriced`; `Other` is last). `All time` has one point for each local month (`Monthly cost` or `Monthly tokens`, the tooltip title `October 2026`), and its empty text is `No usage yet.` The chart labels (`usage_axis_labels`) go on every n-th point, counted from the last point; a label shows only when it ends at least 4 px before the label to its right, and the last point always has its label. Month labels are `This month` on the last point, `Jul 2026` on the leftmost label and where the year changes, and `Aug` on the others. In the wide layout, the round chart is at the top of the overview. A reply that changes only `nowMs` does not draw the window again; the Models rows keep their widgets (by model and app) and change their texts and order in place; the Machines, Apps, and Providers rows keep their widgets by id (`asb_machine`, `asb_app`, `asb_maker`), change in place, and move with `reorder_child_after`, and the five Totals figures and the two type blocks are made once; a change of `small` or `narrow` makes the rows again; **By app** and **By machine** change only the chart. `UsageChart` draws layered monotone curves (ports of T3 Code's `monotoneTangents` and `smoothCurve`), one point per bucket at the bucket center, and builds its `Gsk` paths again only when the data, the width, or the animation progress changes, so a hover draws only the hover line and the tooltip over the kept paths. `UsageChart` and `UsageRound` keep the last pointer position until a leave, so the chart tooltip and the ring center stay on the point or arc under the pointer when the numbers change. The windows open from the ⋮ main menu, Ctrl+M and Ctrl+U, the row panel, and the tray. The tray posts `POST /api/engine/show-window`; the Board that reported last gets the `show-window` event and opens the window. When no Board is open, the tray starts the launcher with `ASB_OPEN_WINDOW=messages` or `usage`, and the new Board opens that window after it shows. Each window keeps only its size (width, height, maximized) in `activity-windows.json` next to `layout.json`, written when it closes; GTK 4 has no window position API. `layout.json` does not change.
- **Profiles** in Preferences (`AppSourcesEditor`, the approved option 1, One list) is one boxed list of activatable rows: the profile dot, the name, `<app> · <N> chats` (`profile_subtitle`; `<app> · Off` when disabled), the ASB Bridge state of the profile on this machine (icon and text from `BRIDGE_STATES`; `Off` when disabled), and a chevron. The `ASB Bridge` caption and the states show only after a valid `GET /api/bridge` reply (`bridge_valid`, `bridge_self_apps`). The footer has **Add a profile**, Refresh, and `N of M profiles`. A row opens one profile dialog, made once and reused (`Adw.Dialog`; on Libadwaita 1.4 a modal `Adw.Window` that hides on close): Name, App, and Enabled; ASB Bridge (the state line, the next step from `bridge_detail`, **Install** or **Update** from `bridge_action`, and Remote Claude sessions for Claude profiles); Files; Color and dot; the ID, the status, and **Remove profile**. Install posts `POST /api/bridge/install` `{sourceId}` and shows `Installed.`, `Updated.`, or `The install did not work. Output:` with the last 12 lines, then reads the bridge state again. Default sources cannot be removed or change provider. The editor reloads when Profiles becomes visible and when an observed dashboard source revision changes, and reads `GET /api/bridge` after each successful load (one request at a time, with one queued request; Refresh adds `?refresh=1` and is the only bridge request that shows its error). It queues refresh while a request or path picker is active. Refresh keeps unsaved edits and Add drafts. An external change to the edited source keeps dirty fields and sets a conflict, which blocks Save and Remove; Cancel closes the dialog, and the next open loads the saved settings. When the edited source changed outside and the form is clean, the dialog shows the new values; when it was removed, the dialog closes. If only another source changed, the form keeps its values and adopts the new revision. Save and Remove send `If-Match`; success closes the dialog and shows `Saved.` or `Removed.`, which stay until you open or add a profile or select Refresh. Rows update in place (one row object per source ID), so a change keeps the keyboard focus; after Remove the focus goes to the row in its place, or to **Add a profile**. While a save or a remove runs, the dialog cannot close (Escape and Cancel do nothing, `Adw.Dialog` has `can-close` false, and `close-request` stops the close on Libadwaita 1.4). The `ASB Bridge` caption measures the right edge of the first shown state text in a one-shot tick after the caption maps or the list renders, so it lines up in any theme. Closing Preferences keeps the open dialog and its edits (`PreferencesWindow.close` hides the window without closing the dialog), and Escape closes only the dialog while it shows. The existing dashboard poll drives revision observation and UI reports; no separate source scan timer is added.
- The approved Soft dot sits at the lower-left of the existing provider icon in both views. Its 6px solid fill and 1px background rim make an 8px circle. This passive, unmeasured overlay appears only when that provider has multiple registered sources. Codex and Claude use the same rule; legacy or single-source rows hide it. Source names remain in search, tooltips, and accessible descriptions, so color is not the only identifier. Row geometry, borders, and provider pills stay unchanged.
- View/width changes preserve filters, search, pins, focus, and the outer frame. Keyboard focus reveals off-screen rows. Ordinary refresh preserves scroll position.
- Dark colors apply to the main and Preferences windows only. A color picker change selects Custom colors; Apply checks and saves the palette for the next start. Any valid six-digit divider color is accepted, including the background color. Dark background and text contrast checks stay in place. Loading or resetting pickers keeps the intended mode. Reset removes the saved custom theme and selects GNOME colors. Validation and save errors keep the previous theme. No global GNOME setting changes.
- Working time uses a stable source start (`workingSinceMs`) and a local two-second clock. Idle age labels update once a minute. While the board window is not active, a Working time from one minute on shows whole minutes (`4m`, also in the accessible text) and the clock wakes at its next whole minute (`clock_wait`); a hover tooltip keeps the exact time, and an active window shows seconds again at once. Label updates do not read sources.
- Provider warnings hide after five seconds. The same warning stays hidden until it changes or clears. Dashboard fetch errors stay visible until a successful refresh. A background poll does not change the native Refresh button. The button is insensitive only while a forced refresh is in flight or queued. A row action error leaves the row when the row state changes or when the row leaves the list.
- The empty-list text follows the list. **No desktop sessions found…** shows only when the dashboard has no sessions. A list with no match shows the search and filter text.

The application ID is `local.asb.AgentSwitchBoard`. The toolbar uses ASB's own disc icon. Codex and Claude row icons identify the original apps. The installer adds only ASB's launcher and icon. It adds no auto-start entry.

## Refresh and performance limits

The web client polls every two seconds while any unarchived root is Working, otherwise every five seconds. The native board reads the list after each `dashboard` event and once after each new event stream connection; its timer (the same intervals) skips the list read only while its event stream is connected and the engine advertised `listEvents: true` in the `connected` event (`EventStream.connected` and `list_events`), because only a service-mode engine runs timer scans and sends an event for every list change; with a desktop server (`npm start`), an older engine, or a failed stream, the poll stays. This uses the full list before filters and continues when ASB loses focus. The clean server snapshot has a five-second TTL. With two-second polling, reconciliation can occur on the next tick after about six seconds. Cold scans and source write delay can take longer.

Board start: the launcher starts the window when the board check is good, also while the engine starts, so that the GTK import of the window (about 1.2 s on Windows, about 70 ms on Linux) and the engine start overlap. `asb-board.py` gets the argument `-`. The class `LauncherStart` in `scripts/asb-native.py` starts at the top of the module, before the GTK import. Its thread reads the first JSON line `{"url","token"}` from descriptor 0 with `os.read` (a thread that waits in `sys.stdin` can stop Python at its exit), allows only a local ASB URL, sets the source token, and then loads the first `GET /api/dashboard` with its `ETag`. `main()` waits for the URL after the import, so the window is built when the URL is known, while the early request may still run. `SwitchboardWindow` takes the early result as its first dashboard (`first_dashboard`); while the request is open, `loading` is true and a refresh waits in the queue. A failed early request shows no notice and the normal first refresh asks again. When the line is missing (the launcher ended) or is not a local address, the window prints one message and exits with code 1. The same thread keeps reading after the first dashboard request. Each later complete address line, at most 4096 bytes, goes to the GTK main loop; an invalid line is ignored and end of input ends the reader. Only a launcher older than the follow mode writes later lines (its first line has no `pid` and `node`); the current launcher ends the pipe after 30 s, and a follow helper result `moved` takes the same path. A reconnect changes the token and the base URL of the application, each open board, and its open Preferences editors. Each board replaces its event stream, clears the dashboard ETag and the last UI report, sends a UI report to the new engine, and queues a dashboard refresh. The window, message state, filters, scroll, and selection stay. Existing error notices can show during the gap and clear after a good dashboard. A window that you start by hand (`python3 scripts/asb-native.py http://127.0.0.1:<port>` with `ASB_SOURCE_TOKEN`) works as before. The Preferences window is made on its first use (`make_preferences`), not at start; its pages are built at start, because the filter, the theme, and the layout code use their widgets, and a custom theme that is set before the first use reaches the new window. On Linux, `make_preferences` costs about 2 ms, so the gain of this part is small there. Linux checks (a synthetic engine and `ASB_SNAPSHOT`, in temporary folders, median of 5 runs, from the start of the launcher to the saved window image, which includes a fixed 250 ms delay before the capture): with no engine running, 699 ms before and 596 ms after; with an engine that answers the dashboard in 300 ms, 792 ms before and 681 ms after; with an engine that answers at once, 526 ms before and 503 ms after.

Source events send only a version, reason, provider flags, and the panel tag (a hash, see the top-bar route). They send no paths or content. Registered source folders and registry changes use these same watchers. Events are grouped for 250 milliseconds. Active scans start no more than once per two seconds. Missing directories and failed watches retry every five seconds, only for the affected required or optional roots. Healthy watcher trees stay in place. A required root failure keeps coverage incomplete; polling remains the fallback. The Claude `claude-code-sessions` folder is an optional root, like the remote cache and the live session folder, so a PC without the Claude app keeps full coverage. Late callbacks from closed or replaced watchers are ignored. On Linux, ASB uses one watch for each directory under the session folders, not one for each file. It follows new and removed folders from `rename` events, with one `stat` for each event entry. Full directory walks remain for start, changed watch paths, root replacement or structure invalidation, a burst of more than 64 structure events, and the 60-second safety check. A watcher error retries its affected root. `performance.dashboard.watchWalks` counts directory walks, including recovered subtrees; a simple absent-root check does not increment it. Other platforms use the native recursive watch and fall back to directory watches. An injected `loadDashboard` with no source registry gets no default watch paths. Profile registration adds no permanent fast poll.

In service mode (`serviceMode: true`), the server scans with no client, so ASB sees each task end while the window is closed, and the phone can show Done notifications on its next read. After it listens, it loads the shared dashboard. Without full watch coverage, it schedules the next scan after two seconds while any unarchived local root is Working, otherwise after five seconds, and the clean snapshot lasts one scan interval. With full watch coverage, these start a scan:

- A source event (the follow-up scan, also with no connected event client).
- A soft invalidation that marks the snapshot dirty, for example a peer change of the machine group (`peer-sessions`). It gets the same follow-up scan, because no file event follows it.
- `nextStatusCheckAtMs`: the earliest time-window expiry (the six-hour activity windows, the 10-second child end grace) or usage limit reset.
- The full scan (the safety scan): every 5 minutes (`SERVICE_REST_SCAN_MS`), also while a local row works. It is counted from the last full scan of each source, so events cannot postpone it. The timer waits until the earlier of that time and `nextStatusCheckAtMs` (at least two seconds for a status time), and never ends inside the retry window of a failed scan.
- Every 30 seconds while a local unarchived row is Working or Waiting, a status check verifies the last scan's live registry processes (their start time on Linux, their PID and message pipe on Windows). It also compares the size and file time of the active transcripts and rollouts, with no read. A changed file gives a hint and a follow-up scan. A process that ended or whose PID was reused starts a scan. The check stops when no local row is Working or Waiting.

A scan between full scans reads only the changed files of a source: `DashboardSnapshot` passes `sourceScans` (a `canReuse(filePath)` check for each of Codex and Claude) to `loadDashboard`. A source gets it when its watch coverage had no break since its last full scan (a watcher error, a replaced watcher, changed watch paths, a failed scan, or a hard invalidation starts a new epoch), each of its events had an exact file hint, and its last full scan is newer than the safety scan time (5 min in service mode; 5 s in the desktop server). Then a Codex rollout, or a Claude session file, transcript, or agent log, under a watched folder and not in the hints keeps its cached stat and result. Hinted files, files with no cache entry, files outside the watched folders, the Codex state database and its WAL, the session index, and the Claude folder index follow the rules of a full scan. The check gives each path its answer once per scan; on POSIX a normal absolute path is its own key (no `path.resolve`), and on Windows the key is the resolved path in lower case. A trusted follow-up scan also skips each Claude desktop, Claude CLI, Claude remote cache, and Codex loader whose watched paths have no hint. It keeps the last loader result, with no session file stat and no read, until a loader time rule passes. The remote cache keeps its parsed responses and still applies its clock rules at each scan, so future and skewed observations change as in a full scan. A trusted follow-up scan with no hint in the watched registry folder reuses its last live records, with no folder or process read. Registry hints, the full scan, and the 30-second process check verify the processes again. In one `loadSwitchboardDashboard` call, the desktop pass and the CLI pass of a Claude source share one project index read (in the same `Map` as the registry reads), so each project folder gets one check per scan. A full scan runs at the first scan, at the safety scan time, for a burst or an unknown file (the `''` hint), without full watch coverage, and for a forced load. A lost event on an active transcript or rollout is found by the 30-second status check. A lost event on any other file, including a new or inactive session, waits for the 5-minute full scan. With 700 Codex rollouts and 100 Claude transcripts, the scan after one rollout append made 12 stat calls (a full scan: 911). Each engine scan also keeps the built row of a thread whose fields (all but `currentTurnElapsedMs`) and machine are the same as at its last build, while no status time of the row passed (the six-hour windows, the child end grace, the approval check, the limit reset) and the clock did not go back: `buildSwitchboardDashboard` gives a copy of that row (the engine's `rowCache`) and builds only the other rows. A remote cache row is always built. The JSON and the hidden row fields are the same as those of a full build.

The scan interval and the busy throttle use the local rows only (`localRefreshIntervalMs`, a property that is not in the JSON). The `refreshIntervalMs` in the JSON also counts the rows of other machines, for the board and the phone. Close stops this timer.

The Claude app log and the Codex `plugin-state/asb-bridge` folder have optional watches. The 30-second status check detects a Claude Code CLI process that stops with no exit write; an open task with no live record shows Unknown after its existing 10-second start grace. The status deadline also covers the two-minute message window and the seven-day CLI transcript window. A scan with offline member rows checks again within 30 seconds, so their 10-minute removal stays current. Unwatched inactive files, including archived rollouts outside `sessions`, use the 5-minute safety scan.

Client reads do not overlap and retain at most one requested follow-up. A forced read (`/api/dashboard?force=1`) that arrives during a scan waits for that scan. It then gets one more scan that starts after the request. Forced requests during the same scan share that scan. A successful open marks the server snapshot dirty and sends a `dashboard` event with the reason `asb-open`; the next read scans again. Successful native session actions request a normal queued refresh and retain a follow-up if a read is already active. The Discard server action still forces its validation scan before arming. Successful opens in both clients use the normal refresh path. Each `200` reply of `GET /api/dashboard`, with or without `?force=1`, has an `ETag` header: a quoted SHA-256 content hash of all reply fields except `generatedAtMs` and `performance`. The dashboard replies also have `Cache-Control: no-store`. A request with `If-None-Match` equal to the current tag gets `304` with no body; a forced request still scans first. `performance.dashboard.notModified` counts these replies. A request whose `Accept-Encoding` allows gzip (`acceptsGzip` in `src/local-http.mjs`) gets the list as gzip (`Content-Encoding: gzip`, `Vary: Accept-Encoding`, the same tag); a 304 and the reply without gzip do not change. The gzip body is one gzip member (browsers read only the first member): its head is the list, compressed at level 1 once per saved list (`gzipHead`), and its tail is the `generatedAtMs` and `performance` of the reply, after the list keys (`gzipWithTail`). `performance.dashboard.compressedLists` counts the compressions. On 820 made-up rows the reply is 1,312,277 bytes without gzip and 72,699 bytes with it. The native window asks for gzip on every route (`request_json`). For a phone that accepts gzip, the phone gateway asks the loopback server for gzip and passes its compressed list or activity report through unchanged. If an older engine sends plain JSON, the gateway compresses it. The snapshot keeps the list text of the cached dashboard (`listText` of `DashboardSnapshot`). A scan with the same ordered row values and top-level list fields reuses the text, with no stringify. A changed row, field, or order makes it again, as does the first read after an in-place change. The server keeps the reply list and its tag for one list text and one palette object, so a request with the same text and palette uses them again, with no `JSON.stringify` and no hash. A scan compares its list text with the text of the cached dashboard. Only a changed list moves the revision (`revision()` of `DashboardSnapshot`) and sends the `dashboard` event, also after a timer scan (the service poll, a status expiry, or the safety scan); a scan that changed nothing sends no event. Each `invalidateDashboard` call also moves the revision. A hard call sends the event at once. In service mode, a soft call that marks the snapshot dirty sends no event at once: its follow-up scan sends one only when the list changed. A follow-up timer after a read that already scanned the change sends no event. A soft call that marks the snapshot dirty (a peer change, an open) keeps the list text, because it does not change the cached rows: the next scan shows the change. The rows can change in place after a scan, so each code path that changes a row in place must call `invalidateDashboard` after the change. These paths do it today: the session actions (Read, Unread, Pin, Unpin, move a pin, drawer, Mute, Discard, and Keep), a successful open, and the unread setting. A peer change of the machine group marks the snapshot dirty, so the next read scans and stores a new dashboard. A saved palette is a new palette object. If a path changes a row and does not call `invalidateDashboard`, the reply keeps the old list and tag until the next scan. A forced read scans first and then uses the list of the new dashboard. Both clients send the tag of the dashboard that they show in `If-None-Match` and skip list updates on `304`. The native window clears the tag after an error and after a POST reply changed its list. The browser clears its tag after an error; a queued forced browser refresh remains forced. Native rows remain keyed by ID and update in place. Rows skip unchanged cached text and action updates. A lazy row cache retains previously shown rows across search and filters, bounded by full dashboard IDs. With a card limit and "show all" off, it is also bounded by twice the limit: after a render that passes this bound, an idle step releases the hidden cards. Hidden rows have no clock work or Read feedback; current data, action state, time, view, and palette apply when they return. Layout signals replace permanent frame checks; IDs removed from the full dashboard and window close release row controllers. On Linux, a list reply starts a 60 s timer when none is pending, and the timer calls glibc `malloc_trim(0)` once (`trim_heap`), so the board gives the freed heap of its list renders back to the system at most once a minute; another system or C library does nothing.

A Comfortable card builds its limit clock, mute glyph, remote machine icon and name, and copied footer page on first use, in their usual places. The board creates column containers for the whole list, but starts a render with cards in at most the first two columns in view. Compact, which has about three times as many cards per column, and a view change start with one column. The rest of the view, then one column on each side, get cards one column per painted frame, in a separate default-priority idle turn after the frame and waiting input. While idle, the same queue fills up to one viewport width on each side, nearest columns first, then stops; it waits while a wheel glide runs. Show all keeps this bound. A scroll or a key that needs a column fills it at once and replaces the queued fill; a render, resize, search, or filter restarts the fill window. Native `SessionColumn` snapshots skip columns outside the viewport. The viewport check includes card-motion bounds. This changes paint only. Row allocation, input, focus, and accessibility stay unchanged. Scroll and layout invalidate skipped snapshots when their columns enter the viewport.

On composited X11, the existing realize hook disables GTK4 compositor-feedback frame sync when the surface API is available. Wayland and noncomposited displays stay unchanged. Native springs, the renderer, and reduced-motion rules stay unchanged. This GTK4 X11 backend API is deprecated and has no replacement. The fallback frame clock may run at about 60 Hz.

Browser rows stay keyed by chat ID, and their cache is bounded by current dashboard IDs. Provider and project groups are retained for the visible list. A title change updates the existing row without rebuilding rows or listeners. Reconciliation preserves in-flight opens, retained focus, and project collapse choices. A `304` skips list reconciliation while age labels still update once a minute through the existing refresh cycle.

The Codex, Claude, and normalization modules have one mode. The ASB view has no token, quota, artifact, model-service, or governance data, except the usage-limit reset time of a stopped session. The Codex reader makes no network request, reads no `auth.json`, and writes no file; only the engine saves its reader positions (see below). `AMC_CODEX_RESET_CREDITS` has no effect. There is only the Claude Desktop Code reader for Claude. Metadata fingerprints reuse unchanged records. Source events can invalidate caches even when file stats do not change. Local Codex and Claude signal and lifecycle caches retain up to 5000 keys per enabled store. The signals hold times, flags, counts, IDs, and a few metadata fields (the title records, folder, model, version, and branch name of a Claude transcript), and never the text of a prompt or an answer: the readers read a message only to set its time and kind (`latestUserMessageAtMs`, `latestAgentFinalAtMs`, `latestMessageKind`), and `test/asb-data-performance.test.mjs` checks that a scan of long prompts keeps none of them. The upstream text fields (`firstUserMessage`, `latestUserMessage`, `latestMeaningfulUserMessage`, `lastAgentMessage`), the Codex native pin (`pinned`, the `is_pinned` column, and `pinned-thread-ids`), and the automation hint (`isAutomation`) were deleted: no code, script, view, phone app, bridge, or export read them. The pins of the board are only the ASB pins of `pending.json`. Shared values are held once. Codex lifecycle checkpoints use the same source ownership, including rollouts outside the store folder. Local metadata caches retain up to 5000 Claude records and 32 Codex records per store. Disabling, removing, or changing a store prunes its local cache owners; values shared with an active store stay available. Standalone readers keep one bounded 5000-key signal scope. Owner tracking permits up to eight registered source stores plus that bounded standalone scope. The Claude directory index has a 32-root limit. Concurrent Claude profiles with the same `projectsDir` share one directory signature check. They also share cached root, subagent, and Workflow lookup maps. Multiple migrated child candidates still get current modification times. Stale in-flight index reads cannot restore invalidated or removed cache entries. Remote response-cache retention is separate from this local source ownership.

The OpenCode reader keeps one result per enabled OpenCode source. On each load it compares the size and modification time of `opencode.db` and `opencode.db-wal` with the cached values, because a WAL append does not change the main file. Equal values reuse the result with no query. The file watcher on the OpenCode folder accepts only `opencode.db` and `opencode.db-wal` events and only marks the snapshot dirty; there is no OpenCode file hint. Source updates and removals clear the OpenCode cache, and disabled sources lose theirs.

The remote reader keeps at most 32 cache roots, 50,000 classified file names per root, 512 response entries, and 5000 projected session records per root. Aggregate eviction preserves only the newest selected response chain so linked updates are not lost. Raw protected storage can reach `512 × 5000` records per root; its metric reports the actual raw count. Key discovery and file-stat scans use six concurrent readers. Approved response bodies queue before allocation. Runtime body reads and worker parsing run one at a time. One lazy shared worker decodes and parses bodies, using `src/claude-remote-data.mjs` itself. Its queue has at most 512 metadata jobs; explicitly submitted async bodies have a 48 MiB aggregate limit. Encoded bodies are limited to 8 MiB and decoded bodies to 16 MiB. Unrelated cache writes do not reread old keys or bodies. Built-in zstd decoding is optional; gzip watch reads work on older supported Node.js versions. The first remote load has worker startup overhead. An idle worker is unreferenced. A 30-second deadline or worker error rejects pending work. Shutdown rejects queued work immediately and stops old scan producers. After watcher cleanup, server close requests a graceful worker exit. Active bounded parsing is allowed to return before the worker closes. A replacement worker starts only after the old worker exits. This avoids forced termination during native decompression. A hung native parse can block worker exit and replacement. Tests retain the pure synchronous parser. This adds no runtime file or dependency.

Codex tails start at 64 KiB and grow to 256 KiB. On the first read of a log that is larger than the tail, ASB does not parse each line of the full log when the tail gives the lifecycle state. It searches the raw bytes before the tail for a real question call (a `function_call` named `request_user_input` or `request_user_input_async`) and reads lines only from such a call until the question state is clear again. The results are the same as a full scan. A log whose tail has no lifecycle event still gets the full scan. Lifecycle and question recovery can scan full logs with bounded memory and append checkpoints held in memory. Later reads continue from the saved offset. Claude transcript tails have an 8 MiB limit and use six concurrent reads. Larger Claude logs recover complete lifecycle and Agent links with bounded memory. Signal reads keep an in-memory state and offset, including loaded questions and metadata. On growth, the reader checks the file identity and a 64-byte boundary guard. A match permits a read from the saved offset. A valid final JSON record without a newline is visible, but the next append rereads that line. Truncation, replacement, equal-size changes, and detected boundary rewrites trigger recovery. The append guard assumes earlier bytes stay unchanged when a file grows. Pending permission entries retain only the newest 5000. Local-command prefixes do not start new work. Broad no-path invalidation reuses unchanged lifecycle data, while targeted and equal-size rewrites retain safe invalidation. Unchanged child logs use cached signals. Retained history can still make cold reads slow. ASB writes no transcript or work-metric cache.

Reader positions (`src/reader-positions.mjs`): only the engine (`runEngine`) turns them on. The file is `readers/positions.json` in the cache folder (`cacheDir`: `~/.cache/asb`, an absolute `XDG_CACHE_HOME`, or `%LOCALAPPDATA%\asb\cache`). The folder is `0700` and the file `0600`; a link or a foreign owner turns the positions off for the run. Each Codex lifecycle checkpoint is a record: the file signature (`dev`, `ino`, `size`, `mtimeMs`, `ctimeMs`), the offset, the `full` flag, the lifecycle, and the open questions (call IDs, times, and indexes). Each Claude transcript that is larger than its tail (8 MiB in the engine) also gets a record: the file signature, the offset, and the committed lifecycle, with its agents, agent tools, wrap-ups, and last 20 incoming messages as rows of IDs, times, and fixed kinds. A Claude record has no text field. A Codex log that the engine read also gets a tail record (section `codexTail`): the file signature, the tail limits, a guard digest, and the result of the tail read, which has only numbers, booleans, and fixed kinds (the 13 lifecycle fields, the question flags and times, the hint flag, and the kind of the last message). A new engine that finds the same signature, the same limits, and the same guard digest uses this result and does not read the tail of the log; a log that grew has its tail read, as before. On Linux, the Claude Desktop cache folder (`Cache/Cache_Data`) gets bucket records (section `remote`). The entry names that start with the same three characters are a bucket. A record has the count of the names, a keyed digest of the sorted names, and the positions of the names that a new engine must open: a session list or watch, an entry that is not complete, and an entry with a bad header. When the names of a bucket are the same as at the save, the new engine opens none of its other entries, because the name of an entry is the digest of its URL, so its key cannot change. Else it opens all entries of the bucket. No entry name, URL, cursor, or title goes to disk. The record key and the guard are HMAC-SHA-256 digests (16 bytes): of `codex\0<path>`, `claude\0<path>`, `codexTail\0<path>`, or `remote\0<cache folder>\0<bucket>`, and of the up to 64 bytes before `size` (for a bucket, of its sorted names). The key comes with HKDF from the machine identity secret (`readerPositionsKey`); with no key in 3 s, the engine reads as before and saves nothing. The file has a version, a key check, and one section for each kind of record (`codex`, `claude`, `codexTail`, `remote`) with the SHA-256 of `codex-data.mjs`, `claude-data.mjs`, or `claude-remote-data.mjs` and `data-cache.mjs`; a change of one drops only the records of its sections, and a file with no section for a kind loads the other kinds. At a cache miss, the reader takes the record of the log and resumes the scan at its offset only when `dev` and `ino` are the same, the log is not smaller, an equal size has the same times, and the guard digest of the bytes before the saved size is the same. Else it reads from the start. A tail record is used only when the whole signature (`dev`, `ino`, `size`, `mtimeMs`, `ctimeMs`) is the same. The save keeps the newest 5000 records of each section, at most 256 KiB for one record and 8 MiB for the file (the newest records of all sections first), and no record older than 30 days. It starts 100 ms after the first scan, so the rows of that scan go out first. It builds the records at most once in 10 minutes (also when the text did not change), writes when the text changed, and writes at a clean stop, with a temporary file and a rename. The HMAC digests use a key object, because a start makes thousands of them. Records hold only numbers, booleans, fixed kinds, hex digests, and IDs that match `^[A-Za-z0-9_:-]{1,160}$`; a record with another ID is not written.

Activity reads: the engine counts the changes of its local session rows (`id`, `state`, `updatedAtMs`, or `archived`; member rows do not count). Messages reuses its local records for 10 s, or for 5 minutes while no local session works and the counter stays the same. It reuses only a result that covers the requested time range, and filters it by the new start time. The records with texts that the engine sends to the group members (one result per period) follow the same rule. A reuse makes no folder listing, stat, database query, or transcript read. Without the counter (`npm start` and the tests), each request reads again. When a read runs, the messages routes read each bridge database again only when the size or change time of the file or its WAL changed; the engine keeps the message rows of the last 30 days of at most 2,000 files in memory. The Claude message reader saves its scan state without names or message text, so the first Messages reply after an engine restart reads the changed transcript bytes and the saved message lines, not every transcript in full. A Usage report (`GET /api/activity/usage`) or a member activity reply uses the ledger first (see [Usage ledger](#usage-ledger)). A usage export and the ledger timer still start a scan, never the Board. While a local session works, Usage shows the numbers of the last scan; the hourly scan waits for idle for at most an hour. A new install without local ledger records can still start its first scan while busy. The scan reads the Codex rollouts (`sessions` and `archived_sessions`, also `*.jsonl.zst`), the Claude transcripts, and the OpenCode databases of the enabled sources, and the Grok, Pi, and Antigravity folders when they exist (`GROK_HOME`, `PI_CODING_AGENT_SESSION_DIR`, `PI_CODING_AGENT_DIR`, `ANTIGRAVITY_DATA_DIR`; see [ASB.md](ASB.md#messages-and-usage)). It keeps all history: there is no age limit. It reads and keeps only the newest 10,000 transcript files by change time (`MAX_CACHE_FILES`), and it selects them before it reads. A rollout with both `x.jsonl` and `x.jsonl.zst` in one folder counts once (the `.jsonl`). When one listing has a rollout name in `sessions` and in `archived_sessions` (the chat was archived while the scan listed the folders), only the `archived_sessions` path counts. Without zstd in `node:zlib` (Node.js before 22.15), ASB lists no `.zst` file. OpenCode and Antigravity have no age limit. The OpenCode query reads pages of 8 rows and yields between them (on a cold disk, one page blocks the event loop for about 0.1 s at most). While the first scan runs, a report gives the records found so far and `scan.complete: false`. The cache is gzip-compressed JSON (`usage-cache.json.gz`). When no gzip file exists, the engine reads the old `usage-cache.json` once and removes it after the first compressed save. Compression and decompression are async; encoding and parsing the JSON still run on the main thread. The cache has version 2: a version 1 cache (pruned at 90 days) is not used, so the first scan after the update reads all files again. Later scans read only the new bytes of a grown file (a file whose first 64 bytes changed is read again from the start; a grown `.zst` file is read again in full). With the engine ledger, an idle request starts a scan only before the first completed scan, after the local row counter moved, or at least 5 minutes after the last scan started (Grok, Pi, and Antigravity have usage but no rows). The 10 s limit since the last scan ended still applies. Without the ledger and the local change counter, reports start scans as before, at most every 10 s. The cache is saved only when it changed: after the first scan that changed it, then at most every 5 minutes. A scan that changed the records sums them once by app, model, and quarter hour (a repeated record ID counts once: the first copy by file key), so a report does not add up all records again. The OpenCode query runs again only when a database or its WAL changed, and at most once a minute while it changes: between two reads, a scan keeps the last records. The first read of a folder never waits. The scan runs in the background, so it rests: after each 25 ms of work it waits 3 times the CPU time of the engine process (all threads) since its last wait, at most 200 ms per wait (`SCAN_REST_RATIO`, `MAX_REST_MS`, `createYielder` in `src/usage-scan-cache.mjs`), so it stays under about a third of one core. With made-up data (3,950 files, 5.63 GB), a first scan used 172 % to 192 % of one core for 10 s at full speed, and 22 % to 40 % for 57 s with the rest. Other users of `createYielder` (the Messages read, and direct calls of `scanFile` or the OpenCode and Antigravity readers) only give the event loop a turn. On the main test PC (12.1 GB of transcripts with all history and the compressed Codex rollouts), the first scan at full speed took about 20 s with a warm file cache and about 47 s with a cold one, a later scan took 53 to 78 ms, and a report took about 1 ms (about 60 ms for the first report after a scan that changed the records).

Price list: only a Usage report loads it, in the background (a report never waits for it). The engine first reads its 24-hour disk cache. When the cache is older than 24 hours or missing, it downloads `https://raw.githubusercontent.com/BerriAI/litellm/main/model_prices_and_context_window.json`, then the GitHub API URL of the same file when that fails, with a 10 s timeout and a 32 MiB limit for each. After a failed download, the next try waits at least 60 s.

### Usage ledger

The engine keeps the hourly token usage of this machine in `usage-ledger.json` in its config folder (`src/usage-ledger.mjs`). Only the engine gives `createActivityUsage` this file (`ledgerFile`), so `npm start` and the tests without it work as before. The usage of a transcript stays after its app deletes the file (Claude Code deletes CLI transcripts after 30 days by default), after the file falls out of the newest 10,000, and after its profile is disabled or removed. A record is the UTC hour start, the app, the model, and the four token counts. After each scan, the hourly records of the transcripts that exist then merge into the ledger: per hour, app, model, and token type, the larger value counts, never the sum. So a record that a scan sees again, or a second import of the same file, adds nothing. A record that fails the record check of an import (below) goes into no ledger and no export. A report adds, per key and token type, the ledger value minus the live value when the ledger value is larger, as one part at the hour start. The member reply (`localHourly`) sends per key the larger of the two. Shortcut limit: when the transcript of an hour is deleted while new usage of the same app and model comes in during that hour, that hour shows the larger value, not the sum.

The engine starts the ledger after the group start (`server.usageLedger.start()`): a scan due 10 minutes after the start (`LEDGER_FIRST_MS`), then one due an hour after the last tick ended (`LEDGER_REFRESH_MS`), also when no window is open. A due tick waits while the last local session list has a non-archived Working row, checks again every 60 seconds, and runs after at most 60 minutes of waiting even if a session still works. It uses the cached local rows without a new session scan; member rows do not delay it. `stop()` cancels a waiting check. Before each report and member usage reply, `ready()` loads only the ledger, not transcripts. While a local session works, requests show the numbers of the last scan and start no scan, except on a new install with no local ledger records and no completed scan. While idle, requests scan before the first completed scan, after the local row counter moved since the last scan started, or 5 minutes after that start, still at most once per 10 s. With no running scan, `scan.complete` is true, with the last file counts or zero. After each timer scan, the engine asks the members for their usage of the period `all` (the `members` option of `createActivityUsage`, `group.activity('all')`), merges the replies with `keepMembers`, and saves the ledger when it changed; a failed ask keeps nothing. Each report, member reply, export, or import (re)starts one release timer (`unref`) of 2 minutes (`RELEASE_AFTER_MS`). When it fires, the engine saves the scan cache when it changed and drops it from memory (`stats.releases`); a running scan keeps the cache, and the check runs again when the scan ends. A timer scan does the same when nothing asked for usage in the last 2 minutes. `stop()` clears the release timer. The ledger stays, so a report shows the saved totals at once; only a scan allowed by the request rule reads the cache file again. Timer scans and exports never download prices. The engine writes the whole file (`writePrivateJson`: mode `0600` in a `0700` folder, a temporary file and a rename) after the first scan of a run that changed the ledger, then at most every 5 minutes, at once after an import, and in the first step of the engine stop (`usage ledger save`). A scan that runs when the engine stops ends before its next file. A failed save logs `ASB cannot save its usage ledger. It keeps the old file.` and keeps the changes for the next save. A file that is not JSON or not a valid version 1 ledger is never overwritten: the engine renames it to `usage-ledger.<ms>.bad.json` in the same folder, logs `ASB set aside an unreadable usage ledger: <file name>.`, and starts empty. A path that is not a regular file (for example a link) or that cannot be read stays unchanged: the engine logs `ASB cannot read its usage ledger. It saves no usage history now.` and saves no ledger in that run. A bad file that cannot be renamed also stays unchanged (`ASB cannot set aside its unreadable usage ledger. It saves no usage history now.`). A good file with another mode loads and gets mode `0600`. The records of the file itself have no upper time limit, so a clock that is wrong at the start does not make the file bad.

An import merges each machine of the file in the same way: the records of this machine ID into this machine, and each other machine into its own entry with the imported name and the newest export time (`exportedAtMs`). A report merges the imported records of a group member with its reply by the larger value: the records of the member's machine ID, and the records of each other imported machine with the member's name (the letter case does not matter), which is an old identity of the same machine, for example after it left the group and joined it again. It keeps the merged records per reply array and ledger version, so a second report with the same replies does not merge again. It lists each imported machine that is not this machine, a member, or a member's name after the members (by name, then ID) with `state: 'offline'` and `lastReportAtMs` = its export time. Imported machines with one name (the letter case does not matter) are one machine: the ID and name of the newest export (then the larger ID), that export time, and the larger value of each record, and `all` counts their records for its first month. The ledger keeps the current machine ID and name, so after `asb group reset-identity` the old records stay with this machine; an imported copy of this machine joins its own records.

A report, an export, and the hourly timer scan also merge each member reply into the ledger, once for each reply (`keepMembers`; the group gives the same `usage` array until a new reply comes, so a `WeakSet` of the arrays skips a reply that is already in). Only a member with a valid machine ID and group name that is not this machine counts, and only the records that pass the record check, so the saved file always loads. Its entry takes the member name and `exportedAtMs` = the newest `lastReportAtMs`. So a member that is offline after a restart keeps its records in the report and the export, and a machine that left the group shows like an imported machine. Nothing is merged before the first ledger load.

### Usage backups

The engine saves a usage backup file each day (`src/usage-backup.mjs`). Only the engine gives `createSwitchboardServer` the settings file (`usageBackupFile`), and the backups exist only with the ledger. The settings file `usage-backup.json` in the ledger folder is `{ version: 1, enabled, time, keep, folder }`, with exactly these keys: `enabled` a boolean, `time` a local `HH:MM`, `keep` from 1 to 30, and `folder` either `''` (the default folder, `backups` next to the settings file) or an absolute path of at most 1024 characters with no control character. Without the file, the defaults hold (`enabled` true, `03:00`, 5, `''`) and nothing is written. A file that is not valid also gives the defaults and logs `ASB cannot read its usage backup settings. It uses the defaults.` one time; it stays until the next save. A save uses `writePrivateJson`.

A backup is the group-aware export (`exportAll` of the switchboard, with every member reply and the kept members) in `asb-usage-<name>-<id8>-<YYYY-MM-DD>.json`: the group name and the first 8 hex characters of the ID of this machine (the first machine of the export), and the local date when the backup starts. In the default folder, `writePrivateJson` writes it (folder `0700`, file `0600`). A folder that the user chose must exist and be a directory (a link to one is fine): ASB never makes it or changes its mode. It writes `<file>.<12 hex>.tmp` with mode `0600` and the `wx` flag, renames it, and always removes the temporary file. A chosen folder that is missing fails before the export (`The backup folder does not exist: <folder>.`). A failed write gives `ASB cannot write the backup to <folder>: <reason>.`, where the reason is `no permission`, `the disk is full`, `the disk is read-only`, `error <code>`, or `an error`; an export failure gives 409 with its message, or `ASB cannot make the usage backup.` A second backup on the same day replaces the file. After a good write, ASB lists the regular files of this machine in the folder (`asb-usage-<any group name>-<id8>-<date>.json`, so the files of an old machine name count too), keeps the `keep - 1` newest by date besides the new file, and deletes the others. It ignores a file that is already gone and logs `ASB cannot delete an old usage backup: <name>.` one time for each file. It never touches other files.
